The topology of Lagrangian submanifolds via open-closed string topology
Shuhao Li

Abstract
. We study the topology of Lagrangian submanifolds in standard symplectic vector spaces ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} using ideas from open-closed string topology. Specifically, for a closed, oriented, spin Lagrangian L L , we construct a (possibly curved) deformation of the dg associative algebra of chains on the based loop space of L L . This is done via pushing forward moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs with boundaries on L L viewed as chains in the free loop space along a string topology closed-open map. As an application, we prove that if π 2 ​ ( L ) = 0 \pi_{2}(L)=0 , then L L has non-vanishing Maslov class, generalizing previous results due to Viterbo [ Vit90 ] , Cieliebak-Mohnke [ CM18 ] , Fukaya [ Fuk06 ] and Irie [ Iri20 ] .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context

This paper is concerned with the topology of closed Lagrangian submanifolds of the standard symplectic vector spaces
(\mathbb{C}^{n},\omega)
with vanishing Maslov classes.

The
Maslov class

\mu_{L}\colon\pi_{1}(L)\to\mathbb{Z}
is an important invariant of a Lagrangian submanifold
L\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, defined by Arnol’d in
[
Arn67
]
.
We briefly recall the construction.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}Gr(n)
the
Lagrangian Grassmannian
in dimension
n
, i.e. the space consisting of all linear Lagrangian subspaces in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Arnol’d in
op. cit.
showed that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}Gr(n))\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
A Lagrangian embedding
i_{L}\colon L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
induces the Lagrangian Gauss map
Ti_{L}\colon L\to\mathcal{L}Gr(n)
, taking each
p\in L
to the Lagrangian subspace
T_{p}L\subset T_{p}\mathbb{C}^{n}\cong\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
This induces a homomorphism
\mu_{L}\colon\pi_{1}L\to\pi_{1}\mathcal{L}Gr(n)\cong\mathbb{Z}
, which is called the Maslov class.
Maslov classes can be defined for Lagrangians in general symplectic manifolds
(M,\omega)
as a homomorphism
\pi_{2}(M,L)\to\mathbb{Z}
, but for our purpose we shall restrict to the case
M=\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

The Maslov class of a Lagrangian plays the role of the relative first Chern class (see e.g. section 2.1.1 of
[
Fuk+09
]
).
Much as symplectic manifolds with vanishing first Chern classes (“symplectic Calabi-Yau’s”) play a special role in the symplectic topology,
Lagrangians with vanishing Maslov classes (which we sometimes abbreviate as
Maslov-zero Lagrangians
) also play a special role.
Their Floer theories are often graded (see e.g.
[
Sei00
]
) and thus better behaved.
Also, just as the vanishing of the first Chern class is the symplectic topological version of the
Calabi-Yau
condition, the vanishing of the Maslov class is the symplectic topological version of the
special Lagrangian
condition (see e.g.
[
HL82
,
Joy05
]
for definitions; also see e.g. the remark after Lemma 3.1 in
[
Aur07
]
).

Given a closed manifold
L
of dimension
n
, the existence or non-existence of a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding
L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a well-studied question in symplectic topology (see e.g.
[
Aud88
]
and
[
ALP94
]
for the discussion of the
Maslov class rigidity
phenomenon, or section 6.1.2 in
[
Fuk+09
]
for the discussion on the
Maslov class conjecture
).
This falls under the more general question of
which homotopy classes of maps in
[L,\mathcal{L}Gr(n)]
are realized by Lagrangian embeddings
. In contrast, all classes in
[L,\mathcal{L}Gr(n)]
are realized by Lagrangian
immersions
(see
[
Lee76
,
Gro86
]
).
The proof of a generalized version of Audin’s conjecture
[
Aud88
]
, a strong Maslov class rigidity phenomenon, is also the key ingredient in the works of
[
Fuk06
,
Iri20
]
on the classification of prime 3-manifolds admitting a Lagrangian embedding in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

Under various topological assumptions on
L
, non-existence of Maslov-zero embeddings into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is established:

(1)

Viterbo
[
Vit90
]
showed that any closed manifold admitting a metric of non-positive sectional curvature does not admit a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
;

(2)

Polterovich
[
Pol91a
]
proved non-vanishing of Maslov classes for certain Lagrangian surfaces (including certain non-compact ones) in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
;

(3)

Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono (see Theorem K in
[
Fuk+09
]
) showed that any closed, spin manifold with vanishing second Betti number does not admit a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
; this builds on Oh’s construction of spectral sequence in Floer theory in
[
Oh96
]
;

(4)

Fukaya
[
Fuk06
]
proposed a proof that any closed, aspherical, spin manifold does not admit a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which was later realized by Irie
[
Iri18
,
Iri20
]
. In fact, they show that in this situation, there always exists a curve with Maslov index 2; this is a version of Audin’s conjecture
[
Aud88
]
(the case of tori, which is the original statement of Audin’s conjecture, is first proved by
[
CM18
]
using neck-stretching analysis for holomorphic curves);

See also e.g. section 6.1.2 in
[
Fuk+09
]
for more history.

On the other hand,
[
Ekh+13
]
proved that
S^{2}\times S^{1}
admits a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.
This is in contrast with the situation of closed
special Lagrangian
submanifolds, which does not exist in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
because special Lagrangians are calibrated submanifolds which are automatically minimal submanifolds (see e.g.
[
HL82
]
), whereas one can always change the volume of closed Lagrangians in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
by scaling.

1.2.
Result

The main result of this paper is a new Maslov class rigidity phenomenon:

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
5.1
)
.

If
L
is a closed, oriented, spin manifold and
\pi_{2}(L)=0
, then
L
does not admit a Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with vanishing Maslov class.

The asphericity condition in the results of
[
Vit90
,
Fuk06
,
Iri20
]
requires the vanishing of all higher homotopy groups, whereas we only require vanishing of
\pi_{2}
. There are many examples of manifolds with vanishing
\pi_{2}
but are not covered by the prior results listed above. For example:

•

All compact connected Lie groups have vanishing
\pi_{2}
(
[
Bot56
]
); e.g.
T^{2}\times SU(2)
is an example not covered by prior results;

•

Arbitary connected sums between aspherical or spherical manifolds of dimension
\geq
4, e.g.
T^{4}\#T^{4}
, or more generally connected sums of manifolds admitting metrics with non-positive sectional curvature; in fact, the condition
\pi_{2}=0
is preserved under taking connected sums for manifolds of dimension at least 4;

•

In Remark 1.23 (a)
[
CM18
]
, they pointed out the dichotomy that the technique in their paper works well for manifolds admitting metrics with non-positive curvature whereas traditional Floer theoretic techniques work well for simply-connected manifolds, and that a test case for combining these techniques is the product of a manifold of positive with a manifold of negative curvature.
Our condition of
\pi_{2}=0
is preserved under taking products, and thus applies to manifolds like the product of a sphere (of dimension
\geq 3
) with a hyperbolic manifold.

See section
5.2
.

Remarks 1.2
.

(1)

Unlike the case with aspherical manifolds, one cannot expect a version of Audin’s conjecture to hold for manifolds with
\pi_{2}=0
. For example, for each
k>1
,
S^{1}\times S^{2k-1}
has vanishing
\pi_{2}
but admits a Lagrangian embedding with minimum Maslov number
2k
by performing Polterovich surgery on the double point on Whitney’s immersed sphere (Theorem 5 in
[
Pol91
]
).

(2)

In fact, the actual topological condition we use in the proof is the vanishing of the first Betti number of the based loop space of
L
.
We state the theorem in terms of
\pi_{2}L
to compare with the previous Maslov class rigidity results.

(3)

One of the initial motivations of this work is to understand the topology of Lagrangian 3-manifolds in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. The existence or non-existence of Lagrangian embeddings of
T^{3}\#T^{3}
, or connected sums of hyperbolic 3-manifolds, into
\mathbb{C}^{3}
is wide open (see e.g. Problem 11.1 in
[
Fuk06
]
, Question 2.1 in
[
Smi15
]
, Question 5 in
[
EK14
]
). However, these connected sums of 3-manifolds have an essential 2-sphere in the connected sum region and thus do not fall under our theorem. We hope to return to these examples in future works.

1.3.
A sketch of the proof

The proof of this theorem goes through the construction of a new Floer-theoretic invariant of Lagrangians in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which was conjectured and sketched by Abouzaid in
[
Abo16
]
, for applications e.g. in the
family Floer theory
construction of SYZ mirrors.
Here we state (imprecisely) an idealized version of this invariant to provide geometric pictures.
For the precise statement, which eventually gives us the same geometric consequences as this idealized version by some additional homological algebra, see Theorem
4.12
.

Let
\Omega_{\star}L
be the based loop space of
L
with basepoint
\star\in L
.
Under a good choice of the chain model, chains on
\Omega_{\star}L
has the structure of a dg associative algebra
(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,\partial,\bullet)
given by the Pontryagin product.
Moreover there is a unit
\underline{\star}\in C_{0}\Omega_{\star}L
given by the constant based loop at
\star\in L
.

Let
\theta:=\sum_{i}x_{i}\,dy_{i}
, a primitive to the standard symplectic form
\omega=\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}\wedge dy_{i}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Define a homomorphism
E\colon\pi_{1}L\to\mathbb{R}
given by the
symplectic energy

E(\gamma):=\int_{\gamma}\theta
.
Under the decomposition of
\Omega_{\star}L
into connected components
\Omega_{\star}(a)
labeled by
a\in\pi_{1}L
, there is a splitting
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L=\bigoplus_{a\in\pi_{1}L}C_{*}\Omega_{\star}(a)
compatible with the dg associative algebra structure.
We define the
energy filtration

\{\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
on
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L:=\bigoplus_{E(a)>\lambda}C_{*}\Omega_{\star}(a).

Denote by
\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
the completion with respect to the energy filtration, and
\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
the corresponding filtration levels.

Idealized Theorem 1.3
.

Let
L
be a closed, oriented, spin Lagrangian submanifold of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then there exists a constant
\hbar>0
and a (gapped) curved dg associative algebra structure on
\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
which is a deformation of the Pontryagin algebra
(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,\partial,\bullet)
, i.e. the data of

(0)

A constant
\hbar>0
;

(1)

\textup{{m}}_{0}\in\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
which lives in
\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
;

(2)

\textup{{m}}_{1}\colon\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}\to\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
given by
\textup{{m}}_{1}=\partial+\textup{{m}}_{1,+}
where
\partial
is induced by the classical differential on
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
and
\textup{{m}}_{1,+}
raises energy by at least
\hbar
;

(3)

\textup{{m}}_{2}\colon\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}^{\otimes 2}\to\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
, induced by the Pontryagin product
\bullet
on
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
,

satisfying

(1)

\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})=0
;

(2)

\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}(\alpha)=[\textup{{m}}_{0},\alpha]
for all
\alpha\in\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
, where the right-hand side is the graded commutator;

(3)

Leibniz rule.

Moreover, if
\mu_{L}\equiv 0
, then

(1)

\textup{{m}}_{0}=0
;

(2)

\deg\textup{{m}}_{1}=-1
;

(3)

There exists an element
\textup{{N}}^{\star}\in\widehat{C_{1}\Omega_{\star}L}
such that
\textup{{m}}_{1}\textup{{N}}^{\star}=\underline{\star}
.

We briefly explain the geometric picture of the terms (see section
2
for more details):

•

The curvature term
\textup{{m}}_{0}=\sum_{\beta\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}\in\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
is given by the boundaries loops of the pseudo-holomorphic discs which pass through the chosen basepoint
\star\in L
.
See Figure
1
.

\displaystyle L
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{n}
\displaystyle\star
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\beta)
A family of free loops from
boundaries of holomorphic
discs in
\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,1}(\beta)
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}_{0,\beta}

Definition of
\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}

•

The term
\textup{{m}}_{1,+}=\sum_{\beta\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\colon\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}\to\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
is a string topology operation, given by taking the intersection between the sweepout of a family of based loops and the geometric image of the boundary loops of pseudo-holomorphic curves, and concatenating them where they intersect. See Figure
2
.

Remark 1.4
.

In the context of homological mirror symmetry, especially in the case
L
is a Lagrangian torus (e.g. a smooth fibre of an SYZ fibration),
\textup{{m}}_{0}
can be thought of as encoding the information of the
superpotential
in the mirror local chart (see e.g.
[
Abo16
]
, section 3 of
[
Aur07
]
, as well as e.g.
[
Ton19
,
Yua25
]
).

In the main text, we sometimes refer to
\textup{{m}}_{0}
as the
anomaly
in order to distinguish it with the
curvature
in ordinary Lagrangian Floer theory of
[
Fuk+09
]
. The terminology of anomaly is also used in various similar contexts in string topology
[
Sul07
]
and Lagrangian Floer theory
[
Fuk+09
,
Fuk+09a
]
.

We expect that such a construction works for closed, (relatively) spin Lagrangian submanifold of any symplectic manifold that is either closed or convex at infinity. We only constructed the structure for Lagrangians in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
because of technical simplifications.
If one works with suitable chain-level intersection theory and virtual techniques, one could expect that Theorem
1.1
to be upgraded to apply to Hamiltonian displaceable Lagrangians in geometrically bounded symplectic manifolds.

A heuristic proof of how the idealized Theorem
1.3
implies Theorem
1.1
.

Assume
L
is a Lagrangian in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with vanishing Maslov class. Then
\textup{{m}}_{0}=0
, and thus
\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}=0
.
Using the energy filtration
\{\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
and gappedness of the energy spectrum, we construct a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration
\{\mathfrak{F}^{q}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}\}_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}
on
\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
by choosing a sufficiently fine subdivision of
\mathbb{R}
, e.g. by choosing
\lambda_{0}>0
satisfying a condition analogous to Condition 6.3.16 in
[
Fuk+09
]
, and set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}^{q}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}:=\mathscr{F}^{q\lambda_{0}}\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L},\quad q\in\mathbb{Z}.

Then take the associated spectral sequence. The
E_{1}
-page computes the (completion of) the ordinary homology
\widehat{H_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
of the based loop space, whereas the
E_{\infty}
-page computes the
\textup{{m}}_{1}
-homology, which is 0 by the existence of a primitive
\textup{{N}}^{\star}
of the identity class
\underline{\star}
under the differential
\textup{{m}}_{1}
, according to Theorem
1.3
.
Now
0\neq\underline{\star}\in\widehat{H_{0}\Omega_{\star}L}
in
E_{1}
-page, and since
\deg\textup{{m}}_{1}=-1
there needs to be some non-zero elements in
\widehat{H_{1}\Omega_{\star}L}
which kills
\underline{\star}
in the spectral sequence.
However by the assumption that
\pi_{2}L=0
, it follows that
\widehat{H_{1}\Omega_{\star}L}=0
, which gives a contradiction.

One can also prove Theorem
1.1
using a filtered version of the homological perturbation lemma, similar to section 2 of
[
Iri20
]
or section 5.4 of
[
Fuk+09
]
.
∎

The paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
contains a sketch of the construction in Theorem
1.3
, emphasizing geometric motivations and related works.
Section
3
is devoted to the construction of various structures in chain-level string topology (in particular a closed-open map) needed for the proof, with detailed verifications of properties (especially signs) relegated to Appendix
A
.
Section
4
is the construction of the curved dg algebra by incorporating contributions from the virtual fundamental chains on moduli spaces of holomorphic curves, with constructions of the relevant virtual fundamental chains relegated to Appendix
B
.
Section
5
contains the proof of the main theorem.

I would like to thank my advisor Mark McLean for constant support and encouragement. I would also like to thank Mohammed Abouzaid, Jiaji Cai, Spencer Cattalani, Yash Deshmukh, Ceyhun Elmacıoğlu, Kenji Fukaya, Sebastian Haney, Kei Irie, John Pardon, Dennis Sullivan, Chris Woodward, Guangbo Xu, and Frank Zheng for useful conversations or correspondences at various stages of the project.
This paper was partially supported by NSF award DMS-2203308 and also by Simons Foundation International, LTD.

2.
Heuristics of the construction and related works

In this section, we present a sketch of proof ignoring technical issues such as transversality (in chain-level string topology and moduli spaces of curves), with emphasis on geometric ideas and motivations. For example, we do not specify the chain model for the loop spaces (the reader may take
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
to mean singular chain complex) and we assume that all the intersections are transversal. The string topology operations are presented in a style similar to the exposition in
[
CS99
]
.

2.1.
Conventions and notations

Throughout, all mentions of “manifold” mean manifold-without-boundary, unless otherwise specified.
We denote the de Rham complex of a manifold
M
to be
\mathscr{A}^{*}(M)
and the subcomplex of compactly-supported forms to be
\mathscr{A}^{*}_{c}(M)
.
We shall work with real coefficients
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
: for a space
X
, unless otherwise specified,
H_{*}(X)
(
resp.

H^{*}(X)
) means the homology (
resp.
cohomology) of
X
with
\mathbb{R}
-coefficient.
We shall frequently identify
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
with
H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
\pi_{1}(L)
with
\pi_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
, without further mention.

Let
L
be a closed oriented manifold of dimension
n
, with a fixed basepoint
\star\in L
.
We identify
S^{1}
as the unit circle in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and fix a marked point
\star_{S^{1}}=1\in S^{1}
.

Fix a Lagrangian embedding
L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Recall the two associated invariants:

(1)

The symplectic energy
E\equiv E_{L}\colon\pi_{1}(L)\to\mathbb{R}
(or
E\equiv E_{L}\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}
);

(2)

The Maslov class
\mu\equiv\mu_{L}\colon\pi_{1}(L)\to\mathbb{Z}
(or
\mu\equiv\mu_{L}\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}
).

2.2.
Holomorphic discs and string topology

Our construction uses moduli spaces of holomorphic discs
[
Gro85
]
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L;\mathbb{Z})
, let
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta)
be the compactified moduli space of holomorphic discs (see e.g. section 7.2.2 of
[
Iri20
]
for a complete definition).
Its “main stratum” is the
uncompactified moduli space of pseudo-holomorphic disc
,
\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
, which we describe as follows (see also section 7.2.1 of
[
Iri20
]
). When
\beta=0
and
k=0
or
1
, define
\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{k}(\beta)=\emptyset
.
Otherwise, define
\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
as the space of equivalence classes of tuples
(u,z_{0},\dots,z_{k})
where

•

u\colon(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
is a smooth map satisfying
\bar{\partial}u=0
and
u_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{2}]=\beta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
;

•

z_{0},\dots,z_{k}\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
are distinct boundary marked points aligned in anti-clockwise order;

•

such that for each automorphism
\rho\in\textup{Aut}(\mathbb{D}^{2})
, we identify

(u,z_{0},\dots,z_{k})\sim(u\circ\rho,\rho^{-1}(z_{0}),\dots,\rho^{-1}(z_{k})).

For each
j=0,\dots,k
, define the
evaluation map

\textup{ev}_{j}\colon\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L,\quad\quad\textup{ev}_{j}(u,z_{0}\dots,z_{k}):=u(z_{j}).

Then the “codimension-1 stratum” of the 1-marked moduli space
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
can be described as the fibre product

(2.1)

\displaystyle\partial^{1}\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)\cong\bigsqcup_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{2}(\beta_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(\beta_{2})

(where in the above expression
\partial^{1}
denotes the codimension-1 boundary).

In
[
Fuk06
]
, Fukaya proposed the following perspective relating these moduli spaces with string topology:

(1)

View
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
as a chain in the free loop space
\mathscr{L}L:=\textup{Map}(S^{1},L)
. More precisely, one defines a map

\textup{ev}\colon\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)\to\mathscr{L}L

and pushforward the “virtual fundamental chain” on
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
to obtain a chain

\textup{{M}}(\beta):=\textup{ev}_{*}[\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)]\in C_{\dim L+\mu_{L}(\beta)-2}\mathscr{L}L;

(2)

View the codimension-1 degeneration (
2.1
) as a (chain-level) string topology operation — specifically, let

[-,-]\colon C_{*}\mathscr{L}L\otimes C_{*}\mathscr{L}L\to C_{*+1-\dim L}\mathscr{L}L

be a chain-level refinement of the loop bracket (see e.g.
[
CS99
]
).

Then (
2.1
) reads

\displaystyle\partial\textup{{M}}(\beta)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}[\textup{{M}}(\beta_{1}),\textup{{M}}(\beta_{2})]=0,

or, if we put together all the classes as an element
\textup{{M}}:=\sum_{\beta}\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in\widehat{C_{*}\mathscr{L}L}
in an appropriate completion of
C_{*}\mathscr{L}L
, the equation becomes a
Maurer-Cartan equation

\displaystyle\partial\textup{{M}}-\frac{1}{2}[\textup{{M}},\textup{{M}}]=0.

This can then be interpreted as a deformation of the dg Lie algebra structure on
\widehat{C_{*}\mathscr{L}L}
provided by the loop bracket. This is used in
[
Fuk06
,
Iri20
]
to prove a generalized version of Audin’s conjecture as well as a classification of orientable, closed, prime 3-manifolds admitting a Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{3}
.

2.3.
Perturbed based holomorphic discs and based loops

In
[
Oh97
]
, Oh defined certain moduli spaces to study the displacing energy of a Lagrangian.
We describe their codimension-0 strata as follows.

Fix a basepoint
\star\in L
.
Let
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
be a compactly supported time-dependent displacing Hamiltonian function satisfying Assumption
4.9
.
Let
\chi\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth function such that

•

\chi(s)\equiv 0
for
s\leq 0
;

•

\chi(s)\equiv 1
for
s\geq 1
.

Define, for each
r\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\chi_{r}(s):=\chi(r+s)\chi(r-s).

By identifying
\mathbb{D}^{2}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
with
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times[0,1]_{t}
, we obtain two coordinate functions
s\colon\mathbb{D}^{2}\setminus\{\pm 1\}\to\mathbb{R}
and
t\colon\mathbb{D}^{2}\setminus\{\pm 1\}\to[0,1].

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\eta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
, we define the
uncompactified moduli space of perturbed pseudo-holomorphic disc

\mathring{\mathscr{N}}^{\star}_{k+1}(\eta)
as the space of tuples
(r,u,z_{0}=1,z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
where

(1)

r\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
;

(2)

u\colon(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
is a smooth map satisfying the perturbed pseudo-holomorphic equation

\bar{\partial}_{r}(u):=\left(du-X_{\chi_{r}(s)H_{t}}(u)\otimes dt\right)^{0,1}=0

and
u_{*}[\mathbb{D}^{2}]=\eta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
;

(3)

1=z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{k}\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
are distinct boundary marked points aligned in anti-clockwise order;

(4)

u(z_{0})=\star
.

These spaces are equipped with evaluation maps

\displaystyle\textup{ev}_{j}\colon\mathring{\mathscr{N}}^{\star}_{k+1}(a)\to L,\quad\textup{ given by }\textup{ev}_{j}(u,z_{0},\dots,z_{k}):=u(z_{j})

for each
j=1,\dots,k
.

This construction is used in
[
Fuk06
]
and is basically the same as in
[
Iri20
]
, section 7.2.1 and 7.2.2,
except we require the marked point
z_{0}
to pass through the basepoint
\star\in L
(condition (4) above)
. Basically the same moduli space without condition (4) is also used by Abouzaid in
[
Abo12
]
.
Also see
[
BC02
,
Pol91b
]
.

The “codimension-1 stratum” of the 1-marked such moduli spaces
\mathscr{N}^{\star}_{1}(\eta)
consists of the following boundaries:

(1)

Bubbling
: A pseudo-holomorphic disc can bubble off from these perturbed pseudo-holomorphic discs, and depending on where
z_{0}
is positioned, we have the two parts in the codimension-1 boundary:

(2.2)

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\eta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\eta}\mathscr{N}_{2}^{\star}(\eta_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta_{2})

and

(2.3)

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\beta_{1}+\eta_{2}=\eta}\mathscr{M}_{2}^{\star}(\beta_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{N}_{1}(\eta_{2})

where the moduli spaces in the above expressions with the
\star
superscript consist of curves satisfying
u(z_{0})=\star
, and ones without consist of curves not necessarily satisfying
u(z_{0})=\star
. For example,
\mathscr{M}_{2}^{\star}(\beta_{1}):=\textup{ev}_{0}^{-1}(\mathscr{M}_{2}(\beta_{1}))
.

(2)

r=0
: When the deformation parameter
r
becomes 0, the perturbed Cauchy-Riemann operator
\overline{\partial}_{0}=\bar{\partial}
becomes the unperturbed Cauchy-Riemann operator, so the curves in this boundary
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{0,\star}(\eta)
are simply (unperturbed) pseudo-holomorphic curves. In particular,
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{0,\star}(\eta)=\emptyset
unless (i)
E(\eta)>0
or (ii)
\eta=0
. Moreover, in case
\eta=0
,
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{0,\star}(0)
is the singleton set consisting of the constant map to
\star\in L
.

That is,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\partial^{1}\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\star}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{1}^{0,\star}(\eta)\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{\eta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\eta}\mathscr{N}_{2}^{\star}(\eta_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta_{2})\right)\sqcup\left(\bigsqcup_{\beta_{1}+\eta_{2}=\eta}\mathscr{M}_{2}^{\star}(\beta_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{N}_{1}(\eta_{2})\right).

Following Fukaya’s proposal outlined above, we interpret these moduli spaces in terms of string topology:

(1)

View
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\star}(\eta)
as a chain in a loop space, but this time the based loop space
\Omega_{\star}L:=\textup{Map}((S^{1},\star_{S^{1}}),(L,\star))
where
\star_{S^{1}}
is a fixed marked point in
S^{1}
: there is an evaluation map

\textup{ev}\colon\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\star}(\eta)\to\Omega_{\star}L

and we pushforward the virtual fundamental chain on
\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\star}(\eta)
to obtain a chain

\textup{{N}}^{\star}(\eta):=\textup{ev}_{*}[\mathscr{N}_{1}^{\star}(\eta)]\in C_{\mu_{L}(\eta)+1}\Omega_{\star}L;

Also, define

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}^{0,\star}(\eta):=\textup{ev}_{*}[\mathscr{N}_{1}^{0,\star}(\eta)]\in C_{\mu_{L}(\eta)}\Omega_{\star}L.

(2)

View the codimension-1 degeneration (
2.2
) as a (chain-level) string topology operation — specifically, for each
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L;\mathbb{Z})
, define

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\colon C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L\to C_{*+\mu_{L}(\beta)-1}\Omega_{\star}L

as following, assuming we are using singular chains and assuming transversality for now. Given a
k
-chain
K_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\Omega_{\star}L
where
K_{\alpha}
is the underlying domain of the chain
\alpha
, define
\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha
to be the chain
K_{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha}\xrightarrow{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha}\Omega_{\star}L
where
K_{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}}
is the pre-image of the diagonal of
L\times L
under

(2.5)

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}\times[0,1]\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)

\displaystyle\to L\times L

\displaystyle(k_{x},s,u)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\alpha(k_{x})(s),\textup{ev}_{0}(u)=u(1))

where we view
\alpha(k_{x})
as a map
S^{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha(k_{x})}L
and
u
as a map
(\mathbb{D}^{2},\partial\mathbb{D}^{2})\xrightarrow{u}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
(where
\mathbb{D}^{2}
is identified as the unit disc of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
). Then define
K_{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha}\xrightarrow{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha}\Omega_{\star}L
by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\big((\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha)(k_{x},s,u)\big)(\tau):=\begin{cases}\alpha(k_{x})(2\tau),&\tau\in[0,\frac{s}{2})\\
u(e^{2\pi i(2\tau-s)}),&\tau\in[\frac{s}{2},\frac{s+1}{2})\\
\alpha(k_{x})(2\tau-1),&\tau\in[\frac{s+1}{2},1]\end{cases}.

This is analogous to the pre-Lie product
*
in section 3 of
[
CS99
]
. See Figure
2
.

(3)

The codimension-1 degeneration (
2.3
) doesn’t have a good description in terms of chains in the based loop space because
\mathscr{N}_{1}(\eta_{2})
is not part of
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
; it is contributed by moduli spaces
\mathscr{M}_{1}^{\star}(\beta):=\textup{ev}_{0}^{-1}(\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta))
of holomorphic discs whose boundary pass through
\star\in L
. We record this data in the form of

\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}:=\textup{ev}_{*}[\mathscr{M}_{1}^{\star}(\beta)]\in C_{\mu_{L}(\beta)-2}\Omega_{\star}L,

where
\textup{ev}\colon\mathscr{M}_{1}^{\star}(\beta)\to\Omega_{\star}L
is the evaluation map. See Figure
1
.

Recall that based loop concatenation endows the based loop space
\Omega_{\star}L
with a product (assume for simplicity that we are using a model of based loop space where the product is strictly associative, e.g. the Moore loop space):

\displaystyle\bullet\colon\Omega_{\star}L\times\Omega_{\star}L\to\Omega_{\star}L

which makes the chains on
\Omega_{\star}L
into a dg associative algebra
(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,\partial,\bullet)
with the Pontryagin product

\displaystyle\bullet\colon C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L\otimes C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L\to C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L.

This dg associative algebra has a unit
\underline{\star}\in C_{0}\Omega_{\star}L
, which is the 0-cycle given by the constant loop at the basepoint
\star\in L
.

Then, equation (
2.2
) is translated into the equation

(2.7)

\displaystyle\partial\textup{{N}}^{\star}(\eta)+\sum_{\beta}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}(\textup{{N}}^{\star}(\eta-\beta))+(\textup{terms involving }\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta})=\textup{{N}}^{0,\star}(\eta),

or, if we define, in an appropriate completion
\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
,

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}^{\star}:=\sum_{\eta}\textup{{N}}^{\star}(\eta),\quad\textup{{m}}_{1}:=\partial+\sum_{\beta}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta},\quad\textup{{m}}_{0}:=\sum_{\beta}\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta},

and notice that

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}^{0,\star}(\eta)=\begin{cases}\underline{\star},&\eta=0\\
0,&E(\eta)<0\end{cases},

summing up equations (
2.7
) for all
\eta
gives

(2.8)

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}\textup{{N}}^{\star}+(\textup{terms involving }\textup{{m}}_{0})=\underline{\star}+(\textup{terms of energy }E>\hbar)

where
\hbar>0
is a constant (given by the minimal holomorphic disc energy).
The right hand side is invertible in the completion
\widehat{C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L}
so we might as well pretend it is the unit
\underline{\star}
.

We have now defined the structures in our idealized Theorem
1.3
. Most of the properties follow from the description (in an ideal situation, ignoring technical issues). For example, when
\mu_{L}=0
, the degree of
\textup{{m}}_{1}
follows directly from dimension-counting, and
\deg\textup{{m}}_{0}=-2
so it should not contribute (homologically), as
H_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
is concentrated in non-negative degrees. Property (3) follows from (
2.8
), since
\textup{{m}}_{0}
vanishes in this situation and the right-hand side is invertible.
We now explain the heuristic for the proof of the identity
\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}(\alpha)=[\textup{{m}}_{0},\alpha]
(where
\alpha\in C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
), modulo signs.

Notice that the identity
\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}(\alpha)=[\textup{{m}}_{0},\alpha]
is equivalent to

(2.9)

\displaystyle\partial\left(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha\right)+\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\left(\partial\alpha\right)+\sum_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}\left(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{2}}\alpha\right)=\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}\bullet\alpha-\alpha\bullet\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}

for each
\beta\in H_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n},L;\mathbb{Z})
.
By our explicit descriptions (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) of
\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha
, its boundary
\partial\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}\alpha
consists of

(1)

The part of
K_{\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}}\subset K_{\alpha}\times[0,1]\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
contained in
\partial K_{\alpha}\times[0,1]\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
, which is responsible for the term
\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}(\partial\alpha)
;

(2)

The part contained in
K\times[0,1]\times\partial\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
, where
\partial\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
is given in (
2.1
); this is responsible for the term
\sum_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{2}}\alpha)
(technically
\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{2}}\alpha)
consists also of compositions of loops where curves from
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta_{1})
and
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta_{2})
lands on two different points of curves in
\alpha
, but they cancel with the same term coming from
\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{2}}(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}\alpha)
in the same way similar to Lemma 4.2 in
[
CS99
]
);

(3)

The part contained in
K_{\alpha}\times\{0,1\}\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
; for an element
(k_{x},s,u)
in this (where
s\in\{0,1\}
), we have
u(\star_{S^{1}})=\star\in L
and acccording to (
2.6
), the part of the boundary in
K_{\alpha}\times\{0\}\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
gives the term
\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}\bullet\alpha
and the part in
K_{\alpha}\times\{1\}\times\mathscr{M}_{1}(\beta)
gives the term
\alpha\bullet\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}
.

This verifies (
2.9
).

2.4.
Open-closed string topology

The way we construct the curved dg algebra is via the construction of a closed-open map in chain-level string topology. We comment on the motivation and other works relevant to this construction.

A general theme in Floer-Fukaya type theories of Lagrangian submanifolds is that
pseudo-holomorphic curves provide quantum deformations of classical algebraic topology.

For example, the Lagrangian Floer (co)homology is an
A_{\infty}
-deformation of the ordinary (co)homology of the Lagrangian submanifold
[
Fuk+09
]
.
The work of
[
Fuk06
,
Iri18
,
Iri20
]
provided a conceptual explanation for Lagrangian Floer cohomology as a deformation of ordinary cohomology via the iterated integration map of Chen
[
Che73
]
:

\displaystyle C_{*}\mathscr{L}L\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C^{*}L,C^{*}L)

where
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C^{*}L,C^{*}L)
is the Hochschild cochain complex of the dg associative algebra
C^{*}L
(with the cup product).

In e.g.
[
CS99
,
CS04
,
Sul04
,
Sul07
]
, a rich collection of algebraic operations on various loop/path spaces are uncovered, including interactions between closed sector (e.g. free loop space) and the open sector (e.g. based loop space), under the name of
open-closed string topology
.
Algebraic frameworks and detailed constructions of various parts of this are done in e.g.
[
MS06
,
Cos07
,
God08
,
BCT09
,
CG
]
.
Combining ideas from open-closed string topology and work of
[
Fuk06
,
Iri20
]
, we construct a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras, a closed-open string map

(2.10)

\displaystyle C_{*}\mathscr{L}L\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L)

where
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L)
is the Hochschild cochain complex of the dg associative algebra
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
(with the Pontryagin product). See Theorem
3.1
.

Remark 2.1
.

The relation between homology of free loop spaces and Hochschild cohomology of chains on based loop spaces is first studied by
[
Goo85
,
BF86
]
. Also see
[
Mal10
]
.

The Maurer-Cartan element
\textup{{M}}\in\widehat{C_{*}\mathscr{L}L}
constructed in
[
Fuk06
,
Iri20
]
out of moduli spaces of holomorphic discs is then pushed forward to a Maurer-Cartan element in
\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}(C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L,C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L)
, a suitable completion of the Hochschild complex, and which is interpreted as a curved deformation of the dg associative algebra
C_{*}\Omega_{\star}L
using deformation theory. See section
4.1
for details.

Remark 2.2
.

The map (
2.10
) can be viewed as a string topology version of the closed-open map in e.g.
[
Abo10
,
Gan12
]
. In the context of symplectic Floer theory, the Maurer-Cartan element can be viewed in terms of a Borman-Sheridan class using (an extension of) the construction in
[
BSV22
]
. One could then possibly use this language to generalize our result to certain singular Lagrangian submanifolds.

3.
A closed-open map in string topology

3.1.
Summary of structures

Let
L
be a closed oriented manifold of dimension
n
, with a fixed basepoint
\star\in L
.
The ingredients of the constructions in this section are purely topological and are intrinsic to
L
itself, but we are going to fix a Lagrangian embedding
i_{L}\colon L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
(not assumed to have vanishing Maslov class), where
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is endowed the standard symplectic structure, which we shall use to define the gradings of our constructions for later convenience.
Specifically we will use the Maslov index
\mu:=\mu_{L}\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}
of the Lagrangian embedding.

As before, denote by

\mathscr{L}L:=\textup{Map}(S^{1},L)

the free loop space of
L
, and

\Omega_{\star}L:=\textup{Map}((S^{1},\star_{S^{1}}),(L,\star))

the based loop space of
L
.
For each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, define
\mathscr{L}(a)
(
resp.

\Omega_{\star}(a)
) to be the space of loops in
\mathscr{L}L
(
resp.

\Omega_{\star}L
) with homology class
a
.
We are only concerned with the homotopy types of these spaces, which are independent of the regularity of the loops (see e.g. section 2.1 of
[
CO15
]
), so we do not specify the regularity.

Theorem 3.1

(Open-closed string topology package)
.

Associate to a closed oriented manifold
L
of dimension
n
together with a Lagrangian embedding
i_{L}\colon L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
are the following structures:

(1)

(Closed string state space) A dg Lie algebra
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
with a decomposition:

C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}:=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a),\quad\partial\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}[-1],\quad[-,-]\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\otimes C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},

which computes the free loop space homology
H_{*}(\mathscr{L}L)
1
1

1

One could expect that the dg Lie algebra descends to the Chas-Sullivan Lie algebra on
H_{*}(\mathscr{L}L;\mathbb{K})
in Proposition 4.3 of
[
CS99
]
, although this is not relevant for our purpose. See the second remark in Section 2.5 of
[
Wan23
]
.
up to grading shifts. More precisely, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle H_{*}(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a),\partial)\cong H_{*+n+\mu(a)-1}(\mathscr{L}(a)).

(2)

(Open string state space) A dg associative algebra with a decomposition:

C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a),\quad\partial\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}[-1],\quad\bullet\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\otimes C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},

which computes the based loop space homology
H_{*}(\Omega_{\star}L)
together with the Pontryagin (concatenation) product, up to a grading shift. More precisely, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle H_{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a),\partial)\cong H_{*+\mu(a)}(\Omega_{\star}(a)),

and

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a)\cong\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}H_{*+\mu(a)}(\Omega_{\star}(a))

as graded algebras.

(3)

(Closed-open string map) A homomorphism between dg Lie algebras preserving the decomposition into
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
classes:

\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})

where
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
is a model of Hochschild cochains together with the Hochschild differential and the Gerstenhaber Lie bracket (see section
3.5.2
for the precise definitions).

Remarks 3.2
.

(1)

For grading and sign conventions regarding dg algebras, see section
A.3
.

(2)

This structure is only a small part of the open-closed string topology package; see the discussion and references in section
2.4
, as well as Remarks
3.20
and
3.22
.

(3)

An “open-closed map” in string topology is constructed in section 4 of
[
Abo11
]
; our construction follows the same vein, as well as ideas from e.g.
[
Abo16
]
. The technical framework of the proof of Theorem
3.1
is heavily inspired by
[
Iri20
,
Wan23
]
.

For readability, the main text in this section is mainly focused on constructions of the relevant structures, with verifications of properties relegated to Appendix
A
.

The organization of this section is as follows. In section
3.2
we review the de Rham chain complex construction in
[
Iri18
]
which we will use to build our chain models. In section
3.3
we define the chain complexes
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial)
and
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial)
in part (1) and (2) of Theorem
3.1
, following the approach of
[
Wan23
]
. In section
3.4
we define various string topology operations on de Rham chains, which we put together in section
3.5
to obtain the stated structures in Theorem
3.1
.

3.2.
Irie’s machinery of de Rham chains

The machinery we shall use for solving chain-level transversality problems is the formalism of de Rham chains, first proposed by Fukaya (under the name “approximate de Rham chains” in
[
Fuk06
]
) and rigorously constructed by Irie
[
Iri18
,
Iri20
]
.
We now briefly review this machinery on free and based loop spaces, heavily borrowing from
[
Iri18
]
.
For conventions on orientations, see section
A.1
.

We are going to use “the space of all manifolds” as domains of the chains. To avoid set-theoretic issues, let
\mathscr{U}
be the set of all oriented
m
-dimensional submanifolds of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for all
0\leq m\leq N
.

Definition 3.3

(
[
Iri18
]
, Section 4.2)
.

A
differentiable space
is a set
X
together with a collection of maps, called
plots
, from
U\in\mathscr{U}
to
X
, satisfying the property that

•

If
\varphi\colon U\to X
is a plot,
U^{\prime}\in\mathscr{U}
and
\theta\colon U^{\prime}\to U
is a submersion, then
\varphi\circ\theta\colon U^{\prime}\to X
for
X
is a plot for
Y
.

A map
f\colon X\to Y
between differentiable spaces is said to be
smooth
if it is a map of sets such that composition of
f
with a plot
\varphi\colon U\to X
is a plot.

Example 3.4

(
[
Iri18
]
, Example 4.2 (i))
.

Let
L
be a smooth manifold.
We define a differentiable space with underlying set
L
itself by stipulating a map
\varphi\colon U\to L
to be a plot if
\varphi
is smooth.

Definition 3.5
.

[
[
Iri18
]
, Section 4.3]
Let
X
be a differentiable space. Define the
de Rham chain complex
of
X
to be

C_{j}^{\textup{dR}}(X):=\left(\bigoplus_{(U,\varphi)}\mathscr{A}_{c}^{\dim U-j}(U)\right)\bigg/\sim

where

•

The direct sum is taken over all
(U,\varphi)
where
U\in\mathscr{U}
and
\varphi\colon U\to X
is a plot;

•

The notation
\mathscr{A}_{c}^{\dim U-j}(U)
is the space of compactly supported smooth forms on
U
of degree
\dim U-j
. Denote an element
\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{\dim U-j}(U)
belonging to the the summand labeled by
(U,\varphi)
to be
(U,\varphi,\omega)
; frequently, to signify the target space
X
, we will also use the notation
(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}X;\omega)
instead to mean the same object as
(U,\varphi,\omega)
.

•

The equivalence relation is taken by quotienting out the subspace generated by

(3.1)

\displaystyle(U,\varphi,\pi_{!}\omega)-(U^{\prime},\varphi\circ\pi,\omega)

where
U,U^{\prime}\in\mathscr{U}
,
\pi\colon U^{\prime}\to U
is a submersion,
\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(U^{\prime})
, and
\pi_{!}\omega
denotes integration over the fibres (which is well-defined since
\omega
is compactly supported).
Denote the equivalence class of the element
(U,\varphi,\omega)
to be
[(U,\varphi,\omega)]\in C_{j}^{\textup{dR}}(X)
, or
[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}X;\omega)]
. In later computations we often drop the square bracket when the meaning is clear for notational simplicity.

•

The differential
\partial^{\textup{dR}}\colon C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(X)\to C_{*-1}^{\textup{dR}}(X)
is defined by

(3.2)

\partial^{\textup{dR}}[(U,\varphi,\omega)]:=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}[(U,\varphi,d\omega)].

Then it follows immediately that a smooth map
f\colon X\to Y
between smooth manifolds induces a pushforward

f_{*}\colon C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(X)\to C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(Y).

Notation 3.6
.

For
x\in C_{j}^{\textup{dR}}(X)
, we write
\deg x:=j
.

Remarks 3.7
.

Consider
U\in\mathscr{U}
and
\overline{U}
denoting
U
with the opposite orientation. The identity map
\textup{id}\colon U\to\overline{U}
is a submersion, but recall sign convention of integration along the fibre (Section 4.2.3 of
[
Iri20
]
) is such that for a submersion
\pi\colon X\to Y
,

\int_{Y}\pi_{!}\omega\wedge\eta=\int_{X}\omega\wedge\pi^{*}\eta

for any
\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(X)
and
\eta\in\mathscr{A}^{*}(Y)
. Therefore for any
\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(U)
, under the orientation-reversing
\pi\colon U\to\overline{U}
, the pushout
\pi_{!}\omega=-\omega
. In particular,

[(U,\varphi,\omega)]=-[(\overline{U},\varphi,\omega)].

3.3.
Definition of the state spaces

Let
L
be as before.
Following the construction of Wang in
[
Wan23
]
(which is a generalization of Adams’s cobar construction
[
Ada56
]
to non-simply-connected cases), we will construct cosimplicial models of the free and based loop spaces, to which we shall apply the machinery of de Rham chains in section
3.2
to obtain the chain complexes for the state spaces.

Let
\Pi_{1}L
be the fundamental groupoid:

\Pi_{1}L:=\{(p,q,[\sigma])\mid p,q\in L,[\sigma]\in\mathcal{P}_{p,q}/\textup{homotopy}\}.

Denote the source and sink maps by

s

\displaystyle\colon\Pi_{1}L\to L;\quad\textsf{s}(p,q,[\sigma]):=p

t

\displaystyle\colon\Pi_{1}L\to L;\quad\textsf{t}(p,q,[\sigma]):=q.

Definition 3.8
.

(1)

Given two composable elements
(p,q,[\sigma]),(q,r,[\tau])\in\Pi_{1}L
, denote their concatenation (the groupoid multiplication) by

(3.3)

\displaystyle(p,q,[\sigma])*(q,r,[\tau]):=(p,r,[\sigma*\tau]).

(2)

Given a point
y\in L
, denote by
\underline{y}\in\mathcal{P}_{p,q}
the constant path at
y
, by
[\underline{y}]\in\mathcal{P}_{p,q}/\textup{homotopy}
the homotopy class, and also by
[\underline{y}]
the element
(y,y,[\underline{y}])\in\Pi_{1}L
.

Define

•

\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
denote the space of elements
(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})\in(\Pi_{1}L)^{k+1}
such that
\textsf{t}(c_{i})=\textsf{s}(c_{i+1})
for all
i=0,\dots,k-1
, and
\textsf{t}(c_{k})=\textsf{s}(c_{0})
.
Then we have the evaluation maps

\textup{ev}=(\textup{ev}_{0},\cdots,\textup{ev}_{k})\colon\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L\to L^{\times(k+1)},\quad(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})\mapsto(\textsf{s}(c_{0}),\textsf{s}(c_{1}),\dots,\textsf{s}(c_{k}));

•

\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L
, where
\star\in L
is the basepoint, denote the space of elements
(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})\in(\Pi_{1}L)^{k+1}
such that
\textsf{t}(c_{i})=\textsf{s}(c_{i+1})
for all
i=0,\dots,k-1
, and
\textsf{t}(c_{k})=\textsf{s}(c_{0})=\star
. Then we have the evaluation maps

\textup{ev}=(\textup{ev}_{1},\dots,\textup{ev}_{k})\colon\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L\to L^{\times k},\quad(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})\mapsto(\textsf{s}(c_{1}),\dots,\textsf{s}(c_{k})).

Lemma 3.9
.

The maps
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}}L^{\times(k+1)}
,
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}}L^{\times k}
are covering maps.

Pulling back the smooth manifold structure on
L^{\times(k+1)}
(
resp.

L^{\times k}
), we obtain a smooth manifold structure on
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
(
resp.

\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}L
) of dimension
(k+1)(\dim L)
(
resp.

k(\dim L)
), so that each of the evaluation maps

\textup{ev}_{i}\colon\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L\to L,\quad\textup{ev}_{j}\colon\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}L\to L,\quad\textup{ where }i=0,\dots,k,\textup{ and }j=1,\dots k

is a smooth map.

We now specify the structures of
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
and
\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}L
as differentiable spaces:

Definition 3.10

(Differentiable spaces structures on
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
and
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L
)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

•

For
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L
, we regard it as a differentiable space simply using its smooth structure (i.e. a map
\varphi\colon U\to\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L
is a plot if
\varphi
is smooth; see Example
3.4
).

•

For
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
, we need an additional constraint: a map
\varphi\colon U\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
is a plot if

(1)

\varphi
is smooth;

(2)

\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi\colon U\to L
is a submersion.

This allows us to define, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the de Rham chain complexes
(C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L),\partial^{\textup{dR}})
and
(C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}L),\partial^{\textup{dR}})
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
denote either of the symbols
\mathscr{L}
or
\Omega_{\star}
.
Then, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

(C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{X}^{k+1}L),\partial^{\textup{dR}})
computes the ordinary homology
H_{*}(\mathcal{X}^{k+1}L)
.

We will be a bit sketchy in the proof about the
\mathcal{X}=\mathscr{L}
case, since strictly speaking we only need the
\mathcal{X}=\Omega_{\star}
part of the lemma for this paper.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{X}=\Omega_{\star}
, this is Theorem 5.1 of
[
Iri18
]
since
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}L
is an oriented smooth manifold and the plots we used are just smooth maps.
For
\mathcal{X}=\mathscr{L}
, since the plots used to define the de Rham chain complex need to satisfy additionally that the compositions with
\textup{ev}_{0}\colon\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L\to L
are submersions (condition (2) in Definition
3.10
), we also need to show that the chain complex is quasi-isomorphic to the one defined using all smooth maps
U\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
as plots. This is a finite-dimensional analogue of Lemma 7.7 in
[
Iri18
]
and the proof is completely analogous.
∎

Still following
[
Wan23
]
,
we then construct cosimplicial spaces
\mathcal{X}L
as a collection of spaces
\{\mathcal{X}^{k+1}L\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
together with the following structure maps (where
\mathcal{X}
denotes either
\mathscr{L}
or
\Omega_{\star}
):

\displaystyle\delta_{i}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{X}^{k}L\to\mathcal{X}^{k+1}L;\quad\delta_{i}(c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1}):=\begin{cases}(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-1},\underline{\textsf{s}(c_{i})},c_{i},\dots,c_{k-1}),&0\leq i\leq k-1\\
(c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1},\underline{\textsf{t}(c_{k-1})}),&i=k\end{cases}

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{X}^{k}L\to\mathcal{X}^{k+1}L;\quad\sigma_{i}(c_{0},\dots,c_{k+1}):=(c_{0},\dots,c_{i}*c_{i+1},\dots,c_{k+1}),\quad i=0,\dots,k,

where for a point
y\in L
,
\underline{y}
denotes the constant path at
y
, and
*
denotes composition (see Definition
3.8
).

For each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, recall that we define
\mathscr{L}(a)
to be the component in
\mathscr{L}L
consisting of loops whose homology class is
a
, and
\Omega_{\star}(a)
to be the component in
\Omega_{\star}L
consisting of loops whose homology class is
a
.

Similarly, for each element
(c_{0},\dots,c_{k})
in
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
or
\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}L
, there is a well-defined homology class
a\in H_{1}(L)
associated to
c_{0}*\dots*c_{k}\in\Pi_{1}L
based at
\textsf{s}(c_{0})=\textsf{t}(c_{k})
, and denote by
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)
and
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a)
accordingly.

Definition 3.12
.

For each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define

\displaystyle C^{\mathscr{L}}(a,k)_{*}:=C^{\textup{dR}}_{*+\dim L+\mu(a)+k-1}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))

and

\displaystyle C^{\Omega_{\star}}(a,k)_{*}:=C^{\textup{dR}}_{*+\mu(a)+k}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a)).

The
closed-string state space
is then defined as

C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}:=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\underbrace{\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\mathscr{L}}(a,k)_{*}}_{=:C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a)}

and the
open-string state space
is defined as

C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}:=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\underbrace{\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\Omega_{\star}}(a,k)_{*}}_{=:C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a)}.

Notation 3.13
.

To clarify the notation on grading, recall that (see Notation
3.6
) for an element
C_{\ell}^{\textup{dR}}(X)
, we use the notation
\deg x=\ell
; more concretely given a de Rham chain
x=[(U,\varphi,\omega)]
, its degree
\deg x:=\dim U-|\omega|
(where
|\omega|
is the degree of
\omega
as a differential form). In contrast, for an (homogeneous) element
x\in C_{\ell}^{\mathcal{X}}
, we use the notation
|x|=\ell
. Thus, in particular,

•

For
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, the
(a,k)
-component
x(a,k)
has

(3.4)

\deg x(a,k)=|x|+\dim L+\mu(a)+k-1;

•

For
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, the
(a,k)
-component
\alpha(a,k)
has

(3.5)

\deg\alpha(a,k)=|\alpha|+\mu(a)+k.

Remark 3.14
.

The grading on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
is defined to be consistent with that in
[
Iri20
]
. The same grading-shift convention (with a Riemann-Roch formula term appearing to homogenize the degrees of the operations) appears also in e.g.
[
Sul05
]
, in the context of closed pseudo-holomorphic curves.

The differentials on both of the state spaces
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
are defined by

(3.6)

\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1}

where

(3.7)

(\partial^{0}x)(a,k):=\partial^{\textup{dR}}(x(a,k)),\quad(\partial^{1}x)(a,k):=(-1)^{\dim L+|x|}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(x(a,k-1)).

It is clear that
\partial
preserves the decompositions

C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a),\quad C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a).

To state the result in
[
Wan23
]
that
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
indeed compute the correct homology groups,
we first need to define the differentiable space structures (and thus de Rham chain complexes) on the
C^{\infty}
free and based loop spaces as in Example 4.2 (ii) of
[
Iri18
]
and show that
C^{\textup{dR}}_{*}(\mathscr{L}L)
,
C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}L)
compute the ordinary homologies of the spaces (in a way similar to section 6 of
[
Iri18
]
).
We then define the chain complexes, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,

\displaystyle C_{*}^{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}(a)):=\left(\prod_{k\geq 0}C^{\textup{dR}}_{*+\dim L+\mu(a)+k-1}(\mathscr{L}(a)\times\Delta^{k}),\partial\right),

\displaystyle C_{*}^{\Delta}(\Omega_{\star}(a)):=\left(\prod_{k\geq 0}C^{\textup{dR}}_{*+\mu(a)+k}(\Omega_{\star}(a)\times\Delta^{k}),\partial\right),

coming from the cosimplicial structure given by the standard
k
-simplices

\Delta^{k}:=\{(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}\mid 0\leq t_{1}\leq\dots\leq t_{k}\leq 1\}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
\mathcal{X}\in\{\mathscr{L},\Omega_{\star}\}
, define

\displaystyle e_{k}\colon\mathcal{X}(a)\times\Delta^{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{X}^{k+1}(a),

\displaystyle\quad(\gamma,t_{1},\dots,t_{k})

\displaystyle\mapsto\big((\gamma(t_{i}),\gamma(t_{i+1}),[\gamma|_{[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}]\big)_{0\leq i\leq k}\in\mathcal{X}^{k+1}(a)\subset(\Pi_{1}L)^{k+1}.

This induces a cosimplicial map for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
:

e_{*}\colon C_{*}^{\Delta}(\mathcal{X}(a))\to C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}}(a).

On the other hand, we have the chain maps

\displaystyle\textup{pr}_{0}\colon(C_{*}^{\Delta}(\mathscr{L}(a)),\partial)\to(C_{*+\dim L+\mu(a)-1}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}(a)),\partial^{\textup{dR}}),\quad(x_{k})_{k\geq 0}\mapsto x_{0};

\displaystyle\textup{pr}_{0}\colon(C_{*}^{\Delta}(\Omega_{\star}(a)),\partial)\to(C_{*+\mu(a)}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}(a)),\partial^{\textup{dR}}),\quad(x_{k})_{k\geq 0}\mapsto x_{0}.

The following theorem then follows from Theorem 2.2.1 and Lemma 2.2.3 in
[
Wan23
]
and section 6 of
[
Iri18
]
(Section 6 of
[
Iri18
]
shows that de Rham chains on the
C^{\infty}
-free loop spaces compute ordinary homology, but the argument can be adapted to based loop spaces in exactly the same way):

Theorem 3.15
.

Both of the maps
e_{*}
and
\textup{pr}_{0}
are quasi-isomorphisms, for either
\mathcal{X}=\mathscr{L}
or
\Omega_{\star}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle H_{*}(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a),\partial)\cong H_{*+\dim L+\mu(a)-1}(\mathscr{L}(a));

\displaystyle H_{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a),\partial)\cong H_{*+\mu(a)}(\Omega_{\star}(a)).

Technically the
(\partial^{1}x)
term in (
3.7
) may have the opposite sign as that in
[
Wan23
]
depending on
\dim L
; however in dimensions where this happen, the statement still holds because the chain map

\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}}(a,k)_{*}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{k}}\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}}(a,k)_{*}

is an isomorphism of chain complexes.

3.4.
String topology operations on de Rham chains

In this section we construct various string topology operations on de Rham chains on the components of the cosimplicial chain complexes
C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
and
C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a))
.

3.4.1.
Closed-string

Define the (closed-string) concatenation map, for
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
i=1,\dots,k_{1}
,

\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}\colon\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})\to\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2})

by

\displaystyle\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}\big((c_{0},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),(c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{k_{2}}^{\prime})\big):=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-2},c_{i-1}*c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\dots,c^{\prime}_{k_{2}-1},c^{\prime}_{k_{2}}*c_{i},c_{i+1},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),&k_{2}\geq 1\\
(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-2},c_{i-1}*c_{0}^{\prime}*c_{i},c_{i+1},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),&k_{2}=0\end{cases}.

Roughly speaking,
\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}
looks for where the
i
-th marked point of the first loop coincides with the
0
-th marked point of the second loop, and concatenate the two at the coinciding point (compare with the construction of the loop product).
Henceforth we will often write
\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}
instead of
\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}
whenever the context is clear.

Let
x\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
and
y\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
.
For each
i=1,\dots,k_{1}
, we define a chain
x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{\deg x+\deg y+\dim L}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2}))
as (up to signs) the composition of the chain-level fibre product and the chain-level pushforward along
\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}
. Explicitly, if
x=[(U_{1},\varphi_{1},\omega_{1})]
,
y=[(U_{2},\varphi_{2},\omega_{2})]
, then, using the abbreviation

U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}:=U_{1}\,{}_{\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\varphi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{2}}U_{2}

(this is a transverse fibre product under our assumption that
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{2}
is a submersion; see Definition
3.10
), define

(3.8)

x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y:=(-1)^{(\dim U_{1}-|\omega_{1}|-\dim L)|\omega_{2}|}[(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}\varphi_{2},\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}|_{U_{1}{}_{i}\times_{0}U_{2}})].

where
\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}\varphi_{2}
denotes the composition

U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})\xrightarrow{\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2}).

We shall frequently abuse notation by making the restriction of
\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}
to the correct domain
U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}
implicit.

Hence this induces a map

\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}\colon C_{i_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))\otimes C^{\textup{dR}}_{i_{2}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))\to C^{\textup{dR}}_{i_{1}+i_{2}-\dim L}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2}))

for
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
i=1,\dots,k_{1}
.

See Appendix
A.2.1
for properties of
\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}
.

3.4.2.
Open-string

Define the (open-string) concatenation map, for
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
and
a_{1},a_{2}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,

*\colon\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1})\times\Omega^{k_{2}+1}_{\star}(a_{2})\to\Omega^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}+a_{2})

by

(c_{0},\dots,c_{k_{1}})*(c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{k_{2}}^{\prime})\mapsto(c_{0},\dots,c_{k_{1}-1},c_{k_{1}}*c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\dots,c_{k_{2}}^{\prime}).

This induces a map of de Rham chain complexes

\bullet\colon C_{i_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}))\otimes C_{i_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))\to C_{i_{1}+i_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}(a_{1}+a_{2})).

We spell this out more explicitly to fix the sign: for
\alpha\in C_{i_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}))
given by
\alpha=[(V_{1},\psi_{1},\eta_{1})]
,
\beta\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{i_{2}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
given by
\beta=[(V_{2},\psi_{2},\eta_{2})]
, define

(3.9)

\displaystyle\alpha\bullet\beta:=(-1)^{(\dim V_{1}-|\eta_{1}|)|\eta_{2}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2})]

where
\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2}
is the abbreviation for the composition

V_{1}\times V_{2}\xrightarrow{\psi_{1}\times\psi_{2}}\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1})\times\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})\xrightarrow{*}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}(a_{1}+a_{2}).

See Appendix
A.2.2
for properties of
\bullet
.

3.4.3.
Open-closed string

Finally, define the (open-closed) concatenation map, for
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
i=1,\dots,k_{1}
,

\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}\colon\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})\to\Omega^{k_{1}+k_{2}}_{\star}(a_{1}+a_{2})

by exactly the same formula as before:

\displaystyle\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}\big((c_{0},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),(c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{k_{2}}^{\prime})\big):=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-2},c_{i-1}*c_{0}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},\dots,c^{\prime}_{k_{2}-1},c^{\prime}_{k_{2}}*c_{i},c_{i+1},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),&k_{2}\geq 1\\
(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-2},c_{i-1}*c_{0}^{\prime}*c_{i},c_{i+1},\dots,c_{k_{1}}),&k_{2}=0\end{cases}.

The same fibre product procedure therefore yields this operation on the chain level.
Explicitly, if
\alpha=[(V,\psi,\eta)]\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
and
x=[(U,\varphi,\omega)]\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
, define

(3.10)

\displaystyle\alpha\circ_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}x:=(-1)^{(\dim V-|\eta|)|\omega|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\psi\circ_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}\varphi,\eta\times\omega)]

where
\psi\circ_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}\varphi
is short for

V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U\xrightarrow{\psi\times\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1})\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})\xrightarrow{\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}_{k_{1},i,k_{2}}}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2}),

and we follow the previous abbreviation of

V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U:=V\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\psi}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U,

as well as the abuses of notation of making the restriction of
\eta\times\omega
to the domain
V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U
implicit.

This induces the chain-level open-closed string topology operation

\circ_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}\colon C_{i_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))\otimes C_{i_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))\to C_{i_{1}+i_{2}-\dim L}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2}))

for
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
a_{1},a_{2}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and
i=1,\dots,k_{1}
.

See Appendix
A.2.3
for properties of
\circ_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

3.4.4.
The anomaly map

Define, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}),k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\mathfrak{o}\colon C_{*+\dim L}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\to C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a))

as follows (here the notation
\mathfrak{o}
is meant for “obstruction”, similar to that in section 3.6.2 of
[
Fuk+09
]
).
Given a de Rham chain
x:=[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a),\omega)]\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
,
define

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x):=(-1)^{(\deg x)+1}[\big((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a),\omega\big)]\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a)).

Here, since we have assumed
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi\colon U\to L
is a submersion (see Definition
3.10
),
(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)
is transversely cut out.
Also,
\varphi
maps points in
(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\subset U
to loops in
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)
where the starting point is
\star
. Thus the image under
\varphi
of
(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)
does land in
\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a)
as claimed.

See Appendix
A.2.4
for properties of
\mathfrak{o}
.

3.5.
Resulting structures on the state spaces

We now describe the algebraic structures on the state spaces
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
by putting together the operations defined on components of the cosimplicial chain complexes in the previous section.

3.5.1.
The closed- and open-string dg algebras

Definition 3.16
.

We define the following operations on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
respectively:

(Loop bracket)

For
x,y\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, define the pre-Lie product

\circ\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\otimes C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}

given by

(3.12)

(x\circ y)(a,k):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(k^{\prime}-1)(|y|+1+k^{\prime\prime})}x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}).

and the
loop bracket

[\,,\,]\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\otimes C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}

given by

(3.13)

[x,y]:=x\circ y-(-1)^{|x||y|}y\circ x.

(Pontryagin product)

For
\alpha,\beta\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, define the
Pontryagin product

\bullet\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\otimes C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}

given by

(3.14)

(\alpha\bullet\beta)(a,k):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k^{\prime}|\beta|}\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\bullet\beta(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})

Remark 3.17
.

The signs in the formulas here do not involve the Maslov classes, since in our case
L
is orientable and
\mu_{L}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
, which do not contribute to the signs.

Definition 3.18
.

We define the element

[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\textup{dR}}_{k}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(0))

as follows:

•

For
k=0
, consider the map
\textup{pt}\to\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0)
where the single point is mapped to the constant based loop
[\underline{\star}]\in\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0)\subset\Pi_{1}L
. The de Rham chain
[(\textup{pt}\to\Omega^{1}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}^{0}_{c}(\textup{pt}))]
defines a closed cycle in
C^{\textup{dR}}_{0}(\Omega^{1}(0))
, which we set to be the
k=0
component of
[\underline{\star}]
;

•

For all
k>0
, set the
k
-the component of
[\underline{\star}]
to be
0
.

Lemma 3.19

(Structures on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega}
)
.

(1)

(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial,[,])
is a dg Lie algebra;

(2)

(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial,\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra with strict unit
[\underline{\star}]
;

(3)

All the structures are compatible with the decomposition into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
: more specifically, for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
denoting either
\mathscr{L}
or
\Omega_{\star}
, and for any
a,b\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, we have
\partial\colon C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}}(a)\to C_{*-1}^{\mathcal{X}}(a)
, and all the binary operations have
C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}}(a)\otimes C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(b)\to C_{*}^{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(a+b)
.

See Appendix
A.3
for conventions regarding signs of dg algebras, and Appendix
A.4
the proof of this Lemma. Specifically, see Lemma
A.11
for (1), and Lemma
A.17
for (2); the statement in (3) regarding compatibility with the decomposition into
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is clear from construction.

Remark 3.20
.

We remark that there should be rich algebraic structures on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
(e.g. a version of Deligne’s conjecture in
[
Iri18
]
), but the only part we will use is the Lie bracket.

Proposition 3.21
.

The isomorphism

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a)\cong\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}H_{*+\mu(a)}(\Omega_{\star}(a))

in Theorem
3.15
is an isomorphism of graded algebras.

Proof.

This is in
Section 2.5 (Proposition 2.5.1) in
[
Wan23
]
.
Similar to the remark after Theorem
3.15
, in cases where the
(\partial^{1}x)
term in (
3.7
) has the opposite sign as that in
[
Wan23
]
, the chain map

\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a,k)_{*}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{k}}\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a,k)_{*}

is an isomorphism of dg associative algebras so the statement still holds.
∎

These structures are parts (1) and (2) of Theorem
3.1
.

3.5.2.
A model for Hochschild cochains

We now start to describe the last component of Theorem
3.1
, i.e. the construction of the dg Lie algebra homomorphism

\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

In this section we first construct a model of
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.

We have shown that
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial,\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra with unit the constant loop class
[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}
. We now construct a chain model of Hochschild cohomology that is similar to the construction of
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
(in particular, the main difference with the usual construction of Hochschild cochains is that we need to make explicit the decomposition into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
).

Remark 3.22
.

Similar to the situation of
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
(see Remark
3.20
), we remark that there are rich algebraic structures on Hochschild cochains of a dg associative algebra (e.g. Gerstenhaber structure on the cohomology in
[
Ger63
]
, and many later work on Deligne’s conjecture of a
E_{2}
-algebra structure), but the only part we will use is the dg Lie algebra on Hochschild cochains.

For degree and sign conventions, we roughly follow section 2.2 of
[
Iri18
]
.

Definition 3.23
.

For any
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let

\displaystyle\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(

\displaystyle C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a,\ell)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\end{subarray}}\textup{Hom}_{*+\ell-1}\left(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{\ell}),C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\left(a_{1}+\dots+a_{\ell}+a\right)\right).

Then define

\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}):=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\underbrace{\prod_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a,\ell)}_{=:\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a)}.

Remark 3.24
.

Here, the degree is shifted by 1 from the usual Hochschild cochain complex degrees (in e.g. Definition 2.27 of
[
Gan12
]
), to keep consistency with that of
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
in
[
Iri18
,
Iri20
]
. In particular, similar to the situation in
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, the advantage for this degree shift is that the Gerstenhaber bracket, which is the structure we shall use, has degree 0 instead of
+1
.

Notation 3.25
.

Later on, for an element
\Phi\in\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, we will refer to its component in
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a)
as
\Phi(a)
, and the component of
\Phi(a)
in
\textup{Hom}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
as the
\ell
-ary part
of the element
\Phi
, and write it as
\Phi_{\ell}(a)\in\textup{Hom}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
. We will also denote by
\Phi_{\ell}\in\textup{Hom}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
by the image of
\Phi
under
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})\to\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a,\ell)\to\textup{Hom}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.

Notation 3.26
.

To clarify the notation on grading (similar to Notation
3.13
), for a homomorphism
\Phi\in\textup{Hom}_{k}(V^{\otimes\ell},W)
where
V
and
W
are graded vector spaces and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we use the notation
\deg\Phi=k
. More precisely, given
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}\in V
, we have

\deg\Phi(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}|\alpha_{i}|+\deg\Phi.

In contrast, for an (homogeneous) element
\Phi\in\textbf{{CH}}^{k}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, we write
|\Phi|=k
. Thus, in particular, for
\Phi\in\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, the
(a,\ell)
-component
\Phi_{\ell}(a)
has

(3.15)

\displaystyle\deg\Phi_{\ell}(a)=|\Phi|+\ell-1.

Definition 3.27
.

We define the following operations on the Hochschild cochain complex:

(Hochschild differential)

\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
has a differential of degree
-1

\delta\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*-1}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}),

where given an element
\Phi:=(\Phi_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\in\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, its differential
\big((\delta\Phi)_{\ell}\big)_{\ell\geq 0}
is given by the two components

(\delta\Phi)_{\ell}:=\delta^{0}\Phi_{\ell}+\delta^{1}\Phi_{\ell-1},

where

•

The component
\delta^{0}\Phi_{\ell}
is induced by the differential on
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, i.e. given
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,

\displaystyle(\delta^{0}\Phi_{\ell})

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\partial(\Phi_{\ell}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}))-(-1)^{\deg\Phi_{\ell}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|\alpha_{j}|}\Phi_{\ell}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\partial\alpha_{i},\cdots,\alpha_{\ell}).

•

The component
\delta^{1}\Phi_{\ell-1}
is given as follows. Define, for each
\ell\geq 1
and
i=0,\dots,\ell
, a chain
\delta^{1}_{\ell,i}\colon\textup{Hom}_{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes(\ell-1)},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})\to\textup{Hom}_{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, by

(\delta^{1}_{\ell,i}\Phi_{\ell-1})(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}):=\begin{cases}(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|(\deg\Phi_{\ell-1})}\alpha_{1}\bullet\Phi_{\ell-1}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}),&i=0\\
\Phi_{\ell-1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i}\bullet\alpha_{i+1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}),&1\leq i\leq\ell-1\\
\Phi_{\ell-1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell-1})\bullet\alpha_{\ell},&i=\ell\end{cases}.

Set

\delta^{1}(\Phi_{\ell-1}):=(-1)^{\deg\Phi_{\ell-1}+\ell-1}\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}(-1)^{i}\delta^{1}_{\ell,i}\Phi_{\ell-1}.

Remark 3.28
.

The two components of the differential,
\delta=\delta^{0}+\delta^{1}
are analogous to the two components
\partial=\partial^{\textup{dR}}+\delta
in
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

(Gerstenhaber bracket)

\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
has a Lie bracket of degree 0

[-,-]\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})\otimes\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})

defined by

[\Phi,\Psi]:=\Phi\circ\Psi-(-1)^{|\Phi|\cdot|\Psi|}\Psi\circ\Phi,

where given two elements
\Phi:=(\Phi_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0},\Psi:=(\Psi_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}\in\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, we define the pre-Lie product
\circ
as follows: given
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,

\displaystyle(\Phi\circ\Psi)_{\ell}

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell+1\\
1\leq i\leq\ell^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\dagger}\Phi_{\ell^{\prime}}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i-1},\Psi_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}(\alpha_{i},\dots,\alpha_{i+\ell^{\prime\prime}-1}),\alpha_{i+\ell^{\prime\prime}},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}),

where the sign is given by

\dagger:=(i-1)(\ell^{\prime\prime}-1)+(|\Psi|+\ell^{\prime\prime}+1)(|\alpha_{1}|+\dots+|\alpha_{i-1}|+\ell^{\prime}-1).

Lemma 3.29

(Structures on
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}
)
.

(1)

(\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}),\delta,[-,-])
is a dg Lie algebra;

(2)

All the structures are compatible with the decomposition into
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
: more precisely, for any
a,b\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\delta\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a)\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*-1}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a),

and

[-,-]\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a)\otimes\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(b)\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a+b).

The proof of (1) is classical (see e.g.
[
Iri18
]
Example 2.7 and Theorem 2.8); for conventions on degrees and signs of dg Lie algebras, see definition
A.7
in the appendix. Part (2) is straightforward.

3.5.3.
The closed-open string map

We now define the closed-open map

\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

We shall define, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{\ell,a}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a)\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a,\ell),

and accordingly, for each (fixed)
\ell
, the composition

\mathcal{CO}_{\ell}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\xrightarrow{\bigoplus_{a}\mathcal{CO}_{\ell,a}}\bigoplus_{a}\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a,\ell)\to\textup{Hom}\big(\left(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\right)^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\big),

so that for
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, under Notation
3.25
,

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{CO}(x)\big)_{\ell}(a):=\mathcal{CO}_{\ell,a}(x),\quad\big(\mathcal{CO}(x)\big)_{\ell}:=\mathcal{CO}_{\ell}(x).

Definition 3.30
.

We define the closed-open map map
\mathcal{CO}
in arity/components as follows:

(0-ary part)

For each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}),k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
define the
anomaly map

\mathfrak{o}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}
by

(3.16)

\displaystyle\big(\mathfrak{o}(x)\big)(a,k):=\mathfrak{o}\big(x(a,k)\big).

Then define

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0,a}(x):={(-1)^{|x|}}\bigg(\big(\mathfrak{o}(x)\big)(a,k)\bigg)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\in C_{|x|-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}.

(Unary part)

Given
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
and
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, define

(3.18)

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\alpha)=:{(-1)^{|\alpha||x|+1}}\alpha\circ x\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},

where for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define

(3.19)

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ x)(a,k):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}{(-1)^{(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+k^{\prime}(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime})}}\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}).

Then for each
a,a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, the map
\mathcal{CO}_{1}
restricts to
