\mathcal{CO}_{1,a}(x)\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a^{\prime})\to C_{*+|x|}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a+a^{\prime})

because in formula (
3.19
), for
\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a^{\prime})
and
x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a^{\prime\prime})
, the resulting
\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime})
.

(Higher arity parts)
All higher arity parts are set to 0. That is, for each
\ell\geq 2
, we set
\mathcal{CO}_{\ell}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textup{Hom}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\otimes\ell},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
to be 0.

Then these operations
\{\mathcal{CO}_{k,a}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\end{subarray}}
are packaged into

\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

This is a degree-0 map.

We claim that this is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebra:

Lemma 3.31

(Lemma
A.21
)
.

\mathcal{CO}
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebra. Moreover,
\mathcal{CO}
respects the decompositions of
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
; that is, if
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}(a)
for some
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, then
\mathcal{CO}(x)\in\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(a)
.

We verify this in Lemma
A.21
in the appendix.

This finishes our construction of the open-closed string topology package, i.e. Theorem
3.1
.

4.
Holomorphic curves as deformation

We now use moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs with boundary on the Lagrangian
L\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
to produce a curved
A_{\infty}
-deformation of the open string dg algebra
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
of
L
.
As mentioned in the introduction, the basic idea comes from Fukaya (
[
Fuk06
]
) in the context of free loop spaces (which is realized by Irie in
[
Iri18
,
Iri20
]
; for our purpose we work in the framework of
[
Wan23
]
) and from the proposal of Abouzaid (
[
Abo16
]
) in the context of based loop spaces.

The main result of this section is Theorem
4.12
, which is a rigorous version of the idealized Theorem
1.3
in the introduction, constructing a gapped (curved) associative algebra deforming the Pontryagin algebra structure on the open string state space
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.
This follows from the construction of the closed-open map in section
3
, as well as the construction of a Maurer-Cartan element in the closed-string state space
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
coming from moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs (Theorem
4.11
). In section
4.1
we discuss the energy filtration and completion of the state space, as well as gappedness of Maurer-Cartan elements and
A_{\infty}
-structures, to deal with various convergence issues.

4.1.
Preliminaries on energy filtration and gappedness

To deal with convergence of operations coming from pseudo-holomorphic discs, we need to consider the energy filtration on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, introduced below. The issue of convergence is dealt with often by introducing the Novikov field or ring (e.g. in ordinary Lagrangian Floer theory, see
[
Fuk+09
,
Fuk+09a
]
). In our setting, it is more convenient to avoid introducing the Novikov field and simply work with the completion of the energy filtration on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, as is done in
[
Iri20
]
.

4.1.1.
Energy filtration and completion

Suppose
L\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a closed Lagrangian. Recall that we have the energy homomorphism
E\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}
, given by e.g. integrating the Liouville 1-form (the primitive to the standard symplectic form).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
C
denote one of the three chain complexes:
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
or
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
. In each case we have, by construction, a decomposition

\textbf{C}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\textbf{C}(a).

(1)

The
energy filtration
on
C
is given by
\{\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{C}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
where for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{C}:=\bigoplus_{E(a)>\lambda}\textbf{C}(a).

(2)

The
completion
of
C
with respect to the energy filtration is denoted

\widehat{\textbf{C}}:=\varprojlim_{\lambda\to+\infty}\textbf{C}\big/\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{C}.

An element
x\in\widehat{\textbf{C}}
can be identified as an (possibly) infinite sum

x=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x(a_{i}),\quad\textup{ where }a_{i}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}),\,\,x(a_{i})\in\textbf{C}(a_{i}),\textup{ and }E(a_{i})\to+\infty.

Since the differential in each of the three cases (
\textbf{C}=C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},
or
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
) preserves the splitting of
C
into
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
classes (Lemma
3.19
(3) and Lemma
3.29
(2)), it preserves the energy filtration by definition, and thus we obtain, on the homology
H
of
C
, a filtration
\{\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{H}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}
. More precisely,
H
splits into

\displaystyle\textbf{H}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\textbf{H}(a),

and the energy filtration on
H
is given by, for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{H}=\bigoplus_{E(a)>E}\textbf{H}(a).

We also define its completion to be

\displaystyle\widehat{\textbf{H}}:=\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}\textbf{H}/\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}\textbf{H}.

By Lemma
3.19
(3), all the string topology operations defined in the previous section respect the splitting into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, and therefore extends to the completions
\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}
and
\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}
.
Similarly by Lemma
3.29
(2), the Gerstenhaber bracket extends to the completion
\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.
In particular,
\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}
and
\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
are dg Lie algebras and
\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}
is a dg associative algebra.
Moreover by Lemma
3.31
, the closed-open map
\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
preserves the splitting into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, so
\mathcal{CO}
extends to
\mathcal{CO}\colon\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}\to\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.

4.1.2.
Gappedness

One essential point for many of our arguments is that the energy levels of pseudo-holomorphic discs as well as the perturbed pseudo-holomorphic discs are distributed according to Gromov compactness theorem. Following
[
Fuk+09
]
(see Condition 3.1.6 and Definition 3.2.26; also see
[
Iri20
,
Yua25
]
), we say

Definition 4.2
.

A subset
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is
a monoid of curve classes
if

(1)

\mathfrak{G}
is a submonoid of
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, i.e.
0\in\mathfrak{G}
, and if
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{G}
then
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{G}
;

(2)

The image of
\mathfrak{G}
under the energy homomorphism
E\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}
is discrete;

(3)

For any
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
, its energy
E(\beta)\geq 0
, and the only
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
with
E(\beta)=0
is
\beta=0
;

(4)

For each energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, there are only finitely many
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
with
E(\beta)=\lambda
.

Let
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
be a compactly supported time-dependent Hamiltonian function.
The
Hofer norm
of the Hamiltonian
H
is

(4.1)

\left\lVert{H}\right\rVert:=\int_{0}^{1}\left(\max H_{t}-\min H_{t}\right)\,dt.

Definition 4.3
.

We say a subset
\mathfrak{N}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
is a
module of
H
-perturbed curve classes over
\mathfrak{G}
if

(1)

\mathfrak{N}
is a module over
\mathfrak{G}
, i.e.
0\in\mathfrak{N}
, and if
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
, then
\eta+\beta\in\mathfrak{N}
(in particular
\mathfrak{G}\subset\mathfrak{N}
);

(2)

The image of
\mathfrak{N}
under the energy homomorphism
E\colon H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}
is discrete;

(3)

For any
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
, its energy
E(\eta)\geq-\left\lVert{H}\right\rVert
;

(4)

For each energy level
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, there are only finitely many
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
with
E(\eta)=\lambda
.

As before, let
C
denote one of the three:
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, or
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.

Definition 4.4
.

An element
x\in\widehat{\textbf{C}}
is
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped
if we can decompose
x
as

x=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{G}}x(\beta),\quad x(\beta)\in\textbf{C}(\beta).

This expression for
x
makes sense as an element in
\widehat{\textbf{C}}
since we can sort elements in
\mathfrak{G}
so that
\mathfrak{G}=\{\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots\}
where
0\leq E(\beta_{0})\leq E(\beta_{1})\leq E(\beta_{2})\leq\cdots\to+\infty
by Definition
4.2
; so
x=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}x(\beta_{i})
is an element in
\widehat{\textbf{C}}
.

Define
\widehat{\textbf{C}}_{\mathfrak{G}}
to be the subspace of
\widehat{\textbf{C}}
consisting of
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped elements.

Similarly, an element
y\in\widehat{\textbf{C}}
is
\mathfrak{N}
-gapped
if we can decompose
y
as

y=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}y(\eta),\quad y(\eta)\in\textbf{C}(\eta).

Define
\widehat{\textbf{C}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
to be the subspace of
\widehat{\textbf{C}}
consisting of
\mathfrak{N}
-gapped elements.

An element
\Phi
in
\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
is a collection of operations

\Phi_{\ell,\beta}:=\Phi_{\ell}(\beta)\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{\ell})\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{1}+\dots+a_{\ell}+\beta)

for each
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
, and
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
. We sometimes refer to this as a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator system

(this is similar to
[
Yua25
]
, Definition 2.1; later on the
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator systems we use will eventually need to satisfy the requirement in
ibid.
that
\Phi_{0}(0)=0
which we don’t require for now).

Recall that the closed-open string map

\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})

is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras which preserves the decomposition into
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
classes (Lemma
3.31
).
Thus it induces maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}\colon\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}}\to\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}),\quad\mathcal{CO}\colon\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}\to\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{N}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

In particular, given a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped element
x\in\widehat{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}}
, applying the closed-open map gives a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator system
\mathcal{CO}(x)\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

Notation 4.5
.

For a monoid of curve classes
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,
we write

\mathfrak{G}^{+}:=\mathfrak{G}\setminus\{0\}.

We denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
the collection of
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped elements with no zero-energy term, i.e.
x\in\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}\subset\widehat{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathfrak{G}}
if

x=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}}x(\beta),\quad x(\beta)\in\mathbf{C}(\beta).

Definition 4.6
.

An element
x\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
is an
Maurer-Cartan element
if it satisfies the
Maurer-Cartan equation

\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0.

The following definition is a working definition tailored for our situation:

Definition 4.7
.

A
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative deformation
of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
is a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator system
\textup{{m}}\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
with

\textup{{m}}_{\ell,\beta}\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{\ell})\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a_{1}+\dots+a_{\ell}+\beta)

for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
, and
a_{1},\dots,a_{\ell}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, such that

(1)

\textup{{m}}\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
. This is equivalent to: for each
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
,

\deg\textup{{m}}_{\ell,\beta}=\ell-2;

(2)

If
\beta=0
, we require

\textup{{m}}_{0,0}=0,\quad\textup{{m}}_{1,0}=\partial,\quad\textup{{m}}_{2,0}=\bullet;

(3)

If either of the following holds:

(a)

\beta=0
and
\ell>2
;

(b)

\beta\neq 0
(so that
E(\beta)>0
) and
\ell\geq 2
,

we require
\textup{{m}}_{\ell,\beta}=0
.

(4)

The following identities hold:

(a)

\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})=0
.
That is, for any
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{G}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}(\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta_{2}})=0;

(b)

\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{1}\alpha)=\textup{{m}}_{0}\bullet\alpha-\alpha\bullet\textup{{m}}_{0}
. That is, for any
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
and
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in\mathfrak{G}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{1}}(\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{2}}\alpha)=\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}\bullet\alpha-\alpha\bullet\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta};

(c)

\textup{{m}}_{1}
is a derivation with respect to
\bullet
. That is, for any
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,

\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}(\alpha_{1})\bullet\alpha_{2}+(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|}\alpha_{1}\bullet\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha_{2}).

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose that
x\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation.
Then the
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator system given by

\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta}:=\begin{cases}0,&\beta=0\\
-\mathcal{CO}_{0,\beta}(x),&\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}\end{cases},\quad\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta}:=\begin{cases}\partial,&\beta=0\\
-\mathcal{CO}_{1,\beta},&\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}\end{cases},\quad\textup{{m}}_{2,\beta}:=\begin{cases}\bullet,&\beta=0\\
0,&\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}\end{cases}

for any
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
,
and
\textup{{m}}_{\ell,\beta}=0
for
\ell\geq 3
, is a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative deformation of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

Proof.

m
is a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped operator system by construction, and properties (1) to (3) in Definition
4.7
is clear.

For (4): for brevity write
\Phi:=\mathcal{CO}(x)
. Since
\mathcal{CO}
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras and by assumption
x
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation, we have

\delta\Phi-\frac{1}{2}[\Phi,\Phi]=0\quad\textup{in }\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}).

By inspecting different arities of this equation, we get, for any
\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{1}(\Phi_{0})=0\\
\Phi_{1}(\Phi_{1}(\alpha))=\partial(\Phi_{1}(\alpha))+\Phi_{1}(\partial\alpha)+(\alpha\bullet\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{0}\bullet\alpha)\\
\Phi_{1}(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=\Phi_{1}(\alpha_{1})\bullet\alpha_{2}+(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|}\alpha_{1}\bullet\Phi_{1}(\alpha_{2})\end{cases}.

Then the three identities in (4) follow:

•

For (a):

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})=-(\partial-\Phi_{1})(\Phi_{0})=0.

•

For (b):

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}\alpha

\displaystyle=(\partial-\Phi_{1})^{2}\alpha=-\partial(\Phi_{1}(\alpha))-\Phi_{1}(\partial\alpha)+\Phi_{1}\circ\Phi_{1}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha\bullet\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{0}\bullet\alpha=\textup{{m}}_{0}\bullet\alpha-\alpha\bullet\textup{{m}}_{0}.

•

For (c):

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=\partial(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})-\Phi(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha_{1})\bullet\alpha_{2}+(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}|}\alpha_{1}\bullet\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha_{2}).

∎

4.2.
Deformation in closed string

In this section we discuss the deformation of the closed string state space coming from moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs, in the form of a Maurer-Cartan element in
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
.

Let
L
be a closed, oriented, spin manifold of dimension
n
.
Assume we are given a Lagrangian embedding of
L
into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
(equipped with the standard symplectic structure
\omega=\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
).

Let
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
be a compactly supported time-dependent Hamiltonian function.
For each
t\in[0,1]
, let
X_{H_{t}}
be the Hamiltonian vector field on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
associated to
H_{t}:=H(-,t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, i.e. satisfying

dH_{t}(-)=-\omega_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}(X_{H_{t}},-).

Let
(\varphi_{H}^{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the time-
t
flow of the 1-parameter family of vector fields
X_{H_{t}}
.
We further assume that

Assumption 4.9
.

The Hamiltonian
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
satisfies

•

H
is a
displacing Hamiltonian function
, i.e.
\varphi^{1}_{H}(L)\cap L=\emptyset
;

•

H_{t}\equiv 0
when
t\in[0,1/3]\cup[2/3,1]
.

Such a displacing Hamiltonian function exists for any compact
L\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Fix such a choice.

Definition 4.10
.

We define the element

\textsf{L}^{0}\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+k}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0))

as follows:

•

For
k=0
, consider the map
L\to\mathscr{L}^{1}(0)
defined by
y\mapsto[\underline{y}]\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(0)\subset\Pi_{1}L
. The de Rham chain
(-1)^{\dim L+1}[(L\to\mathscr{L}^{1}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{0}(L))]
defines a closed cycle in
C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L}(\mathscr{L}^{1}(0))
, which we set to be the
k=0
component of
\textsf{L}^{0}
;

•

For all
k>0
, set the
k
-th component of
\textsf{L}^{0}
to be 0.

Theorem 4.11
.

Under the setup above, there exists the following data:

•

A monoid of curve classes
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
(Definition
4.2
) and a module
\mathfrak{N}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
of
H
-perturbed curve classes over
\mathfrak{G}
(Definition
4.3
);

•

For each
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}
a chain
\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta)
, and for each
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
a chain
\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
and a chain
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
;

such that

(1)

The element

\textup{{M}}:=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}}\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}},

where
\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta)
,
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

\partial\textup{{M}}+\frac{1}{2}\big[\textup{{M}},\textup{{M}}\big]=0.

(2)

The elements

\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{N}},\quad\textup{{N}}^{0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{N}},

where
\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
,
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
,
satisfy

\partial\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}-\big[\textup{{M}},\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}\big]=\textup{{N}}^{0}.

(3)

\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\neq 0
only if
\eta\in\mathfrak{G}
. Moreover, in case
\eta=0
,
\textup{{N}}^{0}(0)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)
is a cycle which is homologous to
\textsf{L}^{0}
.

This theorem is analogous to Theorem 5.1 (and section 5 in general) of
[
Iri20
]
. The elements
\textup{{M}},\textup{{N}},\textup{{N}}^{0}
are constructed using virtual fundamental chains of moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic and perturbed pseudo-holomorphic discs (see section
2
for the geometric motivations of these elements).
The proof of this theorem requires the theory of Kuranishi structures, similar as those in
[
Iri20
]
(but simpler since we are using a simplified chain model by
[
Wan23
]
), and is contained in Appendix
B
.

4.3.
Deformation in open string

In this section we construct a
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative algebra deforming the Pontryagin algebra structure on
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

4.3.1.
Statement

Recall our setup in section
4.2
:
L
is a closed, oriented, spin manifold of dimension
n
, together with a Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
;
H\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
is a Hamiltonian satisfying Assumption
4.9
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Under the setup above, there exists the following data:

•

A monoid of curve classes
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
(Definition
4.2
) and a module
\mathfrak{N}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
of
H
-perturbed curve classes over
\mathfrak{G}
(Definition
4.3
);

•

A
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative deformation of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, which we denote as
\textup{{m}}\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
;

•

For each
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
, a chain
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
and a chain
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
,

such that

(1)

\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(\eta)\neq 0
only if
\eta\in\mathfrak{G}
. Moreover, in case
\eta=0
,
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(0)\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)
is a cycle which is homologous to
(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
(here
[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)
is the unit of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
; see Definition
3.18
).

(2)

When the Maslov class
\mu_{L}
vanishes, (under a deformation of the structures and data keeping all the above properties)

\textup{{m}}_{0}=0

and the elements

\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}},\quad\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}

satisfy

\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})=\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}.

Remark 4.13
.

Pushing forward the Maurer-Cartan element
\textup{{M}}\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
and using Lemma
4.8
to cook up the
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative deformation
m
of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, and defining
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}:=\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0})
,
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}:=\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textup{{N}}^{0})
, would satisfy everything in the Theorem but property (2).

Ideally, the term
\textup{{m}}_{0}
should be 0 in case
L
has vanishing Maslov class, morally because the moduli spaces of Maslov-zero curves with one marked point have virtual dimension
\dim L-2
and the curves do not generically intersect the basepoint
\star\in L
. However, a technical issue that appears is that the moduli spaces of curves with a large number of boundary marked points still have positive virtual dimensions after intersecting with
\star\in L
, and therefore has non-zero contribution to the curvature
\textup{{m}}_{0}
at the chain level.
To deal with this, we use the machinery of bounding chains following
[
Fuk+09
]
.
For completeness, we sketch the obstruction theory of bounding chains developed in e.g. section 3.6 of
[
Fuk+09
]
in our very specific setting of curved dg algebras (instead of curved
A_{\infty}
-algebras in general) in section
4.3.2
, before going into the proof of Theorem
4.12
.

4.3.2.
Obstruction theory for bounding chains

In this section, we temporarily denote by
\textup{{m}}\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}}_{\mathfrak{G}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
the
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped curved dg associative deformation of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
given by
\textup{{M}}\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
and Lemma
4.8
.

Definition 4.14

(
[
Fuk+09
]
Definition 3.6.4, 3.6.16)
.

A
bounding chain over
\mathfrak{G}
is an element
b\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
such that the Maurer-Cartan equation

(4.2)

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}b+b\bullet b=0

holds. We say the curved algebra
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
is
unobstructed over
\mathfrak{G}
if it admits a bounding chain
b
over
\mathfrak{G}
.

We now suppose
L
has vanishing Maslov class
\mu_{L}
.

Lemma 4.15
.

If the Maslov class
\mu_{L}=0\in H^{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, then the deformed algebra
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\textup{{m}})
is unobstructed over
\mathfrak{G}
.

Proof.

This follows from the obstruction theory of bounding (co)chains developed in e.g. Theorem 3.6.18 of
[
Fuk+09
]
. We repeat the argument here because of different grading conventions.

The bounding chain
b
is to be constructed by induction on energy levels.
We sort the elements in the monoid
\mathfrak{G}
by energy:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}=\{0=\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\dots\}\textup{ such that }0=E(\beta_{0})\leq E(\beta_{1})\leq E(\beta_{2})\leq\cdots

Also, sort the set
E(\mathfrak{G})\subset\mathbb{R}
in order:

E(\mathfrak{G})=\{0=E^{(0)}<E^{(1)}<E^{(2)}<\cdots\}.

These are possible by the gappedness assumption (Definition
4.2
).
Denote by
K^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
the largest integer such that
E(\beta_{K^{(i)}})=E^{(i)}
.
In the following proof, for a given energy level
\lambda
, an expression holds “mod
\lambda
” is taken to mean that it holds in

C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\cong\bigoplus_{E(a)\leq\lambda}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a).

For the base case, at energy level
E^{(1)}
, we have
E(\beta_{1})=\dots=E(\beta_{K^{(1)}})=E^{(1)}
.
Define

\displaystyle o_{i}:=\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta_{i}}\in C_{-2}^{\Omega_{\star}},\quad i=1,\dots,K^{(1)}.

We claim that
\partial o_{i}=0
for
i=1,\dots,K^{(1)}
.
Indeed, by Definition
4.7
(4a),
\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})=0
, and since
\textup{{m}}_{0,0}=0
, we have that for

\displaystyle 0=\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{K^{(1)}}\partial\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta_{i}}\mod E^{(1)}.

Then
\partial o_{i}=\partial\textup{{m}}_{0,\beta_{i}}=0
because they are in different summands
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})\subset C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.
Moreover, by Theorem
3.15
and the Maslov-zero assumption (so that the grading works out),
H_{-2}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})\cong H_{-2}(\Omega_{\star}(\beta_{i}))=0
. Therefore

\partial b_{i}+o_{i}=0

for some
b_{i}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
. Define

\displaystyle b^{(1)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{K}b_{i}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}\left(b^{(1)}\right)+b^{(1)}\bullet b^{(1)}\equiv 0\mod E^{(1)}.

Now suppose that for some
j>1
, we have found
b^{(j-1)}=\sum_{i=1}^{K^{(j-1)}}b_{i}
where
b_{i}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
, such that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}\left(b^{(j-1)}\right)+b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\equiv 0\mod E^{(j-1)}.

For
i=K^{(j-1)}+1,K^{(j-1)}+2,\dots,K^{(j)}
, define the component of the left-hand side in
C_{-2}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
as
o_{i}\in C_{-2}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
.
We now claim that
\partial o_{i}=0
for all such
i
. This follows from applying
\textup{{m}}_{1}
to the left-hand side of (
4.3
) in two different ways:

•

On the one hand, since
\textup{{m}}_{1}=\partial+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\textup{{m}}_{1,\beta_{i}}
,

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}\left(\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}(b^{(j-1)})+b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\right)\equiv\partial\sum_{i=K^{(j-1)}+1}^{K^{(j)}}o_{i}\mod E^{(j)};

•

On the other hand, by the
A_{\infty}
-identities,

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}\left(\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}(b^{(j-1)})+b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{m}}_{0})+\textup{{m}}_{1}^{2}(b^{(j-1)})+\textup{{m}}_{1}(b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}(b^{(j-1)})\right)\bullet b^{(j-1)}-b^{(j-1)}\bullet\left(\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}(b^{(j-1)})\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}+b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\bullet b^{(j-1)}\mod E^{(j)}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\,\,0\mod E^{(j)}.

This shows that
\partial o_{i}=0
in each
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
. Again by Theorem
3.15
and the Maslov-zero assumption,
[o_{i}]\in H_{-2}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})=0
is null-homologous and therefore

\displaystyle\partial b_{i}+o_{i}=0

for some
b_{i}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\beta_{i})
for each
i=K^{(j-1)}+1,\dots K^{(j)}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}\left(b^{(j)}\right)+b^{(j)}\bullet b^{(j)}\equiv 0\mod E^{(j)}.

Since this induction can be carried out for every
j
, we can take the limit

\displaystyle b:=\lim_{j\to\infty}b^{(j)}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}

to get a bounding chain over
\mathfrak{G}
.
∎

Definition 4.16
.

Suppose
b\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}}
is a bounding chain over
\mathfrak{G}
.
Then define

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b}\colon\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}\to\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}};\quad\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b}(\alpha):=\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha)+b\bullet\alpha-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\bullet b.

By (
4.2
), it can be verified that
(\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b})^{2}=0
.

For notational simplicity, we temporarily write

\displaystyle\Phi:=\mathcal{CO}(\textup{{M}}),\quad\Psi^{\geq 0}:=\mathcal{CO}(\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}),\quad\Psi^{0}:=\mathcal{CO}(\textup{{N}}^{0}).

Lemma 4.17
.

\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b}(\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b))=\Psi_{0}^{0}+\Psi_{1}^{0}(b),
where

(1)

\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)\in\widehat{C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
;

(2)

\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)\in\widehat{C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
and the component of
\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)
in
C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
is non-zero only if
\eta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}
.

Proof.

By construction of
\mathcal{CO}
, each of
\Phi,\Psi^{\geq 0}
and
\Psi^{0}
only have 0- and 1-ary components in
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.
Since
\mathcal{CO}
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras, we have

\delta\Psi^{\geq 0}-\left[\Phi,\Psi^{\geq 0}\right]=\Psi^{0}.

This equation translates to

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\textup{{m}}_{1}\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}-\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\textup{{m}}_{0})=\Psi_{0}^{0}\\
\textup{{m}}_{1}(\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\alpha))-\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\textup{{m}}_{1}(\alpha))+\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}\bullet\alpha-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\bullet\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}=\Psi_{1}^{0}(\alpha)\\
\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{1}\bullet\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\alpha_{2})+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\alpha_{1})\bullet\alpha_{2}\end{cases}

for all
\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

Then

\displaystyle\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b}(\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b))

\displaystyle=\textup{{m}}_{1}\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}+\textup{{m}}_{1}(\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b))+b\bullet\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}+b\bullet\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)+\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}\bullet b+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)\bullet b

\displaystyle=\Psi_{0}^{0}+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\textup{{m}}_{0})+\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(\textup{{m}}_{1}(b))+b\bullet\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)\bullet b

\displaystyle=\Psi_{0}^{0}+\Psi_{1}^{0}(b),

by the the properties in Definition
4.7
(4) as well as (
4.4
).

The claims that
\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b),\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)\in\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
follow because
\mathfrak{N}
is a module over
\mathfrak{G}
(Definition
4.3
),
\Psi^{\geq 0},\Psi^{0}\in\widehat{\textbf{{CH}}^{*}}_{\mathfrak{N}}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
, and
b\in\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
.
Finally the component of
\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)
in
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
is non-zero only if
\eta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}
, since
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\neq 0
unless
\eta\in\mathfrak{G}
(Theorem
4.11
(3)), and
b\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}
(so in particular the energy of
\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)=(\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\textup{{N}}^{0}))(b)
has to be strictly positive).
∎

4.3.3.
Proof of Theorem
4.12

Lemma 4.18
.

The element
\textsf{L}^{0}\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)
defined in Definition
4.10
pushes forward under
\mathcal{CO}
to an element
\mathcal{CO}(\textsf{L}^{0})\in\textbf{{CH}}^{1}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})(0)
whose 0-nary component
\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textsf{L}^{0})\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)
corresponds to

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textsf{L}^{0})=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]

where
[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)
is the unit of
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
(Definition
3.18
).

Proof.

By Definition
4.10
, the element
\textsf{L}^{0}
has components

\displaystyle\textsf{L}^{0}(0,k)=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\dim L+1}[(L\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathscr{L}^{1}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{0}(L))],&k=0\\
0,&k>0\end{cases}

where
\iota\colon y\mapsto[\underline{y}]\in\mathscr{L}^{1}(0)
.
By (
3.11
),

\mathfrak{o}\left[\big(L\to\mathscr{L}^{1}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{0}(L)\big)\right]=\left[\big((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\iota)^{-1}(\star)\to\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0);1\big)\right]

where
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\iota\colon L\to L
is the identity, and therefore

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(\textsf{L}^{0}(0,k))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\dim L+1}[(\textup{pt}\to\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}^{0}_{c}(\textup{pt}))],&k=0\\
0,&k>0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L+1}[\underline{\star}](0,k),

where
\textup{pt}\to\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0)
sends the point to the constant based loop
[\underline{\star}]\in\Omega^{1}_{\star}(0)
.

Finally by (
3.16
) and (
3.17
),
\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textsf{L}^{0})=(-1)^{|\textsf{L}^{0}|}\mathfrak{o}(\textsf{L}^{0})=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.12
.

It remains to deal with the case where the Maslov class
\mu_{L}
vanishes (see Remark
4.13
).
Under the setting of Lemma
4.15
we choose a bounding chain
b
, and set consider the curved dg associative deformation with unary term
\textup{{m}}_{1}^{b}
(Definition
4.16
), binary term
\bullet
, and all the other operations set to 0.
Set
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}:=\Psi_{0}^{\geq 0}+\Psi_{1}^{\geq 0}(b)
and
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}:=\Psi_{0}^{0}+\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)

in the notation of Lemma
4.17
.
Finally, to show
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(0)
is a cycle which is homologous to
(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
, we have
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}(0)=\Psi_{0}^{0}(0)=\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\textup{{N}}^{0})
since by Lemma
4.17
the 0-ary component of
\Psi_{1}^{0}(b)
vanishes, and
\textup{{N}}^{0}(0)\in C_{0}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)
is a cycle which is homologous to
\textsf{L}^{0}
; the conclusion then follows from Lemma
4.18
.
∎

5.
Lagrangians in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with vanishing Maslov classes

5.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

For convenience, we restate Theorem
1.1
here:

Theorem 5.1
.

(Theorem
1.1
)
If
L
is a closed spin manifold and
\pi_{2}(L)=0
, then
L
does not admit a Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with vanishing Maslov class.

The proof goes through the homology of the based loop space:

Lemma 5.2
.

For any path connected space
L
,

H_{1}(\Omega_{\star}L;\mathbb{Z})=\bigoplus_{a\in\pi_{1}L}\pi_{2}(L).

Proof.

Since
\Omega_{\star}L
is an
H
-space, its fundamental group is abelian and all of its components
\Omega_{\star}(a)
(labeled by
a\in\pi_{1}L\cong\pi_{0}\Omega_{\star}L
) are homotopy equivalent to each other. Therefore by Hurewicz theorem,

\displaystyle H_{1}(\Omega_{\star}L)\cong\bigoplus_{a\in\pi_{1}L\cong\pi_{0}\Omega_{\star}L}H_{1}(\Omega_{\star}(a))\cong\bigoplus_{a\in\pi_{1}L}\pi_{1}(\Omega_{\star}(a))\cong\bigoplus_{a\in\pi_{1}L}\pi_{2}L.

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

As in the proof of Lemma
4.15
, for a given energy level
\lambda
, we say an expression holds “mod
\lambda
” is taken to mean that it holds in

C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\lambda}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\cong\bigoplus_{E(a)\leq\lambda}C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(a).

By Theorem
4.12
, assuming
L
admits a Maslov-zero Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, there exist elements
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}\in\widehat{C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
and
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}\in\widehat{C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}}_{\mathfrak{N}}
with
\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})=\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}
, where

[\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{0}]=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]\quad\textup{ in }H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}

for a sufficiently small
\hbar>0
so that
\hbar<E(\beta)
for all
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
. Thus

(5.1)

[\textup{{m}}_{1}\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}]=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]\quad\textup{in }H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}.

We are going to perform a sequence of modifications on
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}
which increases its energy but still keeps the equation (
5.1
).

Similar to proof of Lemma
4.15
, we sort
\mathfrak{N}
into
\mathfrak{N}=\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\cdots\}
where
E(\eta_{1})<E(\eta_{2})<\cdots
, and sort
E(\mathfrak{N})\subset\mathbb{R}
into
E(\mathfrak{N})=\{E^{(1)}<E^{(2)}<\cdots\}
so that
E^{(K)}=0
for some
K\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
(such
K
exists by Definition
4.3
; also
K
is necessarily larger than 1 since otherwise
\textup{{m}}_{1}\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}
has energy
>0
whereas
\textsf{L}^{0}
doesn’t).
Decompose
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}
into

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})^{(j)},\quad\textup{ where }(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})^{(j)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\mathfrak{N}\\
E(\eta)=E^{(j)}\end{subarray}}\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta),\quad\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta).

Equation (
5.1
) becomes
\partial(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})^{(1)}=0
in
C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{E^{(1)}}C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,
and therefore each
\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta)
where
E(\eta)=E^{(1)}
represents a homology class in
H_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)\cong H_{1}(\Omega_{\star}L(\eta))
, which is 0 by Lemma
5.2
.
Thus there exist
\chi_{\eta}\in C_{2}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
such that
\partial\chi_{\eta}+\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}(\eta)=0
in
C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(\eta)
.
Then the element

(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{1}:=\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\mathfrak{N}\\
E(\eta)=E^{(1)}\end{subarray}}\textup{{m}}_{1}(\chi_{\eta})\in\mathscr{F}^{E^{(1)}}C_{1}^{\Omega_{\star}}

satisfies
[\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{1}]=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
in
H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
still and has energy strictly larger than
E^{(1)}
.
Continue this procedure until we obtain
(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{K-1}
, which satisfies
[\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{K-1}]=(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
in
H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
with
(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{K-1}
consisting only of elements of energy
\geq 0
. But then the energy of
\textup{{m}}_{1}(\textup{{N}}_{\star}^{\geq 0})_{K-1}
is strictly larger than
\hbar
whereas
(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]
has energy 0.
This is a contradiction because
(-1)^{\dim L}[\underline{\star}]\in H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}/\mathscr{F}^{\hbar}H_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
is non-zero in homology.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

Alternatively, using homological perturbation lemma (in the filtered context, as done in e.g. Chapter 5 in
[
Fuk+09
]
or Section 2 in
[
Iri20
]
), we can also show that there is an (uncurved)
\mathfrak{G}
-gapped, filtered
A_{\infty}
-structure on
\widehat{H_{*}^{\Omega}}
, where
\textup{{m}}_{1}
has degree
-1
, which is quasi-isomorphic to
\widehat{C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}}
and thus acyclic. This can also be used to conclude Theorem
1.1
. We do not spell out the detail here.
As an another alternative, one can also use a spectral sequence argument after choosing a bounding chain.

5.2.
Examples

Lemma 5.4
.

The condition
\pi_{2}L=0
is preserved under taking (finite) connected sums if
\dim L\geq 4
, or products in any dimension.

Proof.

The statement for products is straightforward. For connected sums, we use

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(L_{1}\#L_{2})\cong\pi_{2}(\widetilde{L_{1}\#L_{2}})\cong H_{2}(\widetilde{L_{1}\#L_{2}};\mathbb{Z})

and apply Mayer-Vietoris to the universal cover
\widetilde{L_{1}\#L_{2}}
.
∎

Corollary 5.5
.

If
\dim L\geq 4
and
L
is an arbitrary (finite) connected sums or products of

(1)

Aspherical manifolds, i.e.
K(\pi,1)
’s;

(2)

Spherical manifolds, i.e.
S^{n}/\Gamma
where
\Gamma\subset SO_{n+1}
is a finite subgroup acting freely on
S^{n}
by rotations;

(3)

Compact Lie groups,

then, if
L
is spin, any Lagrangian embedding of
L
into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
has non-vanishing Maslov class.

Appendix A
Verification of various identities in open-closed string topology

In section
A.1
, we fix conventions on orientations of direct and fibre products of manifolds which we use in section
A.2
to verify properties of various string topology operations on de Rham chains.
In section,
A.3
we fix signs for various dg algebras, and use them in section
A.4
to verify properties of various string topology dg algebras and homomorphisms between them.

A.1.
Conventions on orientations

We follow the signs from
[
Iri20
]
section 4.2 by default throughout the paper.
Here we briefly review the conventions on orientations of direct and fibre products of manifolds, and point out some consequence on signs that we shall use afterwards.

For direct products, let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be oriented manifolds; then we orient
M_{1}\times M_{2}
by

T(M_{1}\times M_{2})\cong TM_{1}\oplus TM_{2}.

For fibre products, let
M_{1},M_{2},X
be oriented manifolds and
\pi_{i}\colon M_{i}\to X

(i=1,2)
be smooth maps, where
\pi_{2}\colon M_{2}\to X
is a submersion. Then the fibre product
M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2}
can be formed.
We orient
\ker(d\pi_{2})\subset TM_{2}
such that

TM_{2}\cong TX\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})

as oriented vector bundles,
and orient
T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})
as

T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2}).

Remarks A.1
.

In verifying various signs below, we will need to compare orientations in the following three situations:

(1)

If we have
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
oriented smooth manifolds and

\pi_{1}^{1},\pi_{1}^{2}\colon M_{1}\to X,\quad\pi_{2}\colon M_{2}\to X,\quad\pi_{3}\colon M_{3}\to X

smooth maps, with
\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
submersions, then

T((M_{1}\,{}_{\pi^{1}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})\,_{\pi^{2}_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3})\cong T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}^{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})

and

T((M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3})\,_{\pi_{1}^{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})\cong T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2}).

Thus the orientation of these two fibre products differ by
(-1)^{(\dim M_{2}-\dim X)(\dim M_{3}-\dim X)}
.

(2)

Similarly, given

\pi_{1}\colon M_{1}\to X,\quad\pi_{2}^{1},\pi_{2}^{2}\colon M_{2}\to X,\quad\pi_{3}\colon M_{3}\to X

with
\pi_{2}^{1},\pi_{3}
submersions,
then

\displaystyle T((M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}^{1}}M_{2})\,_{\pi_{2}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3})\cong T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}^{1}}M_{2})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2}^{1})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})

and

\displaystyle T(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}^{1}}(M_{2}\,{}_{\pi_{2}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3}))

\displaystyle\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2}^{1}\colon T(M_{2}\,{}_{\pi_{2}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{3}}M_{3})\to TX)

\displaystyle\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(TM_{2}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3})\xrightarrow{d\pi_{2}\oplus 0}TX)

\displaystyle\cong TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2}^{1})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{3}).

Therefore these two fibre products have the same orientation.

(3)

If we have

\pi_{1}^{1},\pi_{1}^{2}\colon M_{1}\to X,\quad\pi_{2}\colon M_{2}\to X

where
\pi_{1}^{1},\pi_{2}
are submersions and
\star\in X
is a point, then

\displaystyle T((\pi_{1}^{1})^{-1}(\star)\,_{\pi_{1}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2})\cong T((\pi_{1}^{1})^{-1}(\star))\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})\cong\ker(d\pi_{1}^{1})\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})

and

\displaystyle(M_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}^{2}}\!\!\times_{\pi_{2}}M_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}^{1}\circ\textup{pr}_{1}}X)^{-1}(\star)\cong\ker(TM_{1}\oplus\ker(d\pi_{2})\xrightarrow{d\pi_{1}^{1}\oplus 0}TX)\cong\ker(d\pi_{1}^{1})\oplus d\pi_{2}.

Therefore these two fibre products have the same orientation.

A.2.
Signs for string topology operations

A.2.1.
Closed string

Recall that given
x\in C_{\dim L+d_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
,
y\in C_{\dim L+d_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
, with
x=[(U_{1},\varphi_{1},\omega_{1})],y=[(U_{2},\varphi_{2},\omega_{2})],

x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y:=(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}[(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}\varphi_{2},\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})].

Lemma A.2
.

(1)

(Leibniz rule) If
x\in C_{\dim L+d}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
,
y\in C_{\dim L+d^{\prime}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
,

\partial^{\textup{dR}}(x\circ_{i}y)=(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)\circ_{i}y+(-1)^{d}x\circ_{i}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}y);

(2)

(Associativity) If
x_{i}\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+d_{i}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{i}+1}(a_{i}))

(i=1,2,3)
,

\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d_{2}d_{3}}(x_{1}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2}\quad(1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq k_{1});

\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle=x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}(x_{2}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})\quad(1\leq i_{1}\leq k_{1},1\leq i_{2}\leq k_{2}).

Proof.

Recall that given a de Rham chain
[(U,\varphi,\omega)]
, the de Rham differential on it is given by

\partial^{\textup{dR}}[(U,\varphi,\omega)]=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}[(U,\varphi,d\omega)],

(1)

Note that
d=\dim U_{1}-|\omega_{1}|-\dim L
, so

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}(x\circ_{i}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}\left((-1)^{d|\omega_{2}|}(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\omega_{1}|+|\omega_{2}|+1}(-1)^{d|\omega_{2}|}(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),d\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|\omega_{1}|+|\omega_{2}|+1}(-1)^{d|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{|\omega_{1}|}(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times d\omega_{2})

Now

\displaystyle(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)\circ_{i}y=(-1)^{(d+1)|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{|\omega_{1}|+1}(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),d\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})

and

\displaystyle x\circ_{i}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}y)=(-1)^{d(|\omega_{2}|+1)}(-1)^{|\omega_{2}|+1}(U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times d\omega_{2}).

Then comparing the exponents checks the signs of (1).

(2)

Write
x_{i}=[(U_{i},\varphi_{i},\omega_{i})]
for
i=1,2,3
. We have
d_{i}=\dim U_{i}-|\omega_{i}|-\dim L
.
Notice that
\deg(x\circ_{i}y)-\dim L=(\deg x-\dim L)+(\deg y-\dim L)
.

For the first identity,

\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}(U_{1}\,{}_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})\circ_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{3}|}((U_{1}\,{}_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2})\,_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ((\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}))\times\varphi_{3}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{3})

and

\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{3}|}(U_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{3}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{3})\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{3}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{3})|\omega_{2}|}((U_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ((\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{3}))\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{3}\times\omega_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{3}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{3})|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{|\omega_{2}||\omega_{3}|}((U_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ((\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{3}))\times\varphi_{2}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{3}).

Notice that the difference between the exponents of
(-1)
in the front of the two expressions is

\displaystyle(d_{1}|\omega_{2}|+(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{3}|)-(d_{1}|\omega_{3}|+(d_{1}+d_{3})|\omega_{2}|+|\omega_{2}||\omega_{3}|)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle d_{2}|\omega_{3}|+d_{3}|\omega_{2}|+|\omega_{2}||\omega_{3}|\equiv(d_{2}+|\omega_{2}|)(d_{3}+|\omega_{3}|)+d_{2}d_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(\dim U_{2}-\dim L)(\dim U_{3}-\dim L)+d_{2}d_{3}\mod 2.

Moreover from Section
A.1
we see that the orientation of
(U_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}
and
(U_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}
differs by
(-1)^{(\dim U_{2}-\dim L)(\dim U_{3}-\dim L)}
, so from Remark
3.7
we see that the first associativity identity is correct.

For the second identity,

\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{3}|}((U_{1}\,{}_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2})\,_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ((\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}))\times\varphi_{3}),\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{3})

and

\displaystyle x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}(x_{2}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{2}|\omega_{3}|}x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}(U_{2}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3},\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{2}\times\varphi_{3}),\omega_{2}\times\omega_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{2}|\omega_{3}|}(-1)^{d_{1}(|\omega_{2}|+|\omega_{3}|)}(U_{1}\,{}_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}(U_{2}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{3}),\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ((\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}\circ(\varphi_{2}\times\varphi_{3}))\times\varphi_{1},\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{3}).

By the discussion in Section
A.1
, the two domains of these two de Rham chains have the same orientation, and comparing the exponents of
(-1)
shows that these two de Rham chains are the same. This proves the second associativity identity.

∎

A.2.2.
Open string

Recall that given
\alpha\in C_{d_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}))
,
\beta\in C_{d_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k_{2}+1}_{\star}(a_{2}))
, with
\alpha=[(V_{1},\psi_{1},\eta_{1})]
,
\beta=[(V_{2},\psi_{2},\eta_{2})]
(so that
d_{1}=\deg\alpha=\dim V_{1}-|\eta_{1}|
and similarly for
\beta
),

\alpha\bullet\beta:=(-1)^{d_{1}|\eta_{2}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2})].

Lemma A.3
.

(1)

(Leibniz rules) If
\alpha\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{d_{1}}(\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1})),\beta\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{d_{2}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
,

\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\alpha\bullet\beta)=(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha)\bullet\beta+(-1)^{d_{1}}\alpha\bullet(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\beta);

(2)

(Associativity of
\bullet
) If
\alpha_{i}\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{*}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{i}+1}(a_{i}))

(i=1,2,3)
,

(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})\bullet\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{1}\bullet(\alpha_{2}\bullet\alpha_{3}).

Proof.

(1)

Given
\alpha=[(V_{1},\psi_{1},\eta_{1})]
and
\beta=[(V_{2},\psi_{2},\eta_{2})]
,

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\alpha\bullet\beta)

\displaystyle=\partial^{\textup{dR}}\left((-1)^{(\deg\alpha_{1})|\eta_{2}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2})]\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\eta_{1}|+|\eta_{2}|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\eta_{2}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},d\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2})]

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\eta_{1}|+|\eta_{2}|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\eta_{2}|}(-1)^{|\eta_{1}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times d\eta_{2})].

On the other hand

\displaystyle(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha)\bullet\beta=(-1)^{|\eta_{1}|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha-1)|\eta_{2}|}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},d\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2})]

and

\displaystyle\alpha\bullet(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\beta)=(-1)^{|\eta_{2}|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)(|\eta_{2}|+1)}[(V_{1}\times V_{2},\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times d\eta_{2})].

(2)

This follows from the fact that the concatenation product
*
is associative, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1})\times\Omega^{k_{2}+1}_{\star}(a_{2})\times\Omega^{k_{3}+1}_{\star}(a_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}+a_{2})\times\Omega^{k_{3}+1}_{\star}(a_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1})\times\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+k_{3}+1}(a_{2}+a_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+1}(a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3})}

strictly commutes.
Also the sign that appears on the left-hand side of the expression in (2) is
(-1)^{(\deg\alpha_{1})|\eta_{2}|}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha_{1}+\deg\alpha_{2})|\eta_{3}|}
and the sign on the right hand side is
(-1)^{(\deg\alpha_{1})(|\eta_{2}|+|\eta_{3}|)}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha_{2})|\eta_{3}|}
.

∎

A.2.3.
Open-closed string

Recall that given
\alpha\in C_{d_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k_{1}+1}_{\star}(a_{1}))
,
x\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+d_{2}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
, with
\alpha=[(V,\psi,\eta)],x=[(U,\varphi,\omega)]
,

\alpha\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x:=(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\psi\circ_{i}^{\Omega}\varphi,\eta\times\omega)].

Lemma A.4
.

(1)

(Leibniz rule) If
\alpha\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{d_{1}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
,
x\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+d_{2}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
,

\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\alpha\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x)=(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x+(-1)^{d_{1}}\alpha\circ_{i}^{\Omega}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x);

(2)

(Associativity) If
\alpha\in C_{d_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
,
x\in C_{\dim L+d_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
,
y\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+d_{3}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{3}+1}(a_{3}))
,

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x)\circ^{\Omega}_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}y

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d_{2}d_{3}}(\alpha\circ_{i_{2}}^{\Omega}y)\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x\quad(1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq k_{1});

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x)\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}^{\Omega}y

\displaystyle=\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}(x\circ_{i_{2}}^{\mathscr{L}}y)\quad(1\leq i_{1}\leq k_{1},1\leq i_{2}\leq k_{2}).

(3)

(Compatibility with
\bullet
) If
\alpha\in C_{d_{1}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
,
\beta\in C_{d_{2}}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
,
x\in C_{\dim L+d_{3}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{3}+1}(a_{3}))
,

\displaystyle(\alpha\bullet\beta)\circ^{\Omega}_{i}x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d_{2}d_{3}}(\alpha\circ^{\Omega}_{i}x)\bullet\beta\quad(1\leq i\leq k_{1})

\displaystyle(\alpha\bullet\beta)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x

\displaystyle=\alpha\bullet(\beta\circ_{i-k_{1}}^{\Omega}x)\quad(k_{1}+1\leq i\leq k_{1}+k_{2})

Proof.

(1)

Suppose that
\alpha=[(V,\psi,\eta)],x=[(U,\varphi,\omega)]
, then

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\alpha\circ_{i}x)

\displaystyle=\partial^{\textup{dR}}\left((-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}\circ(\psi\times\varphi),\eta\times\omega)]\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\eta|+|\omega|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}\circ(\psi\times\varphi),d\eta\times\omega)]

\displaystyle\quad+(-1)^{|\eta|+|\omega|+1}(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}(-1)^{|\eta|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}\circ(\psi\times\varphi),\eta\times d\omega)].

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha)\circ_{i}x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{((\deg\alpha)+1)|\omega|}(-1)^{|\eta|+1}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}\circ(\psi\times\varphi),d\eta\times\omega]

and

\displaystyle\alpha\circ_{i}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)=(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)(|\omega|+1)}(-1)^{|\omega|+1}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,\textup{conc}^{\Omega_{\star}}\circ(\psi\times\varphi),\eta\times d\omega)].

(2)

Write

\displaystyle\alpha=[(V,\psi,\eta)],\quad x=[(U_{1},\varphi_{1},\omega_{1})],\quad y=[(U_{2},\varphi_{2},\omega_{2})].

We have that
d_{1}=\deg\alpha
and
d_{2}=\deg x-\dim L,d_{3}=\deg y-\dim L
.

For the first identity,

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x)\circ^{\Omega}_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left((-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{1}|}[(V\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1},\psi\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}\varphi_{1},\eta\times\omega_{1})]\right)\circ_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{1}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{2}|}[((V\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1})\,_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},(\psi\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}\varphi_{1})\circ_{k_{2}+k_{2}-1}^{\Omega}\varphi_{2},\eta\times\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})]

and

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i_{2}}^{\Omega}y)\circ^{\Omega}_{i_{1}}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}[(V\,_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},\psi\circ_{i_{2}}^{\Omega}\varphi_{2},\eta\times\omega_{2})]\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{3})|\omega_{1}|}[((V\,_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1},(\psi\circ_{i_{2}}^{\Omega}\varphi_{2})\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}\varphi_{1},\eta\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{1})].

The orientation of the domains
(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1})\,_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}
and
(V\,_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2})\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1}
of these two de Rham chains differ by
(-1)^{(\dim U_{1}-\dim L)(\dim U_{2}-\dim L)}
and to turn the differential forms
\eta\times\omega_{2}\times\omega_{1}
into
\eta\times\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}
requires a sign of
(-1)^{|\omega_{1}||\omega_{2}|}
, so the overall sign difference between the two de Rham chains is

\displaystyle d_{1}|\omega_{1}|+(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{2}|-d_{1}|\omega_{2}|-(d_{1}+d_{3})|\omega_{1}|-(\dim U_{1}-\dim L)(\dim U_{2}-\dim L)-|\omega_{1}||\omega_{2}|

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle d_{2}|\omega_{2}|-d_{3}|\omega_{1}|-(\dim U_{1}-\dim L)(\dim U_{2}-\dim L)-|\omega_{1}||\omega_{2}|

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle d_{2}|\omega_{2}|-d_{3}|\omega_{1}|-(d_{2}+|\omega_{1}|)(d_{3}+|\omega_{2}|)-|\omega_{1}||\omega_{2}|\equiv d_{2}d_{3}\mod 2,

using that
d_{2}=\dim U_{1}-|\omega_{1}|-\dim L,d_{3}=\dim U_{2}-|\omega_{2}|-\dim L
.

For the second identity,

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}x)\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}^{\Omega}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{1}|}(-1)^{(d_{1}+d_{2})|\omega_{2}|}[((V\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U_{1})\,_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2},(\psi\circ^{\Omega}_{i_{1}}\varphi_{1})\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}+1}^{\Omega}\varphi_{2},\eta\times\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})]

and

\displaystyle\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}(x\circ_{i_{2}}^{\mathscr{L}}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{2}|\omega_{2}|}\alpha\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}[(V_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}V_{2},\varphi_{1}\circ_{i_{2}}^{\mathscr{L}}\varphi_{2},\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{2}|\omega_{2}|}(-1)^{d_{1}(|\omega_{1}|+|\omega_{2}|)}[(U\,_{i_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}(V_{1}\,{}_{i_{2}}\!\!\times_{0}V_{2}),\psi\circ_{i_{1}}^{\Omega}(\varphi_{1}\circ_{i_{2}}^{\mathscr{L}}\varphi_{2}),\eta\times\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})].

The signs cancel.

(3)

Write

\displaystyle\alpha=[(V_{1},\psi_{1},\eta_{1})],\quad\beta=[(V_{2},\psi_{2},\eta_{2})],\quad x=[(U,\varphi,\omega)].

For the first identity,

\displaystyle(\alpha\bullet\beta)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{\alpha}|\eta_{2}|}(-1)^{(d_{\alpha}+d_{\beta})|\omega|}[((V_{1}\times V_{2})\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U,(\psi_{1}\bullet\psi_{2})\circ_{i}^{\Omega}\varphi,\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2}\times\omega)]

and

\displaystyle(\alpha\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x)\bullet\beta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{\alpha}|\omega|}(-1)^{(d_{\alpha}+d_{x})|\eta_{2}|}[((V_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U)\times V_{2},(\psi_{1}\circ_{i}^{\Omega}\varphi)\bullet\psi_{2},\eta_{1}\times\omega\times\eta_{2})].

The orientation difference between the two domains is
(-1)^{\dim V_{2}(\dim U-\dim L)}
and the sign difference between the two differential forms is
|\eta_{2}||\omega|
, so the overall sign difference is

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}|\eta_{2}|+(d_{\alpha}+d_{\beta})|\omega|-d_{\alpha}|\omega|-(d_{\alpha}+d_{x})|\eta_{2}|-\dim V_{2}(\dim U-\dim L)-|\eta_{2}||\omega|

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle d_{\beta}|\omega|-d_{x}|\eta_{2}|-\dim V_{2}(\dim U-\dim L)-|\eta_{2}||\omega|

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle d_{\beta}|\omega|-d_{x}|\eta_{2}|-(d_{\beta}-|\eta_{2}|)(d_{x}-|\omega|)-|\eta_{2}||\omega|\equiv d_{\beta}d_{x}.

For the second identity,

\displaystyle\alpha\bullet(\beta\circ_{i-k_{1}}^{\Omega}x)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{\alpha}(|\eta_{2}|+|\omega|)}(-1)^{d_{\beta}|\omega|}[(V_{1}\times(V_{2}\,{}_{i-k_{1}}\!\!\times_{0}U),\psi_{1}\bullet(\psi_{2}\circ_{i-k_{1}}\varphi),\eta_{1}\times\eta_{2}\times\omega)].

The sign cancels with the sign in front of
(\alpha\bullet\beta)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}x
.

∎

A.2.4.
The anomaly map

Recall that given
x\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{*}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
represented by the de Rham chain
[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a),\omega)]
, we defined

\mathfrak{o}(x):=(-1)^{(\deg x)+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a);\omega)]\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a)).

Lemma A.5
.

(1)

(Compatibility with
\circ^{\mathscr{L}}
and
\circ^{\Omega}
) If
x\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))
and
y\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{*}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2}))
, then

\mathfrak{o}(x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y)=(-1)^{\deg(y)-\dim L}\mathfrak{o}(x)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}y;

(2)

(Compatibility with
\partial^{\textup{dR}}
) If
x\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
, then

\partial^{\textup{dR}}\mathfrak{o}(x)=-\mathfrak{o}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x).

(3)

(Compatibility with
\delta_{i}
) If
x\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{*}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
, then for
i=0,\dots,k+1
,

\mathfrak{o}((\delta_{i})_{*}(x))=(\delta_{i})_{*}(\mathfrak{o}(x)).

(See section
3.3
for the definition of
\delta_{i}
.)

Proof.

(1)

Write

x=[(U_{1}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1});\omega_{1})]\in C_{d_{1}+\dim L}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1}))

and

y=[(U_{2}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2});\omega_{2})]\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{d_{2}+\dim L}(\mathscr{L}^{k_{2}+1}(a_{2})).

Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}\mathfrak{o}([U_{1}\,{}_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}\varphi_{2}}\mathscr{L}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2});\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{d_{1}|\omega_{2}|+\deg(x\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}y)+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ(\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}\varphi_{2}))^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}\varphi_{2}}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2});\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})],

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x)\circ_{i}^{\Omega}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{(\deg x)+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{1})^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+1}(a_{1});\omega_{1})]\circ_{i}^{\Omega}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{(\deg x)+1+d_{1}|\omega_{2}|}[(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{1})^{-1}(\star)\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}\varphi_{2}}\Omega_{\star}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(a_{1}+a_{2});\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2})]

By Remark
A.1
(3) in section
A.1
, the two domains
(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ(\varphi_{1}\circ_{i}\varphi_{2}))^{-1}(\star)
and
(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{1})^{-1}(\star)\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U_{2}
have the same orientation. The claim now follows from the fact that
\deg(x\circ_{i}y)=\deg(x)+\deg(y)-\dim L
.

(2)

Write
x=[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a);\omega)]
. We have that

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}\mathfrak{o}(x)=(-1)^{|\omega|+1+\deg x+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a);d\omega)]

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{o}\big((-1)^{|\omega|+1}[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a);d\omega)]\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\omega|+1+(\deg x+1)+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a);d\omega)].

(3)

Write
x=[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a);\omega)]
. To distinguish
\delta_{i}
in the context of free and based loop spaces, we temporarily use the notations

\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)\xrightarrow{\delta_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}}\mathscr{L}^{k+2}(a)\quad\textup{and}\quad\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a)\xrightarrow{\delta_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}}\Omega_{\star}^{k+2}(a).

We have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}((\delta^{\mathscr{L}}_{i})_{*}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{o}[(U\xrightarrow{\delta^{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\circ\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k+2}(a);\omega)]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\deg x+1}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\delta^{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\delta^{\mathscr{L}}_{i}\circ\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+2}(a);\omega)]

and

\displaystyle(\delta^{\Omega_{\star}}_{i})_{*}(\mathfrak{o}(x))=(-1)^{\deg x+1}[(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\xrightarrow{\delta^{\Omega_{\star}}_{i}\circ\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k+2}(a);\omega)].

Now the two maps
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}\xrightarrow{\delta^{\mathscr{L}}_{i}}\mathscr{L}^{k+2}\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{0}}L
and
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{0}}L
are identical, so the domains of the two de Rham chains are the same. The two maps from the domains to
\Omega_{\star}^{k+2}(a)
are the same because the two maps
\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a)\xrightarrow{\delta_{i}^{\Omega_{\star}}}\Omega_{\star}^{k+2}(a)
and
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)\xrightarrow{\delta_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}}\mathscr{L}^{k+2}(a)
are compatible under
\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(a)\subset\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)
.

∎

A.3.
Conventions on dg algebras

We fix signs for various dg algebras used in this paper, following Chapter 2 of
[
Fuk03
]
(adjusting for the difference that the degrees we use is homological whereas they are cohomological in
[
Fuk03
]
) and are consistent with
[
Iri20
]
.

Given a graded
\mathbb{K}
-module
C
and a homogeneous element
x\in C_{k}
, we write
|x|=k
.

Definition A.6
.

A
differential graded associative algebra (dg associative algebra)
is a graded
\mathbb{K}
-module
C
together with maps

\partial\colon C_{*}\to C_{*-1}

of degree
-1
and

\bullet\colon C_{*}\otimes C_{*}\to C_{*}

of degree
0
, such that

(A.1)

\displaystyle\partial(x\bullet y)=(\partial x)\bullet y+(-1)^{|x|}x\bullet(\partial y);

(A.2)

\displaystyle(x\bullet y)\bullet z=x\bullet(y\bullet z).

Definition A.7
.

A
differential graded Lie algebra (dg Lie algebra)
is a graded
\mathbb{K}
-module
C
together with maps

\partial\colon C_{*}\to C_{*-1}

of degree
-1
and

[,]\colon C_{*}\otimes C_{*}\to C_{*}

of degree
0
, such that

(A.3)

\displaystyle\partial[x,y]=[\partial x,y]+(-1)^{|x|}[x,\partial y];

(A.4)

\displaystyle[x,y]=(-1)^{|x||y|+1}[y,x];

(A.5)

\displaystyle[[x,y],z]+(-1)^{(|x|+|y|)|z|}[[z,x],y]+(-1)^{(|y|+|z|)|x|}[[y,z],x]=0.

Definition A.8
.

A
homomorphism of dg Lie algebras

(C_{*},\partial,[,])
and
(C^{\prime}_{*},\partial,[,])
is a degree-0 linear map
f\colon C_{*}\to C^{\prime}_{*}
that is a chain map preserving the Lie bracket, i.e.

\displaystyle\partial f(x)=f(\partial x)\quad\textup{for all }x\in C_{*};

\displaystyle[f(x),f(y)]=f([x,y])\quad\textup{ for all }x,y\in C_{*}.

For later purposes, we record the following well-known facts (modulo some unconventional signs) about deformation theories of dg algebras in the forms we need. For general theory of deformations of dg algebras, see e.g.
[
Fuk03
]
and
[
Fuk+09
]
.

Lemma A.9
.

(1)

Let
(C_{*},\partial,[,])
be a dg Lie algebra, and
x\in C_{-1}
. Then if
x
is a Maurer-Cartan element, i.e.

\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0,

then the following deformation of
\partial
,

\partial^{x}\colon C_{*}\to C_{*-1};\quad\partial^{x}(y):=\partial y-[x,y],

is a differential. Moreover,
(C_{*},\partial^{x},[,])
is still a dg Lie algebra.

(2)

Let
(C_{*},\partial,\bullet)
be a dg associative algebra, and
\alpha\in C_{-1}
. Then
\alpha
is a Maurer-Cartan element (“bounding chain” in
[
Fuk+09
]
terminology), i.e.

\partial\alpha-\alpha\bullet\alpha=0,

then the following deformation of
\partial
,

\partial^{\alpha}\colon C_{*}\to C_{*-1},\quad\partial^{\alpha}(\beta):=\partial\alpha-\alpha\bullet\beta+(-1)^{|\beta|}\beta\bullet\alpha,

is a differential. Moreover,
(C_{*},\partial^{\alpha},\bullet)
is still a dg Lie algebra;

(3)

Let
(C_{*},\partial,\bullet)
be a dg associative algebra. Consider the Hochschild cochain complex
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*},C_{*})=\bigoplus_{\ell\geq 0}\textup{Hom}_{*+\ell-1}(C_{*}^{\otimes\ell},C_{*})
. Suppose
\Phi\in\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}(C_{*},C_{*})
is only non-zero in the
1
-ary component and write that component as
\Phi_{1}\in\textup{Hom}_{-1}(C_{*},C_{*})
, and that it satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation in
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*},C_{*})
:

\delta\Phi-\frac{1}{2}[\Phi,\Phi]=0.

Then the following deformation of
\partial
,

\partial^{\Phi}\colon C_{*}\to C_{*-1},\quad\partial^{\Phi}(\beta):=\partial\beta-\Phi_{1}(\beta)

is a differential, with
(C_{*},\partial^{\Phi},\bullet)
still a dg associative algebra. Denote this deformed dg associative algebra by
C_{*}^{\Phi}:=(C_{*},\partial^{\Phi},\bullet)
.

(4)

Under the same setting, the Hochschild cochain complex differential of
C_{*}
deformed by
\Phi\in\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}(C_{*},C_{*})
(as a dg Lie algebra)

\delta_{C_{*}}^{\Phi}\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*},C_{*})\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*},C_{*});\quad\Psi\mapsto\delta\Psi-[\Phi,\Psi]

agrees with the Hochschild cochain complex differential of
C_{*}^{\Phi}

\delta_{C_{*}^{\Phi}}\colon\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Phi},C_{*}^{\Phi})\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Phi},C_{*}^{\Phi}).

Here we have used that the underlying graded vector spaces of
C_{*}
and
C_{*}^{\Phi}
are identical.

A.4.
String topology dg algebras

The goal of this section is to verify Lemma
3.19
and Lemma
3.31
regarding the dg algebra structures on
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
and the homomorphism
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{CO}}\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
.

A.4.1.
Closed string state space

Definition A.10
.

Define an element

\textsf{L}\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L+k-2}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0))

where

•

For
k=2
, consider the map
L\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0)
defined by
y\mapsto(\underline{y},\underline{y},\underline{y})
(recall that for a point
y\in L
, we use
\underline{y}
to denote the constant path at
y
; see Section
3.3
). The de Rham chain
[(L\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{0}(L))]
defines a closed cycle in
C^{\textup{dR}}_{\dim L}(\mathscr{L}^{3}(0))
, which we set to be the
k=2
component of
L
;

•

For all
k\neq 2
, set the
k
-th component of
L
to be 0.

Define

\widetilde{\textsf{L}}:=(-1)^{\dim L+1}\textsf{L}.

The following proof imitates the construction in section 5 in
[
Iri20
]
, where energy-zero moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic curves (whose role is played by
L
in our proof) are used to deform the dg Lie algebra
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
.

Lemma A.11

(Lemma
3.19
(1))
.

(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial,[,])
is a dg Lie algebra.

Instead of checking directly, we will first use Lemma
A.2
to directly verify that
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
is a dg Lie algebra, and show that the term
\partial^{1}=\partial-\partial^{\textup{dR}}=\partial-\partial^{0}
can be given by deforming
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
by a certain Maurer-Cartan element, and thus
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial,[,])
is a dg Lie algebra.

Lemma A.12
.

(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
is a dg Lie algebra.

Proof.

The Leibniz identity (
A.3
) follows from the Leibniz rule of
\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}
(Lemma
A.2
(1)), anti-symmetry (
A.4
) follows from the definition (
3.13
) directly, and the Jacobi identity follows from the fact that
\circ
is a pre-Lie product:

(x\circ y)\circ z-x\circ(y\circ z)=(-1)^{|y||z|}\big((x\circ z)\circ y-x\circ(z\circ y)\big),

which in turn follows from associativity of
\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}
(Lemma
A.2
(2)).
∎

Lemma A.13
.

\widetilde{\textsf{L}}:=(-1)^{\dim L+1}\textsf{L}
is a Maurer-Cartan element in
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
, i.e.

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{dR}}\widetilde{\textsf{L}}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\widetilde{\textsf{L}},\widetilde{\textsf{L}}\right]=0.

Proof.

Now we show this by first verifying that
L
is a Maurer-Cartan element.
The term
\partial^{\textup{dR}}\textsf{L}
vanishes since the de Rham chain representing only non-vanishing component (
a=0,k=2
) of
L
is
[(L\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}^{0}_{c}(L))]
. By arity reasons,
\textsf{L}\circ\textsf{L}
is only non-zero in the component
a=0
,
k=3
, and moreover by definition (
3.12
),

\textsf{L}\circ\textsf{L}(0,3)=\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}\textsf{L}(0,2)-\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}\textsf{L}(0,2).

We claim that
\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}\textsf{L}(0,2)=\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}\textsf{L}(0,2)
.
By definition of
\circ_{i}^{\mathscr{L}}
(see (
3.8
)), we have, for
i=1
or
2
,

\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{i}\textsf{L}(0,2)=[(L\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}L,\iota\circ_{i}\iota,1\times 1)];

here the two evaluation maps
\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\iota
and
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\iota
from
L
to
L
coincide, so
L\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}L\cong L\subset L\times L
is simply the diagonal. Also,
\iota\circ_{i}\iota
denotes the composition

L\cong L\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}L\xrightarrow{\iota\times\iota}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0)\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0)\xrightarrow{\textup{conc}^{\mathscr{L}}_{2,i,2}}\mathscr{L}^{4}(0).

For any
y\in L\cong L\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}L
, one directly checks that
\iota\circ_{i}\iota(y)=(\underline{y},\underline{y},\underline{y},\underline{y})
for either
i=1
or
2
(since the composition of two constant loops
\underline{y}*\underline{y}
returns the same constant loop
\underline{y}
).
Therefore
\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}\textsf{L}(0,2)=\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}\textsf{L}(0,2)
, and thus

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\textsf{L},\textsf{L}](a,k)

\displaystyle=\textsf{L}\circ\textsf{L}(a,k)=\begin{cases}\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}\textsf{L}(0,2)-\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}\textsf{L}(0,2),&a=0,k=2\\
0,&\textup{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=0

for any
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z}),k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
This verifies that
L
is a Maurer-Cartan element.
Now since
\partial^{\textup{dR}}\textsf{L}=0
, it follows immediately that modifying
L
by a sign,
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}:=(-1)^{\dim L+1}\textsf{L}
, also gives a Maurer-Cartan element.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.11
.

We claim that
-[\widetilde{\textsf{L}},-]=\partial^{1}
.
Given any
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, we have, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle[\textsf{L},x](a,k)=

\displaystyle(\textsf{L}\circ x)(a,k)-(-1)^{|x|}(x\circ\textsf{L})(a,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|x|+k}\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}x(a,k-1)+(-1)^{|x|}\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}x(a,k-1)

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|x|}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i-1}x(a,k-1)\circ_{i}\textsf{L}(0,2).

Now recall that (see (
3.7
))

(\partial^{1}x)(a,k):=(-1)^{\dim L+|x|}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(x(a,k-1)),

where

\delta_{i}\colon\mathscr{L}^{i}L\to\mathscr{L}^{i+1}L;\quad\delta_{i}(c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1}):=\begin{cases}(c_{0},\dots,c_{i-1},\underline{\textsf{s}(c_{i})},c_{i},\dots,c_{k-1}),&0\leq i\leq k-1\\
(c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1},\underline{\textsf{t}(c_{k-1})}),&i=k\end{cases}.

One checks that (see section
A.1
for orientation convention)

\displaystyle\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{1}x(a,k-1)=(\delta_{k})_{*}(x(a,k-1)),\quad\textsf{L}(0,2)\circ_{2}x(a,k-1)=(\delta_{0})_{*}(x(a,k-1)),

and for each
i=1,\dots,k-1
,

x(a,k-1)\circ_{i}\textsf{L}(0,2)=(\delta_{i})_{*}(x(a,k-1)).

We see that the signs in front of each terms also match up once we replace
L
by
-\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
:

(A.6)

\displaystyle-[\widetilde{\textsf{L}},x]=\partial^{1}x.

From general deformation theory of dg Lie algebras
(Lemma
A.9
(1)), since
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
is a Maurer-Cartan element, the deformed algebra
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}}-[\widetilde{\textsf{L}},-],[,])
is a dg Lie algebra. Since
-[\widetilde{\textsf{L}},-]=\partial^{1}
, we can conclude that
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1},[,])
is a dg Lie algebra.
∎

A.4.2.
Open string — the de Rham chain part

For the verification of part (2) of Lemma
3.19
and Lemma
3.31
, we partially imitate the strategy in section
A.4.1
; e.g. to show
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial,[,])
is a dg associative algebra,

(1)

Prove the statements for the “de Rham chain” part of the structures, i.e.
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[,])
is a dg associative algebra;

(2)

Use general deformation theory machinery to deform the the dg associative structures using tautological elements in
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
;

(3)

Identify the deformation with
\partial^{1}=\partial-\partial^{0}=\partial-\partial^{\textup{dR}}
.

We start with part (1) in this section.

Lemma A.14
.

(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra with strict unit
[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

Proof.

The Leibniz rule (
A.1
) follows from the Leibniz rule of
\bullet
(Lemma
A.3
(1)), and
associativity follows from the associativity of
\bullet
(Lemma
A.3
(2)).
The strict unitality property is clear.
∎

A.4.3.
Closed-open string map preserves the Lie bracket

A remark on notations:
recall that, given
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
, we have
\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
and
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\in\textup{Hom}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
. For ease of reading, we will put a curly bracket
\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}
when thinking of it as a map, so that e.g.
\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(y)\big)
means applying the homomorphism
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\colon C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\to C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
on the element
\mathcal{CO}_{0}(y)\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

Lemma A.15
.

The map
\mathcal{CO}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
preserves the Lie bracket.

Proof.

We need to verify that for all
x,y\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
,

\mathcal{CO}([x,y])=[\mathcal{CO}(x),\mathcal{CO}(y)].

We verify it arity-by-arity.

•

For the 0-ary part
:
We need to show that

\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x\circ y-(-1)^{|x||y|}y\circ x)=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(y)\big)-(-1)^{|x||y|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(y)\big\}\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\big).

It suffices to show that

\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x\circ y)=(-1)^{|x||y|+1}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(y)\big\}\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\big).

Using (
3.18
), this is the same as
