\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x\circ y)=(-1)^{|x||y|+1}(-1)^{(|x|-1)|y|+1}\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\circ y=(-1)^{|y|}\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\circ y.

where
\circ
on the right-hand side is the open-closed product.
Using (
3.17
) and (
3.19
) to expand, for each
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x\circ y)(a,k)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{1}}(-1)^{|x|+|y|}\mathfrak{o}\big(x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\big)

and

\displaystyle(-1)^{|y|}(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\circ y)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{2}}(-1)^{|x|+|y|}\mathfrak{o}\big(x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\big)\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})

where (see equations (
3.12
) and (
3.19
))

\displaystyle\ddagger_{1}

\displaystyle=(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(k^{\prime}-1)(|y|+1+k^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\ddagger_{2}

\displaystyle=(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+k^{\prime}(|y|+1+k^{\prime\prime})

and therefore the discrepancy is
|y|+1+k^{\prime\prime}\equiv\deg(y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}))-\dim L\mod 2
(see (
3.4
)).
The result then follows from the fact that (Lemma
A.5
(1))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}\big(x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\big)=(-1)^{\deg(y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}))-\dim L}\mathfrak{o}\big(x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\big)\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}).

•

For the unary part:
We need to show that

\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}([x,y])\big\}(\alpha)=\big\{[\mathcal{CO}(x),\mathcal{CO}(y)]_{1}\big\}(\alpha)

for
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
and
x,y\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
.

Expanding both sides, this really means

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x\circ y)\big\}\alpha

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|x||y|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(y\circ x)\big\}\alpha

\displaystyle=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\big(\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(y)\}\alpha\big)-(-1)^{|x||y|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(y)\big\}\big(\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\}\alpha\big).

Using (
3.18
), this turns into

\alpha\circ(x\circ y)-(\alpha\circ x)\circ y=(-1)^{|x||y|}\left(\alpha\circ(y\circ x)-(\alpha\circ y)\circ x\right).

This formula is analogous to the Jacobi for the loop bracket (or really that the
\circ
-product is a pre-Lie product), e.g. Lemma 4.2 of
[
CS99
]
.
It follows from Lemma part (2) (the associativity part) of
A.4
, analogous to that in the proof of Lemma
A.12
.

•

For higher arity parts:
both sides of the equation automatically vanish by construction.

∎

A.4.4.
Closed-open string map — the de Rham chain part

We will temporarily use the notation
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}
to denote the dg Lie algebra
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[-,-])
, and
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}
to denote the dg associative algebra
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial^{\textup{dR}},[-,-])
.
We temporarily use the following notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{1}\colon(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}\big((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}\big)

to mean the map with only the unary component being non-zero and is given by
\mathcal{CO}_{1}
as in (
3.18
), and all the
\ell
-ary component where
\ell\neq 1
are set to 0.

Lemma A.16
.

The map
\mathcal{CO}_{1}\colon(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}\big((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}\big)
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras.

Proof.

By arity reasons, to verify that
\mathcal{CO}_{1}
preserves the Lie bracket, the only equation needed to be checked is

\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}([x,y])\big\}(\alpha)=\big\{[\mathcal{CO}(x),\mathcal{CO}(y)]_{1}\big\}(\alpha)\quad\quad\textup{for all }\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}\textup{ and }x,y\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}.

This follows from (the proof of) Lemma
A.15
.

To show that
\mathcal{CO}
preserves the differential, we need to verify that for all
x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
,

\delta\big(\mathcal{CO}(x)\big)=\mathcal{CO}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x).

•

For the unary part
: we need

\big\{\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))\big\}_{1}(\alpha)=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)\big\}(\alpha)

for
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.
That is,

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x)\big\}(\alpha)=\partial^{\textup{dR}}

\displaystyle\left(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\alpha\right)-(-1)^{|x|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha).

Using (
3.18
) we see that this is the same as

\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\alpha\circ x)=(\partial^{\textup{dR}}\alpha)\circ x+(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha\circ(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x).

But this follows from the Leibniz rule of
\circ=\circ^{\Omega}
in Lemma
A.4
(1).

•

For the binary part
, we need

\big\{\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))\big\}_{2}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{2}(\partial x)\big\}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.
The right-hand side vanishes because
\mathcal{CO}_{2}
is
0
by definition.
Expanding the left-hand side, we see that this equation is the same as

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})=(-1)^{|\alpha_{1}||x|}\alpha_{1}\bullet\big(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\alpha_{2})\big)+\big(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\alpha_{1})\big)\bullet\alpha_{2},

or, using (
3.18
),

(\alpha_{1}\bullet\alpha_{2})\circ x=\alpha_{1}\bullet(\alpha_{2}\circ x)+(-1)^{|\alpha_{2}||x|}(\alpha_{1}\circ x)\bullet\alpha_{2}.

But this follows from the compatibility of
\circ=\circ^{\Omega}
with
\bullet
in Lemma
A.4
(3).

•

For
0
-ary and higher arity parts:
both sides of the equation automatically vanish by construction.

∎

A.4.5.
Open string

In this section we will show:

Lemma A.17

(Lemma
3.19
(2))
.

(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial,\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra with strict unit
[\underline{\star}]\in C_{0}^{\Omega_{\star}}
;

We have previously shown that
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}
is a dg associative algebra.
The proof will proceed by deforming it to
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
in two stages:

•

Step 1
(Lemma
A.18
): We first consider an intermediate algebra
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}}:=\big(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial^{\textup{int}},\bullet\big)
with differential

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{int}}:=\partial^{0}+(\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}

where, for each
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

(A.7)

\displaystyle\partial^{0}:=\partial^{\textup{dR}},\quad\big((\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\alpha\big)(a,k):=(-1)^{\dim L+|\alpha|}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)).

The difference between this and the differential
\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1}
(in equations (
3.6
), (
3.7
)) is that the summation in
\partial^{\textup{int}}
does not contain the two “boundary” marked points
i=0
and
k
.
This step is achieved by pushing forward the Maurer-Cartan element
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}\in(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}
along
\mathcal{CO}^{1}\colon(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}})
and using general deformation theoretic machinery.

•

Step 2
(Lemma
A.20
): We then identify a Maurer-Cartan element of
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}}
to deform it into the desired dg associative algebra
C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.

Lemma A.18
.

(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}}
is a dg associative algebra.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.13
,
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}
is a Maurer-Cartan element. By Lemma
A.16
, the element
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})\in\textbf{{CH}}^{-1}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}})
is a Maurer-Cartan element. By general deformation theory (Lemma
A.9
(3)), the chain complex
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial^{\textup{dR}}-\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}}),\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra, since the only non-zero-arity component of
\mathcal{CO}_{1}\colon(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}\big((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}\big)
is the unary one.
Therefore we just need to identify
\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})\}\in\textup{Hom}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
with
(\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}
.

Given
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
, by definition (
3.18
) and that
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}=(-1)^{\dim L+1}\textsf{L}
, we have
\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})\big\}(\alpha)=(-1)^{|\alpha|+1}\alpha\circ\widetilde{\textsf{L}}=(-1)^{\dim L+|\alpha|}\alpha\circ\textsf{L}
. By (
3.19
), for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
:

\displaystyle\big(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})\big\}\alpha\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L+|\alpha|}(\alpha\circ\textsf{L})(a,k)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L+|\alpha|}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i-1}\alpha(a,k-1)\circ_{i}\textsf{L}(0,2).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\big((\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\alpha\big)(a,k)=(-1)^{\dim L+|\alpha|}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)).

Following the definition and the orientation conventions, one can check that
\alpha(a,k-1)\circ_{i}\textsf{L}(0,2)=(\delta_{i})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)).

Thus we have identified
-\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})
with
(\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}
.
∎

Definition A.19
.

Define an element

\star^{1}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}C^{\textup{dR}}_{k-1}(\Omega_{\star}^{k+1}(0))

as follows:

•

For
k=1
, consider the map
\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0)
mapping to
(\underline{\star},\underline{\star})
(recall that
\star\in L
is the chosen basepoint, and
\underline{\star}
means the constant path at
\star
). The de Rham chain
[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}^{0}_{c}(\textup{pt}))]
defines a closed cycle in
C^{\textup{dR}}_{0}(\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0))
, which we set to be the
k=1
component of
\star^{1}
;

•

For any other
k\neq 1
, set the component
\star^{1}(0,k)=0
.

Define

(A.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\star^{1}}:=(-1)^{(\dim L+1)}\star^{1}\in C_{-1}^{\Omega_{\star}}(0).

Lemma A.20
.

\widetilde{\star^{1}}=(-1)^{(\dim L+1)}\star^{1}
is a Maurer-Cartan element (bounding chain) in
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{int}}\widetilde{\star^{1}}-\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}=0.

Proof.

We have
\partial^{\textup{dR}}\star^{1}=0
, and
\big((\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\star^{1}\big)(a,k)=0
unless
a=0
and
k=2
, in which case

\displaystyle\big((\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\star^{1}\big)(0,2)=(-1)^{\dim L}(\delta_{1})_{*}[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)].

On the other hand, we also have
\star^{1}\bullet\star^{1}(a,k)=0
unless
a=0
and
k=2
, in which case

(\star^{1}\bullet\star^{1})(0,2)=-[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)]\bullet[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)].

Now both
(\delta_{1})_{*}[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)]
and
[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)]\bullet[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0);1)]
are equal to

[(\textup{pt}\xrightarrow{\iota}\Omega^{3}_{\star}(0);1)]\in C_{0}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega^{3}_{\star}(0)),\quad\iota(\textup{pt}):=(\underline{\star},\underline{\star},\underline{\star}).

Therefore

(-1)^{\dim L}(\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\star^{1}+(\star^{1}\bullet\star^{1})=0.

Thus with
\widetilde{\star^{1}}:=(-1)^{\dim L+1}\star^{1}
, we have

(\partial^{1})^{\textup{int}}\widetilde{\star^{1}}-\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}=0.

∎

Proof of Lemma
A.17
.

It remains to identify the deformation provided by
\widetilde{\star^{1}}
with
\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1}
.
That
[\underline{\star}]
is the identity is straightforward from definition.

We claim that for any
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
,
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha(a,k-1)=(\delta_{0})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)),\quad\alpha(a,k-1)\bullet\star^{1}(0,1)=(\delta_{k})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)).

Say
\alpha(a,k-1)=[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Omega^{k}_{\star}(a);\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(U))]
. Then from the definitions, both sides of the first equation above are equal to
[(U\xrightarrow{\delta\circ\varphi}\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a);\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(U))]
where
\Omega_{\star}^{k}(a)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a)
is given by

(c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1})\in\Omega_{\star}^{k}(a)\mapsto(\underline{\star},c_{0},\dots,c_{k-1})\in\Omega^{k+1}_{\star}(a).

The second equation is similar.

These equations then translate to (using (
3.14
) and (
A.8
))

-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)(a,k)=(-1)^{|\alpha|+\dim L}(\delta_{0})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1))

and

(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})(a,k)=(-1)^{|\alpha|+\dim L+k}(\delta_{k})_{*}(\alpha(a,k-1)).

It follows from general deformation theory (Lemma
A.9
(3)) that

\alpha\mapsto\partial^{\textup{int}}\alpha-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})

is a differential, and together with
\bullet
, they make
C_{*}^{\Omega}
into a dg associative algebra. But
\partial=\partial^{\textup{int}}-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet-)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(-\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}).
Therefore
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},\partial,\bullet)
is a dg associative algebra.
∎

A.4.6.
Closed-open string map

In this section we finish the proof of:

Lemma A.21

(Lemma
3.31
)
.

\mathcal{CO}
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebra.
Moreover,
\mathcal{CO}
respects the decompositions of
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}},C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})
into
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma A.22
.

\mathcal{CO}_{1}\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}})
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras.

As before,
\mathcal{CO}_{1}
means the map with only the unary component being non-zero and is given by
\mathcal{CO}_{1}
as in (
3.18
).

Proof.

This follows from the facts that the dg Lie algebra
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
is given by deforming
(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}
using the Maurer-Cartan element
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
, that the dg associative algebra
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{int}}
is given by deforming
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}
using
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})
, that
\mathcal{CO}_{1}\colon(C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}})^{\textup{dR}}\to\textbf{{CH}}^{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}})
is a homomorphism of dg Lie algebras (Lemma
A.16
), and the general deformation theory fact that deforming the dg Lie algebra
\textbf{{CH}}^{*}((C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}},(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}})
using the Maurer-Cartan element
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})
is the same as taking Hochschild cochains of the dg associative algebra obtained by deforming
(C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}})^{\textup{dR}}
using
\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\widetilde{\textsf{L}})
(see Lemma
A.9
(4)).
∎

Lemma A.23
.

Suppose
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))
and
x\in C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a^{\prime\prime}))
.

(1)

For
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
, i.e.
2\leq i+1\leq k^{\prime}+1
,

\displaystyle\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\big(\alpha\circ_{i}x\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{i+1}x;

(2)

For
i+1=1
,

(-1)^{\deg x+(\deg x-\dim L)(\deg\alpha)}\mathfrak{o}(x)\bullet\alpha=-\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{1}x;

(3)

For
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

\big(\alpha\circ_{i}x\big)\bullet\star^{1}(0,1)=\big(\alpha\bullet\star^{1}(0,1)\big)\circ_{i}x;

(4)

For
i=k^{\prime}+1
,

(-1)^{\deg x}\alpha\bullet\mathfrak{o}(x)=-\big(\alpha\bullet\star^{1}(0,1)\big)\circ_{k^{\prime}+1}x.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:=[(V\xrightarrow{\psi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime});\eta)]
and
x:=[(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathscr{L}^{k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a^{\prime\prime});\omega)]
.
Then for
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\big(\alpha\circ_{i}x\big)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U\xrightarrow{\psi\circ_{i}\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}}(a);\eta\times\omega)]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U\xrightarrow{\star^{1}\bullet(\psi\circ_{i}\varphi)}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a);\eta\times\omega)].

where the map
V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U\xrightarrow{\star^{1}\bullet(\psi\circ_{i}\varphi)}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a)
is the composition

\textup{pt}\times(V\,_{i}\!\!\times_{0}U)\xrightarrow{(\underline{\star},\underline{\star})\times(\psi\circ_{i}\varphi)}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0)\times\Omega^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}}_{\star}(a)\xrightarrow{*}\Omega^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}_{\star}(a).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{i+1}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(V\xrightarrow{\star^{1}\bullet\psi}\Omega^{k^{\prime}+2}_{\star}(a^{\prime});\eta)]\circ_{i+1}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{\textup{ev}_{i+1}\circ(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\xrightarrow{(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)\circ_{i+1}\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a);\eta\times\omega)]

where the map
V\xrightarrow{\star^{1}\bullet\psi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+2}(a^{\prime})
is the composition

\displaystyle\textup{pt}\times V\xrightarrow{(\underline{\star},\underline{\star})\times\psi}\Omega^{2}_{\star}(0)\times\Omega^{k^{\prime}+1}_{\star}(a)\xrightarrow{*}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+2}(a).

Then (1) follows from the fact that the two maps
V\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\psi}L
and
V\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}_{i+1}\circ(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)}L
are identical, and that the two maps

V\,_{\textup{ev}_{i}\circ\psi}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\xrightarrow{\psi\circ_{i}\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}}(a)\xrightarrow{\star^{1}\bullet-}\Omega^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}_{\star}(a)

and

V\,_{\textup{ev}_{i+1}\circ(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\xrightarrow{(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)\times\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+2}(a^{\prime})\,_{\textup{ev}_{i+1}}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}}\mathscr{L}^{k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{\circ_{i+1}}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a)

are identical.

For (2),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x)\bullet\alpha

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\deg x+(\deg x-\dim L)|\eta|}[((\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)\times V\xrightarrow{\varphi\bullet\psi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a);\omega\times\eta)]

Notice that

\displaystyle\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{1}x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{\textup{ev}_{1}\circ(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\xrightarrow{(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)\circ_{i+1}\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a);\eta\times\omega)]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}[(V\,_{\star}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\xrightarrow{(\star^{1}\bullet\psi)\circ_{i+1}\varphi}\Omega_{\star}^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}+1}(a);\eta\times\omega)]

where
V\xrightarrow{\star}L
denotes the constant map to
\star\in L
. Therefore
V\,_{\star}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi}U\cong V\times(\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi)^{-1}(\star)
, and therefore the difference in orientations of the two de Rham chains is
(-1)^{(\dim V)(\dim U-\dim L)}
. Also, the de Rham forms differ by
\eta\times\omega=(-1)^{|\eta||\omega|}\omega\times\eta
. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x)\bullet\alpha

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\dim V)(\dim U-\dim L)+|\eta||\omega|+\deg x+(\deg x-\dim L)|\eta|+(\deg\alpha)|\omega|}\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{1}x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\deg x+(\deg x-\dim L)(\deg\alpha)}\big(\star^{1}(0,1)\bullet\alpha\big)\circ_{1}x.

This gives (2). The verifications of (3) and (4) are analogous.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.21
.

We have already checked that the Lie bracket is preserved in Lemma
A.15
. To check that the differential is preserved, we again check that
\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))=\mathcal{CO}(\partial x)
arity-by-arity.

•

For the 0-ary part
: We need to show that

\big(\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))\big)_{0}=\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\partial x),

that is,

\partial\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\big)=\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\partial x).

Recalling that
\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1}
(see (
3.6
)) and
\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)(a,k)=(-1)^{|x|}\mathfrak{o}(x(a,k))
(see (
3.17
)), we have that for
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\partial\big(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\big)(a,k)=(-1)^{|x|}\big(\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\mathfrak{o}(x(a,k)))\big)+\partial^{1}(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x))(a,k)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\partial x)(a,k)=(-1)^{|x|-1}\big(\mathfrak{o}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x(a,k))\big)+\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\partial^{1}x)(a,k).

First, we have
\partial^{\textup{dR}}(\mathfrak{o}(x(a,k)))=-\mathfrak{o}(\partial^{\textup{dR}}x(a,k))
by Lemma
A.5
(2).
On the other hand, we have, for each
i=0,\dots,k
,

\mathfrak{o}\big((\delta_{i})_{*}(x(a,k-1))\big)=(\delta_{i})_{*}\big(\mathfrak{o}(x(a,k-1))\big)

by Lemma
A.5
(3). Therefore

\displaystyle\partial^{1}(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x))(a,k)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L+|x|-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)(a,k-1))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}(\delta_{i})_{*}(\mathfrak{o}(x(a,k-1)))

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{CO}_{0}(\partial^{1}x)(a,k)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|x|-1}\mathfrak{o}(\partial^{1}x(a,k))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim L-1}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}\mathfrak{o}((\delta_{i})_{*}(x(a,k-1)))

are the same.

•

For the unary part
: we need

\big\{\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))\big\}_{1}(\alpha)=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\partial x)\big\}(\alpha)

for
\alpha\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
.
That is,

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(\partial x)\big\}(\alpha)=\partial

\displaystyle\left(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\alpha\right)-(-1)^{|x|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\partial\alpha)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|x|-1}\left((-1)^{|\alpha|(|x|-1)}\alpha\bullet\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)-\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\bullet\alpha\right).

It follows from Lemma
A.22
that

\displaystyle\big\{(\mathcal{CO}_{1})(\partial x)\big\}(\alpha)=\partial^{\textup{int}}\left(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\alpha\right)-(-1)^{|x|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}(\partial^{\textup{int}}\alpha).

Also, from (the proof of) Lemma
A.17
we have

(\partial-\partial^{\textup{int}})(\alpha)=-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}).

Therefore it suffices to show that

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle-\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\left(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\alpha\right)+(-1)^{|x|+|\alpha|}\left(\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\alpha\right)\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}

\displaystyle-(-1)^{|x|}\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{1}(x)\big\}\big(-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})\big)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{|x|-1}\left((-1)^{|\alpha|(|x|-1)}\alpha\bullet\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)-\mathcal{CO}_{0}(x)\bullet\alpha\right).

Unwinding the definitions, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{o}(x)\bullet\alpha+(-1)^{|\alpha|(|x|-1)}\alpha\bullet\mathfrak{o}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha||x|}\left(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet(\alpha\circ x)-(-1)^{|\alpha|+|x|}(\alpha\circ x)\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)\circ x+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})\circ x\right).

At
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet(\alpha\circ x)\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{1}}\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1)\bullet\big(\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\big);

\displaystyle\big((\alpha\circ x)\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{2}}\big(\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\big)\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1);

\displaystyle\big((\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)\circ x\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k^{\prime}+1)+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}+1\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{3}}\big(\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1)\bullet\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\big)\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime});

\displaystyle\big((\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})\circ x\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k^{\prime}+1)+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}+1\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{4}}\big(\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1)\big)\circ_{i}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime});

\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}(x)\bullet\alpha(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{5}}\mathfrak{o}(x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}))\bullet\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime});

\displaystyle\alpha\bullet\mathfrak{o}(x)(a,k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\ddagger_{6}}\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\bullet\mathfrak{o}(x)(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}).

where

\displaystyle\ddagger_{1}

\displaystyle=|\alpha|+|x|+(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+k^{\prime}(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime});

\displaystyle\ddagger_{2}

\displaystyle=(k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+k^{\prime}(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime});

\displaystyle\ddagger_{3}

\displaystyle=(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(k^{\prime}+1)(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime})+|\alpha|;

\displaystyle\ddagger_{4}

\displaystyle=(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(k^{\prime}+1)(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime})+k^{\prime};

\displaystyle\ddagger_{5}

\displaystyle=k^{\prime\prime}|\alpha|;

\displaystyle\ddagger_{6}

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}(|x|-1).

By parts (1) and (3) of Lemma
A.23
we have

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha||x|}\big(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet(\alpha\circ x)-(\widetilde{\star^{1}}\bullet\alpha)\circ x\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha||x|}\left(-\sum(-1)^{(k^{\prime}+1)(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime})+|\alpha|}(\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1)\bullet\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime}))\circ_{1}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime})\right)

and

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha||x|}\big(-(-1)^{|\alpha|+|x|}(\alpha\circ x)\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}+(-1)^{|\alpha|}(\alpha\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}})\circ x\big)(a,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{|\alpha|(|x|+1)}\sum(-1)^{k^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime}+(k^{\prime}+1)(|x|+1+k^{\prime\prime})}\big(\alpha(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\bullet\widetilde{\star^{1}}(0,1)\big)\circ_{k^{\prime}+1}x(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}).

The desired equality then follows from parts (2) and (4) of Lemma
A.23
.

•

For the binary part
, we need

\big\{\delta(\mathcal{CO}(x))\big\}_{2}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\big\{\mathcal{CO}_{2}(\partial x)\big\}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in C_{*}^{\Omega_{\star}}
. This follows from the proof of Lemma
A.16
.

∎

Appendix B
Kuranishi structures and virtual fundamental chains

In this appendix, we explain the proof of Theorem
4.11
using the theory of Kuranishi structures and virtual techniques, following
[
Fuk+20
,
Iri20
]
.
The structure of the proof is completely analogous to that in
[
Iri20
]
(and much simpler since in working with the model of the free loop space from
[
Wan23
]
, we only need to work with finite-dimensional spaces).

B.1.
Kuranishi structures on the moduli spaces

To construct the necessary virtual fundamental chains of the moduli spaces and verify their compatibilities at the chain level in general requires using virtual techniques. We will use the theory of Kuranishi structures in
[
Fuk+09
,
Fuk+09a
,
Fuk+20
]
.

We briefly recall the basic definitions in the theory of Kuranishi structures.
We follow Section 10 of
[
Iri20
]
.
We remark that our proof is largely independent of the details of the constructions of Kuranishi structures, and only relies on certain expected properties of the Kuranishi structures. For example, one could follow the global Kuranishi chart approach in e.g.
[
AMS21
,
Rab25
,
HH25
]
.
Also, the main extra ingredient relative to
[
Iri20
]
is that extra care needs to be taken for energy-zero moduli spaces, which can be covered with one Kuranishi chart.
For these reasons, and to avoid lengthy discussions of chart transitions, we introduce various required notions on one Kuranishi chart, and refer to
[
Fuk+20
]
for more details on chart transitions.

Remark B.1
.

The notion of Kuranishi structures we use differs from the notion of Kuranishi charts in e.g.
[
Fuk+09a
]
in that we use smooth manifolds and vector bundles instead of orbifolds and orbibundles, because all of our moduli spaces contain at least one marked point and has no sphere bubblings (see Remark 10.1 in
[
Iri20
]
).

B.1.1.
Kuranishi charts and Kuranishi spaces

Definition B.2

(
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 3.1)
.

Let
X
be a separable, metrizable topological space.
A
Kuranishi chart
on
X
is a tuple
\mathscr{U}:=(U,\mathscr{E},s,\psi)
such that

(1)

U
is a smooth manifold;

(2)

\mathscr{E}\to U
is a smooth vector bundle;

(3)

s\colon U\to\mathscr{E}
is a section of the bundle
\mathscr{E}
;

(4)

\psi\colon s^{-1}(0)\to X
is a homeomorphism onto an open set in
X
.

The (virtual)
dimension
of
\mathscr{U}
is by definition
\textup{v}\dim\mathscr{U}:=\dim U-\textup{rank}\mathscr{E}
.
An
orientation
on
\mathscr{U}
is a pair of orientations on
U
and
\mathscr{E}
.

There is a notion of
oriented-preserving embeddings
of Kuranishi charts (
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 3.2, 3.4) and of
coordinate changes
of Kuranishi charts (
op. cit.
, Definition 3.6). A
Kuranishi structure

\widehat{\mathscr{U}}
(
op. cit.
, Definition 3.9) on a space
X
is a collection of Kuranishi charts
\mathscr{U}_{p}=(U_{p},\mathscr{E}_{p},s_{p},\psi_{p})
at each
p\in X
together with coordinate changes between overlapping charts satisfying certain consistency. The pair
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
is called a
Kuranishi space
(
op. cit.
, Definition 3.11).

Definition B.3

(
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 3.40)
.

Let
\mathscr{U}=(U,\mathscr{E},s,\psi)
be a Kuranishi chart and
M
be a
C^{\infty}
-manifold.
A
strongly continuous map
from
\mathscr{U}
to
M
is a continuous map
f\colon U\to M
. It is called
strongly smooth
if
f\colon U\to M
is smooth. It is called
weakly submersive
if
f
is a submersion.

A strongly continuous (
resp.
strongly smooth, weakly submersive) map
\widehat{f}
from a Kuranishi space
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
to
M
assigns a continuous (
resp.
smooth, submersive) map
f_{p}\colon U_{p}\to Y
to each
p\in X
, satisfying compatibility with chart transition maps.

To work with moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic discs with Lagrangian boundary conditions, we need to introduce Kuranishi spaces with boundaries and corners, as well as the notions of
admissible Kuranishi charts
and
admissible Kuranishi spaces
, where admissibility roughly means that the coordinate changes satisfy exponential decay estimates near the boundaries. Notions like embeddings and coordinate changes also have “admissible” versions. See
[
Fuk+20
]
, Section 25.

Maps between admissible Kuranishi spaces with corners are required to respect the corner stratifications. There are notions of
corner-stratified smooth maps
and
corner-stratified wewak submersions
. See
[
Fuk+20
]
, Section 26.

An
isomorphism
of admissible Kuranishi spaces
(X_{1},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{1}})
and
(X_{2},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{2}})
is a pair of admissible embeddings
(X_{1},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{1}})\to(X_{2},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{2}})
and
(X_{2},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{2}})\to(X_{1},\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{1}})
whose compositions are the identity embeddings (
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 4.24).

B.1.2.
Existence of Kuranishi structures

The theorem we will use is Theorem 7.20 of
[
Iri20
]
. We will not reproduce the entire statement of the theorem here due to its length, but we will recall the main points and properties below.

Let
L
be a closed, connected, oriented, and spin manifold of dimension
n
, together with a Lagrangian embedding into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
equipped with the standard symplectic structure.
Take the standard complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
2\varepsilon
is less than the minimal energy of non-constant
J
-holomorphic discs with boundaries on
L
.

Take a Hamiltonian
H\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times[0,1]_{t})
satisfying the displaceability Assumption
4.9
.
Also recall that
\left\lVert{H}\right\rVert
is the Hofer norm (
4.1
) of
H
.
Let
U\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be such that
\varepsilon(U-1)\geq 2\left\lVert{H}\right\rVert
.

We consider the moduli spaces
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(a),\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(a),\mathscr{N}^{0}_{k+1}(a)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\beta\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
. These are defined in detail in section 7.2.1 and 7.2.2 of
[
Iri20
]
, and are Gromov compatifications/bordifications of the uncompactified moduli spaces in section
2
.

Define

\mathfrak{G}_{0}:=\{\beta\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta)\neq\emptyset\textup{ for some }k\},

and let
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
be the submonoid generated by
\mathfrak{G}_{0}
. Then by Gromov compactness,
\mathfrak{G}
satisfies the condition in Definition
4.2
, i.e. it is a monoid of curve classes (see e.g. (3.1.8) in
[
Fuk+09
]
).
Similarly, define

\mathfrak{N}_{0}:=\{\eta\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta)\neq\emptyset\textup{ for some }k\},

and let
\mathfrak{N}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
be the
\mathfrak{G}
-module generated by
\mathfrak{N}_{0}
. Then
\mathfrak{N}
satisfies the condition in Definition
4.3
, i.e. it is a module of
H
-perturbed curve classes over
\mathfrak{G}
.

Theorem B.4

(
[
Iri20
]
, Theorem 7.20)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
P\in\{\{m\},[m,m+1]\}
, there exist the following data:

(1)

Compact, oriented, admissible Kuranishi spaces

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\beta\in\mathfrak{G},\,\,E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k);

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{k+1}^{0}(\eta:P),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\eta\in\mathfrak{N},\,\,E(\eta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k);

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta:P),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\eta\in\mathfrak{N},\,\,E(\eta)<\varepsilon(m-k-U),

whose underlying topological spaces are

P\times\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta),\quad P\times\mathscr{N}^{0}_{k+1}(\eta),\quad P\times\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta)

respectively. The (virtual) dimensions of these Kuranishi spaces are

\displaystyle\dim\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)

\displaystyle=\mu(\beta)+n+k-2+\dim P;

\displaystyle\dim\mathscr{N}^{0}_{k+1}(\eta:P)

\displaystyle=\mu(\eta)+n+k+\dim P;

\displaystyle\dim\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta:P)

\displaystyle=\mu(\eta)+n+k+1+\dim P.

(2)

Corner stratified strongly smooth maps (
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 26.6 (1)):

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M},P}

\displaystyle\colon\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1};

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{0},P}

\displaystyle\colon\mathscr{N}^{0}_{k+1}(\eta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1};

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0},P}

\displaystyle\colon\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1},

such that their underlying maps are

\textup{id}_{P}\times\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}},\quad\textup{id}_{P}\times\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{0}},\quad\textup{id}_{P}\times\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}}

respectively. We require that the maps
(\textup{id}_{P}\times\textup{pr}_{0})\circ\textup{ev}
are corner stratified weak submersions (
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 26.6 (2)), for each of these evaluation maps, where
\textup{pr}_{0}\colon L^{\times(k+1)}\to L
is the projection to the first factor.

(3)

An isomorphism of admissible Kuranishi structures

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:P)\cong P\times L\times D^{k-2}

for each
k\geq 2
, so that
\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M},P}\colon\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:P)\to P\times L^{\times(k+1)}
coincides with
\textup{pr}_{P}\times(\textup{pr}_{L})^{k+1}
. Here
D^{k-2}
is identified with the Stasheff cell (
[
FO97
]
).

(4)

Various boundary and corner compatibility conditions, spelled out in parts (iv)-(vi) in Theorem 7.20 of
[
Iri20
]
.

Furthermore, we need maps from these moduli spaces to
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
. In
[
Iri20
]
, this is done in sections 7.3 and 7.4. Our situation is much simpler because our
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
is simpler than the infinite-dimensional ones in
[
Iri20
]
.
The following proposition is an analogue of Proposition 7.26 in
[
Iri20
]
.
For example, we can obtain
strongly smooth
maps in the statement, due to the simpler definition of
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
.

Proposition B.5
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and
P\in\{\{m\},[m,m+1]\}
, one can define strongly smooth maps

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}\colon\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\beta\in\mathfrak{G},\,\,E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k);

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{0}}\colon\mathscr{N}_{k+1}^{0}(\eta:P)\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\eta),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\eta\in\mathfrak{N},\,\,E(\eta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k);

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}}\colon\mathscr{N}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\eta:P)\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\eta),\quad

\displaystyle\textup{ where }\eta\in\mathfrak{N},\,\,E(\eta)<\varepsilon(m-k-U),

so that the diagrams analogous to those in Proposition 7.26 of
[
Iri20
]
commute.
Moreover, when
\beta=0
, under the identification (
B.1
), the map
\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}
is given by

(B.2)

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}\colon\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:P)\cong P\times L\times D^{k-2}

\displaystyle\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta)

\displaystyle(s,y,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto(s,([\underbrace{\underline{y}],\dots,[\underline{y}]}_{(k+1)\textup{ times}}))

where
[\underline{y}]\in\Pi_{1}L
denotes the constant path at
y\in L
(see Definition
3.8
).

Proof.

We give the proof for
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
only since the others are analogous.

We need the explicit description of the Kuranishi charts
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
from Lemma 7.22 of
[
Iri20
]
.
We will borrow terminologies from
[
Iri20
]
, section 7.2.2, on e.g. decorated rooted ribbon trees.
Let
\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta)
be the set of tuples
(u,z_{0},z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
where
u\colon(\mathbb{D},\partial\mathbb{D})\to(\mathbb{C}^{n},L)
is a
C^{\infty}
-map such that
\bar{\partial}u=0
on a neighborhood of
\partial\mathbb{D}
and
[u]=\beta
, and
z_{0},\dots,z_{k}\in\partial\mathbb{D}
are distinct points aligned in anti-clockwise order. Let
p\in\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
. Then there is a Kuranishi chart
\mathscr{U}_{p}=(U_{p},\mathscr{E}_{p},s_{p},\psi_{p})
at
p
under the Kuranishi structure in Theorem
B.4
, where for a decorated rooted ribbon tree
(T,B)\in\mathcal{G}(k+1:\beta)
such that

\displaystyle p\in P\times\left(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T)}L\right)\,_{\Delta}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{\textup{int}}}\left(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T)}\mathring{\mathscr{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\right)

then
U_{p}
can be embedded into

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})}P\times\left(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T^{\prime})}L\right)\,_{\Delta}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{\textup{int}}}\left(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T^{\prime})}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v))\right)

where
(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})
runs over all reductions of
(T,B)
.

Then we define a map

\textup{ev}_{p}^{\mathscr{M}}\colon U_{p}\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta)

as follows. Each
x\in U_{p}
is identified with an element

x=\left(\pi,(u^{v},z_{0}^{v},\dots,z_{k_{v}}^{v})_{v}\right)\in P\times\left(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T^{\prime})}L\right)\,_{\Delta}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{\textup{int}}}\left(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T^{\prime})}\mathscr{M}\mathscr{M}_{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v))\right),

where
(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is a reduction of
(T,B)
.
For each
v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T^{\prime})
, we define
\textup{ev}(u^{v},z_{0}^{v},\dots,z_{k_{v}}^{v})\in\mathscr{L}^{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v))
as follows. For each
j=0,\dots,k_{v}
, let

\gamma_{j}^{u^{v}}=\left(\textup{ev}_{j}(u),\textup{ev}_{j+1}(u),\left[u|_{[z_{j}^{v},z_{j+1}^{v}]}\right]\right)\in\Pi_{1}L

in the fundamental groupoid, where
j+1
is taken modulo
k_{v}+1
,
u|_{[z_{j}^{v},z_{j+1}^{v}]}\in\mathcal{P}_{\textup{ev}_{j}(u),\textup{ev}_{j+1}(u)}
is the path in
L
given by restricting
u
to the arc from
z_{j}^{v}
to
z_{j+1}^{v}
in
\partial\mathbb{D}
and
\left[u|_{[z_{j}^{v},z_{j+1}^{v}]}\right]
is its homotopy class.
Then define

\textup{ev}(u^{v},z_{0}^{v},\dots,z^{v}_{k_{v}}):=\left(\gamma_{0}^{u^{v}},\dots\gamma_{k_{v}}^{u^{v}}\right)\in\mathscr{L}^{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v)).

Finally, define

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T)}P\times L\right)\,_{\Delta}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{\textup{int}}}\left(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T)}P\times\mathscr{L}^{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\right)\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta),

where the fibre product is taken over
\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T)}(P\times L)^{\times 2}
,
by concatenating paths using (
3.3
).
We then compose it with

\displaystyle U_{p}

\displaystyle\to\left(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\textup{int}}(T)}P\times L\right)\,_{\Delta}\!\!\times_{\textup{ev}_{\textup{int}}}\left(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\textup{int}}(T)}P\times\mathscr{L}^{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\right);

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\left(\pi,(u^{v},z_{0}^{v},\dots,z_{k_{v}}^{v})_{v}\right)\mapsto\left(\pi,\gamma_{j}^{u^{v}}\right)_{v}

to get the desired map
\textup{ev}_{p}^{\mathscr{M}}\colon U_{p}\to P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta)
.
The family of maps
\left(\textup{ev}_{p}^{\mathscr{M}}\right)_{p\in\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(\beta:P)}
is compatible with coordinate changes by the construction of the Kuranishi structure (see e.g. Lemma 7.28 of
[
Iri20
]
, although in our case this is a lot easier since we don’t have to deal with reparametrizations of loops and paths).
Moreover, the smoothness of
\textup{ev}_{p}^{\mathscr{M}}
follows from the fact that the smooth structure on
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
is given so that the evaluation map

\textup{ev}_{0}\times\dots\times\textup{ev}_{k}\colon\mathscr{L}^{k+1}\to L^{\times(k+1)}

is a smooth covering map, and that the composition

U_{p}\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}}P\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}\xrightarrow{\textup{id}_{P}\times(\textup{ev}_{0}\times\dots\times\textup{ev}_{k})}P\times L^{\times(k+1)}

is equal to
\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M},P}
in part (2) of Theorem
B.4
, which is smooth.
The commutativity of the diagrams are analogous to that in
[
Iri20
]
.
The statement about energy-zero moduli spaces also follow directly from the description.
∎

B.2.
CF perturbations

Again we explain the notion of CF perturbation in one chart.
Consider a Kuranishi chart
\mathscr{U}=(U,\mathscr{E},s,\psi)
on
X
as in Definition
B.2
.

Definition B.6

(
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 7.16; also see section 8.1.1 of
[
Iri20
]
)
.

A (representative of) a
CF-perturbation

\mathfrak{S}=(\mathfrak{S}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
of
\mathscr{U}
is the data of
(\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}})_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
where

•

\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}=(V_{\mathfrak{r}},E_{\mathfrak{r}},\phi_{\mathfrak{r}},\widehat{\phi}_{\mathfrak{r}})
is a manifold chart of
(U,\mathscr{E})
such that
(\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}(V_{\mathfrak{r}}))_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
covers
U
. Let
s_{\mathfrak{r}}\colon V_{\mathfrak{r}}\to E_{\mathfrak{r}}
be the pullback of
s
by
\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}
;

•

\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}}=(W_{\mathfrak{r}},\eta_{\mathfrak{r}},\{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon})
is a CF-perturbation of
\mathscr{U}
on
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}
:

(1)

W_{\mathfrak{r}}
is an open neighborhood of 0 in a finite-dimensional oriented real vector space;

(2)

\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}\colon V_{\mathfrak{r}}\times W_{\mathfrak{r}}\to E_{\mathfrak{r}}
is a family of maps, depending smoothly on
\varepsilon
, such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{r}}
is transversal to
0
for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, and

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{s}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{r}}(y,\xi)=s_{\mathfrak{r}}(y)

in compact
C^{1}
-topology on
V_{\mathfrak{r}}\times W_{\mathfrak{r}}
;

(3)

\eta_{\mathfrak{r}}\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{\dim W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(W_{\mathfrak{r}})
is a differential form such that
\int_{W_{\mathfrak{r}}}\eta_{\mathfrak{r}}=1
.

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we write

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}=(W_{\mathfrak{r}},\eta_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}),\quad\mathfrak{S}^{\varepsilon}=\{\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathscr{S}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{r}}\}_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}.

Definition B.7

(
[
Fuk+20
]
, Definition 7.9, Definition-Lemma 7.26)
.

Let
f\colon U\to M
be a smooth submersion to a smooth manifold
M
.
We say
f
is
strongly submersive
with respect to
\mathfrak{S}
if for each
\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}
, the map

\displaystyle f\circ\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}\circ\textup{pr}_{V_{\mathfrak{r}}}\colon(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0)\to L

is a submersion for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

There are definitions for an admissible CF-perturbation of an admissible Kuranishi chart and for a map
f\colon U\to M
to be a stratified strong submersion with respect to
\mathfrak{S}
.

Let
X
be one of the moduli spaces in (1) of Theorem
B.4
. Assign
\tau(X)\in(1/2,1)
as in Remark 7.23 of
[
Iri20
]
(as well as section 3 in
[
Iri
]
).
The following plays the role of Theorem 7.33 (and Remark 7.34) of
[
Iri20
]
.

Theorem B.8
.

Let
X
be one of the moduli spaces in (1) of Theorem
B.4
where
P=\{m\}
, with Kuranishi structure
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
, together with the corner-stratified admissible strongly smooth maps
\widehat{\textup{ev}}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})\to L^{k+1}
, such that
\textup{pr}_{0}\circ\widehat{\textup{ev}}\colon(X,\mathscr{U})\to L
is a stratified weak submersion. Then, upon shrinking
\tau(X)
to
\tau^{\prime}:=\tau^{\prime}(X)\in(0,\tau(X))
, there exist the following data:

•

A
\tau^{\prime}
-collared Kuranishi structure
\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}}
on
X
, which is a thickening of
\widehat{\mathscr{U}}
(see section 5.2 of
[
Fuk+20
]
);

•

An isomorphism of
\tau^{\prime}
-collared Kuranishi structures
\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{\ell}(X)}\cong\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{\ell}^{+}}
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
;

•

A
\tau^{\prime}
-collared CF-perturbation
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}^{+}}
of
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}})
such that
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{\ell}(X)}
coincides with
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}^{+}_{\ell}}
via the isomorphism of Kuranishi spaces
\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{\ell}(X)}\cong\widehat{\mathscr{U}_{\ell}^{+}}
;

•

A
\tau^{\prime}
-collared admissible map
\widehat{\textup{ev}^{+}}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}})\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
such that:

–

\widehat{\textup{ev}^{+}}
coincides with
\widehat{\textup{ev}}
under the KK-embedding
\widehat{\mathscr{U}}\to\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}}
;

–

\textup{pr}_{0}\circ\widehat{\textup{ev}^{+}}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}})\to L
is a stratified strong submersion with respect to
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}^{+}}
;

•

For
\beta=0
and
k>2
, the structures associated to
X=\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:P)
are isomorphic to
P\times L\times(D^{k-2})^{\boxplus\tau^{\prime}}
where
D^{k-2}
is the Stasheff cell, and the CF perturbation is trivial: that is, in the notations of Definition
B.6
, the Kuranishi structure on
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:P)
is given by a single Kuranishi chart, covered by a set of manifold charts
\{\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}=(V_{\mathfrak{r}},E_{\mathfrak{r}},\phi_{\mathfrak{r}},\widehat{\phi}_{\mathfrak{r}})\}_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
with
E_{\mathfrak{r}}=\{0\}
, and the CF-perturbation
\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}}=(W_{\mathfrak{r}},\eta_{\mathfrak{r}},\{\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon})
is given by
W_{\mathfrak{r}}=\{0\}
,
\eta=1\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{0}(W)
, and
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}=0
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

As in the Remark 7.34 of
[
Iri20
]
, there is an analogous statement for the cases where
P=[m,m+1]
and

\widehat{\textup{ev}}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})\to[a,b]^{\boxplus\tau}\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}

which we do not spell out.

Proof.

By Proposition
B.5
, we have a strongly smooth map
X\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta)
.
By Lemma-Definition 17.38 and Lemma 17.40 (3) of
[
Fuk+20
]
, this map extends to a 1-collared strongly smooth map
X^{\boxplus 1}\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(\beta)
.
Then successively apply Proposition 17.78 and Proposition 17.81 of
[
Fuk+20
]
to obtain Kuranishi structures
\widehat{\mathscr{U}^{+}}
and CF perturbations
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}^{+}}
.
In the case
\beta=0
, the moduli spaces
\mathscr{M}_{k+1}(0:\{m\})
are not the boundaries of other moduli spaces. We do not need to change the Kuranishi structure for the thickening because the obstruction bundle is trivial, and we can choose the CF perturbations to be trivial because the identity map
L\to L
is already a submersion.
∎

B.3.
Pushforward of differential forms

B.3.1.
Chain model of
[-1,1]\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, define a chain complex
\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
in exactly the same way as section 4.4 of
[
Iri20
]
.
Roughly speaking, an element in
\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
is represented by a quintuple
(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)
where

•

U\in\mathscr{U}
is an oriented submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
for some
N
.

•

\varphi:=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}},\varphi_{\mathscr{L}})\colon U\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)
, such that
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi_{\mathscr{L}}
are
C^{\infty}
-smooth (notice that unlike in
[
Iri20
]
, here
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)
is an ordinary finite-dimensional
C^{\infty}
-manifold and the notion of
C^{\infty}
-smooth is just the usual notion), and

U\to\mathbb{R}\times L;\quad u\mapsto(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u),\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\varphi_{\mathscr{L}}(u))

is a submersion. For each interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote
U_{I}:=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}})^{-1}(I)
.

•

\tau_{+}\colon U_{\geq 1}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\times U_{1}
is a diffeomorphism such that

\varphi|_{U_{\geq 1}}=(\iota_{\geq 1}\times\varphi_{\mathscr{L}}|_{U_{1}})\circ\tau_{+}

where
\iota_{\geq 1}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the inclusion map.

•

\tau_{-}\colon U_{\leq-1}\to\mathbb{R}_{\leq-1}\times U_{-1}
is a diffeomorphism such that

\varphi|_{U_{\leq-1}}=(\iota_{\leq-1}\times\varphi_{\mathscr{L}}|_{U_{-1}})\circ\tau_{-}

where
\iota_{\leq-1}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\leq-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the inclusion map.

•

\omega\in\mathscr{A}^{\dim U-*+1}(U)
such that
\omega|_{U_{[-1,1]}}
is compactly supported, and

\omega|_{U_{\geq 1}}=(\tau_{+})^{*}(1\times\omega|_{U_{1}}),\quad\omega|_{U_{\leq-1}}=(\tau_{-})^{*}(1\times\omega|_{U_{-1}}).

Denote the vector space of all such differential forms on
U
as
\mathscr{A}^{*}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-}).

Two such elements are identified if there is a submersion, respecting the ends of
\mathbb{R}
, between the domains of the de Rham chains, such that the differential forms pushforward from one to the other. See Section 4.4 of
[
Iri20
]
.

There are naturally defined chain maps

\displaystyle i\colon C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\to\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))

\displaystyle e_{+}\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\to C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a)),\quad e_{-}\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\to C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))

satisfying that

•

e_{+}\circ i=e_{-}\circ i=\textup{id}
;

•

(e_{+},e_{-})\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\to C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\oplus C_{*}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))
is surjective;

•

i\circ e_{+}
and
i\circ e_{-}
are chain homotopic to
id
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
j=1,\dots,k
and
a,a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
, the following fibre product operation is also defined in Section 4 of
[
Iri20
]
:

\displaystyle\circ_{j}\colon\overline{C}^{\textup{dR}}_{n+d}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))\otimes\overline{C}_{n+d^{\prime}}^{\textup{dR}}(\mathscr{L}^{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))\to\overline{C}^{\textup{dR}}_{n+d^{\prime}+d^{\prime}}(\mathscr{L}^{k+k^{\prime}}(a+a^{\prime}));\quad x\otimes y\mapsto x\circ_{j}y.

We then consider

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}:=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})}\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\overline{C}^{\textup{dR}}_{*+n+\mu(a)+k-1}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(a))

and its completion
\widehat{\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}
, and define a dg Lie algebra structure on it in the same way as
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
.
Then there are naturally defined morphisms of dg Lie algebras

\displaystyle i\colon C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\quad e_{+}\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}},\quad e_{-}\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}

satisfying that

•

e_{+}\circ i=e_{-}\circ i=\textup{id}_{C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}
;

•

(e_{+},e_{-})\colon\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\to C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}\oplus C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
is surjective;

•

i\circ e_{+}
and
i\circ e_{-}
are chain homotopic to
\textup{id}_{\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}}
. One can take chain homotopies to respect the decompositions over
(a,k)\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition B.9
.

Define

\overline{\textsf{L}}\in\overline{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}(0)=\prod_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\overline{C}^{\textup{dR}}_{n+k-2}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0))

as follows:

•

For
k=2
, consider the map
\mathbb{R}\times L\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{L}^{3}(0)
defined by
(r,y)\mapsto(r,(\underline{y},\underline{y},\underline{y}))
; then for any interval
i\subset\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}^{-1}(I)\cong I\times L
, and define
\tau_{+},\tau_{-}
to be the obvious diffeomorphisms. Set
\overline{\textsf{L}}(0,2):=(-1)^{n+1}[(\mathbb{R}\times L\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathscr{L}^{3}(0);1\in\mathscr{A}^{0}(\mathbb{R}\times L,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-}))]\in\overline{C}^{\textup{dR}}_{n}(\mathscr{L}^{3}(0))
;

•

For all
k\neq 2
, set
\overline{\textsf{L}}(0,k)=0
.

Then it follows that

(B.3)

\displaystyle e_{+}(\overline{\textsf{L}})=\textsf{L}.

B.3.2.
Strongly smooth maps from a K-space with a CF-perturbation gives a de Rham chain

The following is an analogue of Theorem 7.9 in
[
Iri20
]
(where the meaning of
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
differs: in our case,
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
is a finite-dimensional smooth manifold whereas in
[
Iri20
]
it is an infinite-dimensional differentiable space).
For the definition of a map
f\colon(X,\widehat{U})\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
to be admissible, see
[
Iri20
]
Definition 7.7 with the modifications of the meaning of
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
taken into account.

Theorem B.10

(
[
Iri20
]
Theorem 7.9)
.

Let

(1)

(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
be a compact, oriented, admissible Kuranishi space of dimension
d
;

(2)

\widehat{f}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
be an admissible map;

(3)

\widehat{\omega}
be an admissible differential form on
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
;

(4)

\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}
be an admissible CF-perturbation of
(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})
such that
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}
is transversal to 0, and such that
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ\widehat{f}\colon(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}})\to L
is a stratified strong submersion with respect to
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Then one can define

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{\mathscr{U}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\textup{dR}}_{d-|\widehat{\omega}|}(\mathscr{L}^{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, so that Stoke’s formula (analogous to Theorem 7.11 of
[
Iri20
]
) and the fibre product formula (analogous to Theorem 7.12 of
[
Iri20
]
) hold.

We also need a version of this for admissible K-spaces over an interval. For the statement see Theorem 7.14 of
[
Iri20
]
.
For the proofs see section 8 of
[
Iri20
]
.

For later purposes, we recall part of the explicit construction from section 8.1.2 of
[
Iri20
]
.
Suppose
X
can be covered by a single Kuranishi chart with boundary. Given the data of

(1)

\mathscr{U}=(U,\mathscr{E},s,\psi)
an admissible Kuranishi chart on
X
;

(2)

f\colon U\to\mathscr{L}^{k+1}
an admissible map;

(3)

\omega\in\mathscr{A}_{c}^{*}(U)
a admissible differential form;

(4)

\mathfrak{S}=(\mathfrak{S}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
an admissible CF-perturbation of
\textup{supp }\omega
such that
\textup{ev}_{0}\circ f\colon U\to L
is a stratified strong submersion with respect to
\mathfrak{S}
,

and for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
the de Rham chain
f_{*}(\mathscr{U},\omega,\mathfrak{S}^{\varepsilon})
is defined as follows.
Let
(\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}})_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
be a representative of the CF-perturbation
\mathfrak{S}
, and
(\chi_{\mathfrak{r}})_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
(\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}(V_{\mathfrak{r}}))_{\mathfrak{r}\in\mathfrak{R}}
.
We first define
f_{*}(\mathscr{U},\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\omega,\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon})
as follows. Let
D:=\dim U
. For a manifold chart
V_{\mathfrak{r}}
in
\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}
, given as an open neighborhood of
(t_{1},\dots,t_{D})\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{D}
, define the
retraction map
(terminology from Definition 17.7 of
[
Fuk+20
]
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{D};\quad(t_{1},\dots,t_{D})\mapsto(t_{1}^{\prime},\dots,t_{D}^{\prime}),\quad\textup{where }t_{i}^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}t_{i}&t_{i}\geq 0\\
0,&t_{i}<0\end{cases}.

Take a cutoff function
\kappa\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},[0,1])
such that
\kappa\equiv 1
on a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\kappa\equiv 0
on a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}_{\leq-1}
. Define

(1)

\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}:=\mathscr{R}^{-1}(V_{\mathfrak{r}})
,
\overline{E}_{\mathfrak{r}}:=\mathscr{R}^{*}E_{\mathfrak{r}}
;

(2)

\overline{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}:=(\mathscr{R}|_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\times\textup{id}_{W_{\mathfrak{r}}})^{*}(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon})
;

(3)

\overline{f}_{\mathfrak{r}}:=f\circ\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}\circ\mathscr{R}|_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}}
;

(4)

\overline{\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\omega}(t_{1},\dots,t_{D}):=\kappa(t_{1})\cdots\kappa(t_{D})\cdot(\phi_{\mathfrak{r}}\circ\mathscr{R}|_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}})^{*}(\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\omega)
.

Then define

(B.4)

\displaystyle f_{*}(\mathscr{U},\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\omega,\mathfrak{V}_{\mathfrak{r}},\mathscr{S}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon}):=(-1)^{\dagger}\big((\overline{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0),\overline{f}_{\mathfrak{r}}\circ\textup{pr}_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}},\textup{pr}_{\overline{V}_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{*}(\overline{\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\omega})\wedge\textup{pr}^{*}_{W_{\mathfrak{r}}}(\eta_{\mathfrak{r}})\big),

where

\displaystyle\dagger:=\dim W_{\mathfrak{r}}\cdot(\textup{rank}\,\mathscr{E}+|\omega|).

B.4.
Construction of low-energy approximate solutions

The following is analogous to Theorem 6.1 of
[
Iri20
]
(and Theorem
6.1^{+}
in
[
Iri
]
).
Recall that
\varepsilon>0
is chosen such that
2\varepsilon
is less than the minimal energy of non-constant
J
-holomorphic discs with boundaries on
L
.
For each
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the filtration

\displaystyle F^{m}C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\\
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
E(a)\geq\varepsilon(m+1-k)\end{subarray}}C^{\mathscr{L}}(a,k),

and similarly for
\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
. We abbreviate
C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
and
\overline{C}_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}
by
C
and
\overline{C}
.
Also, for each of the moduli spaces
X
in Theorem
B.4
, denote by
\bar{X}:=X^{\boxplus 1/2}
; Theorem
B.8
provides
\bar{X}
with an admissible CF-perturbation and an admissible strongly smooth map to
\mathscr{L}^{k+1}L
.

Theorem B.11
.

There exists integers
I,U\geq 2
and a sequence
(\textup{{M}}_{i},\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i},\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i},\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}},\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}},\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}})_{i\geq I}
for every
i\geq I
, where

\displaystyle\textup{{M}}_{i}\in F^{1}C_{-1},\,\,\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}\in F^{1}\overline{C}_{-1},\,\,\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}\in F^{-U}C_{2},\,\,\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}}\in F^{-U}\overline{C}_{2},\,\,\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}\in F^{-1}C_{1},\,\,\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}\in F^{-1}\overline{C}_{1},

such that

\displaystyle\textup{{M}}_{i}=e_{-}(\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}),\quad\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}=e_{-}(\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}}),\quad\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}=e_{-}(\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}),

\displaystyle\partial\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}[\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}},\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}]\in F^{i}\overline{C}_{-2},\quad\partial\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}}-[\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}},\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}}]-\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}\in F^{i-U-1}\overline{C}_{1}\quad\partial\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}-[\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}},\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}]\in F^{i-2}\overline{C}_{0},

\displaystyle\textup{{M}}_{i+1}-e_{+}(\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}})\in F^{i}C_{-1},\quad\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i+1}-e_{+}(\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}})\in F^{i-U-1}C_{2},\quad\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i+1}-e_{+}(\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}})\in F^{i-2}C_{1}

and such that

(1)

\textup{{M}}_{i}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
(i)

E(a)\geq 2\varepsilon
, or
(ii)

a=0
,
k\geq 2
. Moreover, in case
a=0
,
\{\textup{{M}}_{i}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(0)
(see the definition of
L
in Definition
A.10
)
and,
\{\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\overline{\textsf{L}}\in\overline{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}(0)
(see Definition
B.9
);

(2)

\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
(i)

E(a)\geq 2\varepsilon
, or
(ii)

a=0
. Moreover,
\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}(0)
is a cycle which is homologous to
\textsf{L}^{0}
(see Definition
4.10
);

(3)

Define the following subsets of
H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
:

\displaystyle A_{\textup{{M}}}

\displaystyle:=\{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}(a,k)\neq 0\textup{ for some }(i,k)\};

\displaystyle A_{\textup{{M}}}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\{a_{1}+\dots+a_{m}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid m\geq 1,a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in A_{\textup{{M}}}\};

\displaystyle A_{\textup{{N}}}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\{a\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid(\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{i}}(a,k),\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{i}}(a,k))\neq(0,0)\textup{ for some }(i,k)\};

\displaystyle A_{\textup{{N}}}^{+}

\displaystyle:=\{a_{1}+\dots+a_{m}\in H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})\mid m\geq 1,a_{1}\in A_{\textup{{N}}},a_{2},\dots,a_{m}\in A_{\textup{{M}}}\}.

Then for any
\lambda>0
,

A_{\textup{{M}}}^{+}(\lambda):=\{a\in A_{\textup{{M}}}^{+}\mid E(a)<\lambda\}\textup{ and }A^{+}_{\textup{{N}}}(E):=\{a\in A^{+}_{\textup{{N}}}\mid E(a)<\lambda\}

are finite sets.

Remark B.12
.

One difference between our version of Theorem
B.11
with Theorem 6.1
+
of
[
Iri
]
is that we require the zero-energy elements
\textup{{M}}_{i}(a,k)
and
\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}(a,k)
to match up with
\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
and
\overline{\textsf{L}}
on the chain-level exactly. The proof will be exactly the same as that for Theorem 6.1 in
[
Iri20
]
and Theorem
6.1^{+}
of
[
Iri
]
other than this point, so in the proof sketch below we will only be focused on verifying this.

Proof sketch.

Using Theorem
B.10
, we get

\displaystyle\textup{{M}}_{m}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k)}(-1)^{n+1}\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(\beta,\{m\})),

\displaystyle\overline{\textup{{M}}_{m}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k)}(-1)^{k+1}\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(\beta,[m,m+1])),

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{m}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-U-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{N}}_{k+1}^{\geq 0}(\beta,\{m\})),

\displaystyle\overline{\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}_{m}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-U-k)}\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{N}}_{k+1}^{\geq 0}(\beta,[m,m+1])),

\displaystyle\textup{{N}}^{0}_{m}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{N}}_{k+1}^{0}(\beta,\{m\})),

\displaystyle\overline{\textup{{N}}^{0}_{m}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{E(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k)}-\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{N}}_{k+1}^{0}(\beta,[m,m+1])).

See Remarks 7.35 and 7.36 of
[
Iri20
]
for notations (also see 5(v) in
[
Iri
]
for signs). In particular, the differential forms taken to be
1
.
The properties in the theorem (other than the energy-zero chains) are proved using the same argument as in section 7.6 in
[
Iri20
]
. In case
\beta=0
, by Theorem
B.8
we we have
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:P)\cong P\times L\times(D^{k-2})^{\boxplus\tau^{\prime}}
with trivial obstruction bundles and trivial CF-perturbations.
By the explicit description of the de Rham chain
\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:P))
discussed after Theorem
B.10
, we have the following cases:

•

In case
k<2
,
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:P)=\emptyset
, so
\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:P))=0
;

•

In case
k=2
,
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0:P)\cong P\times L
. In the case
P=\{m\}
, the evaluation map
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0:\{m\})\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0)
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0:\{m\})\cong L

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\iota_{3}}\mathscr{L}^{3}(0);

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto([\underline{y}],[\underline{y}],[\underline{y}])

by (
B.2
).
By the formula (
B.4
) and Definition
A.10
,
\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0,\{m\}))=\textsf{L}
;

•

In case
k>2
,
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:P)\cong P\times L\times(D^{k-2})^{\boxplus\tau^{\prime}}
and in the case
P=\{m\}
, the evaluation map
\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:\{m\})\xrightarrow{\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0)
is given by

\displaystyle\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:\{m\})\cong L\times(D^{k-2})^{\boxplus\tau^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\textup{pr}_{L}}L

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\iota_{k+1}}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0);

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto([\underline{y}],[\underline{y}],\dots,[\underline{y}])

by (
B.2
). The factorization of
\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}=\iota_{k+1}\circ\textup{pr}_{L}
shows that
\textup{ev}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0,\{m\}))
is a degenerate de Rham chain: more precisely, by (
3.1
) in Definition
3.5
,

\displaystyle\textup{ev}^{\mathscr{M}}_{*}(\bar{\mathscr{M}}_{k+1}(0:\{m\}))=[(L\xrightarrow{\iota_{k+1}}\mathscr{L}^{k+1}(0);(\textup{pr}_{L})_{!}1=0)]=0.

The discussion with the case
P=[m,m+1]
is similar.
∎

B.5.
Taking limits of approximate solutions

We now take limits of the low-energy approximations in Theorem
B.11
to prove Theorem
B.13
, which is analogous to Theorem 5.1 in
[
Iri20
]
.

Theorem B.13
.

Under the setup in Theorem
4.11
,
there exists the following data:

•

A constant
\hbar>0
,

•

A monoid of curve classes
\mathfrak{G}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
(Definition
4.2
) and a module
\mathfrak{N}\subset H_{1}(L;\mathbb{Z})
of
H
-perturbed curve classes over
\mathfrak{G}
(Definition
4.3
);

•

For each
\beta\in\mathfrak{G}
a chain
\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta)
, and for each
\eta\in\mathfrak{N}
a chain
\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
and a chain
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
;

such that

(1)

The element

\textup{{M}}:=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{G}}\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}},

where
\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta)
,
satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

\partial^{\textup{dR}}\textup{{M}}+\frac{1}{2}\big[\textup{{M}},\textup{{M}}\big]=0.

(2)

The elements

\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{N}},\quad\textup{{N}}^{0}:=\sum_{\eta\in\mathfrak{N}}\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in\widehat{C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{N}},

where
\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}(\eta)\in C_{2}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
,
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
,
satisfy

\partial^{\textup{dR}}\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}-\big[\textup{{M}},\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}\big]=\textup{{N}}^{0}.

(3)

Let
\textup{{M}}(\beta,k)
be the component of
\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta)
in
C^{\mathscr{L}}(\beta,k)_{-1}
. Then
\textup{{M}}(\beta,k)\neq 0
only if
(i)

E(\beta)\geq\hbar
, or
(ii)

\beta=0
,
k\geq 2
. Moreover, in case
\beta=0
,
\textup{{M}}(0)=\textsf{L}
(see the definition of
L
in Definition
A.10
).

(4)

Let
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta,k)
be the component of
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta)\in C_{1}^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta)
in
C^{\mathscr{L}}(\eta,k)_{1}
. Then
\textup{{N}}^{0}(\eta,k)\neq 0
only if
(i)

E(\eta)\geq\hbar
, or
(ii)

\eta=0
. Moreover, in case
\eta=0
,
\textup{{N}}^{0}(0)
is a cycle homologous to
\textsf{L}^{0}
(see the definition of
\textsf{L}^{0}
in Definition
4.10
).

Proof.

The proof is the same as section 6 of
[
Iri20
]
(also see
[
Iri
]
), but we need to keep track of the energy-zero parts. For simplicity we just focus on
\textup{{M}}(0,k)
, by following the construction in
[
Iri20
]
.
The construction in
[
Iri20
]
Lemma 6.4 has the following steps (with changes in notation):

(1)

Define
\textup{{M}}_{i,0}=\textup{{M}}_{i}
and
\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,0}}=\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i}}
, so that
\{\textup{{M}}_{i,0}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
,
\{\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,0}}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\overline{\textsf{L}}
;

(2)

Inductively define
\Delta^{i}_{\textup{{M}}}:=\textup{{M}}_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j}})
; by (
B.3
),
e_{+}\overline{\textsf{L}}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
, so that
\Delta^{i}_{\textup{{M}}}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
;

(3)

Pick
\overline{\Delta}_{\textup{{M}}}^{i}\in F^{i+j}\overline{C}_{-1}
such that

e_{+}(\overline{\Delta}_{\textup{{M}}}^{i})-\Delta^{i}_{\textup{{M}}}\in F^{i+j+1}C_{-1}\,\,\textup{ and }\,\,\partial\overline{\Delta}_{\textup{{M}}}^{i}+\left(\partial\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j}}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j}},\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j}}\right]\right)\in F^{i+j+1}\overline{C}_{-2}.

Since
\Delta^{i}_{\textup{{M}}}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we may take
\overline{\Delta}_{\textup{{M}}}^{i}(0,k)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
;

(4)

Set

\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j+1}}:=\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j}}+\overline{\Delta}_{\textup{{M}}}^{i},\quad\textup{{M}}_{i,j+1}:=e_{-}(\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j+1}}).

Thus
\{\overline{\textup{{M}}_{i,j+1}}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\overline{\textsf{L}}
and
\{\textup{{M}}_{i,j+1}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
;

(5)

Finally, define
\textup{{M}}:=\lim_{j\to\infty}\textup{{M}}_{i,j}
for a fixed
i\geq I
. It then follows that
\{\textup{{M}}(0,k)\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}.

∎

B.6.
Proof of Theorem
4.11

Lemma B.14
.

The element

\textup{{M}}^{0}:=\sum_{k\geq 2}\textup{{M}}(0,k)\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}}

satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

\partial^{\textup{dR}}\textup{{M}}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}[\textup{{M}}^{0},\textup{{M}}^{0}]=0,

and

-[\textup{{M}}^{0},x]=\partial^{1}x\quad\textup{ for any }x\in C_{*}^{\mathscr{L}}

where
\partial^{1}
is defined in (
3.7
).

Proof.

By Theorem
B.13
(3),
\textup{{M}}^{0}=\widetilde{\textsf{L}}
.
That
\textup{{M}}^{0}
satisfies Maurer-Cartan equation then follows from Lemma
A.13
(this also follows from conditions (1) and (3) of Theorem
B.13
and energy considerations, analogous to the paragraph after Theorem 5.1 of
[
Iri20
]
).
That
-[\textup{{M}}^{0},-]=\partial^{1}
follows from (
A.6
).
∎

Recall our notation
\mathfrak{G}^{+}:=\mathfrak{G}\setminus\{0\}
.

Corollary B.15
.

The element

\textup{{M}}^{+}:=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{G}^{+}}\textup{{M}}(\beta)\in\widehat{C_{-1}^{\mathscr{L}}}_{\mathfrak{G}^{+}}

satisfies the Maurer-Cartan equation

\partial\textup{{M}}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}[\textup{{M}}^{+},\textup{{M}}^{+}]=0,

where
\partial=\partial^{0}+\partial^{1}
is the differential in (
3.6
).
Also

\partial\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}-[\textup{{M}}^{+},\textup{{N}}^{\geq 0}]=\textup{{N}}^{0}.

Proof.

This is the same as
[
Iri20
]
, Lemma 5.3.
∎

Theorem
4.11
then follows by replacing
M
with
\textup{{M}}^{+}
.
